When it comes to snarking the president,

00:46:22 --> 00:46:24
I'm not gonna stop that.

00:46:23 --> 00:46:25
I think the president deserves to be snarked.

00:46:24 --> 00:46:27
- So you're not afraid of attacking Trump?

00:46:26 --> 00:46:29
- No, I mean, I've done it before.

00:46:30 --> 00:46:33
- Can you say what your favorite

00:46:32 --> 00:46:36
and least favorite things are about President Trump

00:46:35 --> 00:46:38
and President Biden, one at a time?

00:46:37 --> 00:46:42
So maybe one thing that you can say is super positive

00:46:41 --> 00:46:45
about Trump, and one thing super negative about Trump.

00:46:44 --> 00:46:47
- Okay, so the super positive thing about Trump

00:46:46 --> 00:46:49
is that because he has no preconceived views

00:46:48 --> 00:46:51
that are establishmentarian,

00:46:50 --> 00:46:53
he's sometimes willing to go outta the box and do things

00:46:52 --> 00:46:55
that haven't been tried before.

00:46:54 --> 00:46:57
And, sometimes that works.

00:46:56 --> 00:46:59
The best example being the entire foreign policy

00:46:58 --> 00:47:01
establishment telling him that he couldn't get

00:47:00 --> 00:47:02
a Middle Eastern deal done

00:47:01 --> 00:47:04
unless he centered the Palestinian Israeli conflict.

00:47:03 --> 00:47:06
And instead he just went right around that,

00:47:05 --> 00:47:07
and ended up cutting a bunch of peace deals

00:47:06 --> 00:47:08
in the Middle East

00:47:07 --> 00:47:10
or moving the embassy in Jerusalem.

00:47:09 --> 00:47:12
Sometimes he does stuff and it's really outta the box,

00:47:11 --> 00:47:13
and it actually works.

00:47:12 --> 00:47:17
And, that's kind of awesome in politics, and neat to see.

00:47:16 --> 00:47:20
The downside of Trump is that he has no capacity

00:47:19 --> 00:47:23
to use any sort of...

00:47:23 --> 00:47:26
There's no filter between brain and mouth.

00:47:25 --> 00:47:28
Well, whatever happens in his brain,

00:47:27 --> 00:47:29
is the thing that comes out of his mouth.

00:47:28 --> 00:47:31
I know a lot of people find that charming and wonderful,

00:47:30 --> 00:47:34
and from time, and it is very funny.

00:47:33 --> 00:47:36
But, I don't think that it is a particularly

00:47:35 --> 00:47:38
excellent personal quality in a person

00:47:37 --> 00:47:40
who has as much responsibility, as President Trump has.

00:47:39 --> 00:47:42
I think he says a lot of damaging,

00:47:41 --> 00:47:44
and bad things on Twitter.

00:47:43 --> 00:47:47
I think that he seems,

00:47:46 --> 00:47:50
consumed, in some ways, by his own grievances;

00:47:49 --> 00:47:51
which is why you've seen him focusing

00:47:50 --> 00:47:53
in on election 2020 so much.

00:47:52 --> 00:47:55
And, I think that that is very negative

00:47:54 --> 00:47:56
about President Trump.

00:47:55 --> 00:47:57
I'm very grateful to president Trump, as a conservative,

00:47:56 --> 00:47:59
for many of the things that he did.

00:47:58 --> 00:48:01
I think that a lot of his personality issues

00:48:00 --> 00:48:03
are pretty severe.

00:48:02 --> 00:48:06
- What about Joe Biden?

00:48:06 --> 00:48:09
- I think that the thing that I like most about Joe Biden.

00:48:08 --> 00:48:11
- Yes. - I will say,

00:48:10 --> 00:48:14
that Biden, two things.

00:48:13 --> 00:48:19
One, Biden seems to be a very good father

00:48:18 --> 00:48:21
by all available evidence, right.

00:48:20 --> 00:48:23
There are a lot of people who are put out,

00:48:22 --> 00:48:25
kind of tape of him talking to Hunter;

00:48:24 --> 00:48:27
and Hunter's having trouble with drugs or whatever.

00:48:26 --> 00:48:30
And, I keep listening to that tape and thinking,

00:48:29 --> 00:48:31
he seems like a really good dad.

00:48:30 --> 00:48:33
The stuff that he's saying to his son

00:48:32 --> 00:48:34
is stuff that God forbid,

00:48:33 --> 00:48:35
if that were happening with my kid,

00:48:34 --> 00:48:36
I'd be saying to my kid.

00:48:35 --> 00:48:38
And so, you can't help but feel for the guy.

00:48:37 --> 00:48:41
He's had an incredibly difficult go of it

00:48:40 --> 00:48:42
with his first wife

00:48:41 --> 00:48:45
and the death of members of his family,

00:48:44 --> 00:48:46
and then Beau dying.

00:48:45 --> 00:48:47
I mean, that kind of stuff,

00:48:46 --> 00:48:49
obviously is deeply sympathetic in his...

00:48:48 --> 00:48:52
He seems like a deeply sympathetic father.

00:48:51 --> 00:48:55
As far as his politics,

00:48:54 --> 00:48:57
he seems like a slap on the back, kind of guy.

00:48:56 --> 00:48:58
And I don't mind that.

00:48:57 --> 00:49:00
I think that's nice, so far as it goes.

00:48:59 --> 00:49:02
It's sort of an old school politics,

00:49:01 --> 00:49:03
where things are done

00:49:02 --> 00:49:06
with handshake and personal relationships.

00:49:05 --> 00:49:06
The thing I don't like about him

00:49:05 --> 00:49:08
is I think sometimes that's really not genuine.

00:49:07 --> 00:49:10
I think that that sometimes,

00:49:09 --> 00:49:12
I think that's his personal tendency,

00:49:11 --> 00:49:15
but I think sometimes he allows the prevailing wins

00:49:14 --> 00:49:18
of his party to carry him to incredibly radical places.

00:49:17 --> 00:49:22
And then, he just doubles down on the radicalism

00:49:21 --> 00:49:24
in some pretty disingenuous ways.

00:49:23 --> 00:49:25
And, there I would cite

00:49:24 --> 00:49:28
the Independence Hall speech;

00:49:27 --> 00:49:30
which I thought was truly one of the worst speeches

00:49:29 --> 00:49:31
I've seen a president give.

00:49:30 --> 00:49:32
- So, you don't think he's trying to be

00:49:31 --> 00:49:33
a unifier in general?

00:49:32 --> 00:49:34
- Not at all.

00:49:33 --> 00:49:36
I mean, that's what he was elected to do.

00:49:35 --> 00:49:38
He was elected to do two things,

00:49:37 --> 00:49:39
not be alive, and be a unifier.

00:49:38 --> 00:49:40
Those were the two things.

00:49:39 --> 00:49:42
And like, and when I say not be alive,

00:49:41 --> 00:49:44
I don't mean like physically dead,

00:49:43 --> 00:49:45
this is where the snark comes in.

00:49:44 --> 00:49:47
But, what I do mean is that

00:49:46 --> 00:49:51
he was elected to not be particularly activist.

00:49:50 --> 00:49:53
Basically, the mandate was don't be Trump.

00:49:52 --> 00:49:55
Be sane, don't be Trump, calm everything down.

00:49:54 --> 00:49:56
And instead he got in, he is like,

00:49:55 --> 00:49:58
"What if we spend 7 trillion dollars?

00:49:57 --> 00:50:00
"What if we pull out of Afghanistan

00:49:59 --> 00:50:01
"Without any sort of plan?

00:50:00 --> 00:50:04
"What if I start labeling all of my political enemies,

00:50:03 --> 00:50:05
"Enemies of the republic?

00:50:04 --> 00:50:09
"What if I start bringing Dylan Mulvaney to the White House

00:50:08 --> 00:50:11
"And talking about how it is a moral sin

00:50:10 --> 00:50:14
"To prevent the general mutilation of minors?"

00:50:13 --> 00:50:16
This kinda stuff is very radical stuff.

00:50:15 --> 00:50:18
And, this is not a president who has pursued

00:50:17 --> 00:50:18
a unifying agenda;

00:50:17 --> 00:50:21
which is why his approval rating sank from 60%

00:50:20 --> 00:50:24
when he entered office, to low 40s or high 30s today.

00:50:23 --> 00:50:25
Unlike President Trump,

00:50:24 --> 00:50:26
who never had a high approval rating.

00:50:25 --> 00:50:27
Trump came into office,

00:50:26 --> 00:50:29
and he had like a 45% approval rating,

00:50:28 --> 00:50:30
and when he left office,

00:50:29 --> 00:50:32
he had about a 43% approval rating.

00:50:31 --> 00:50:33
It bounced around between 45 and 37

00:50:32 --> 00:50:35
pretty much his entire presidency.

00:50:34 --> 00:50:37
Biden went from being a very popular guy coming in,

00:50:36 --> 00:50:39
to a very unpopular guy right now.

00:50:38 --> 00:50:40
And, if you're Joe Biden,

00:50:39 --> 00:50:40
you should be looking in the mirror

00:50:39 --> 00:50:42
and wondering exactly why.

00:50:41 --> 00:50:44
- Do you think that pulling out from Afghanistan

00:50:43 --> 00:50:46
could be flipped as a pro for Biden,

00:50:45 --> 00:50:48
in terms of he actually did it.

00:50:47 --> 00:50:50
- I think it's gonna be almost impossible.

00:50:49 --> 00:50:53
I think the American people are incredibly inconsistent

00:50:52 --> 00:50:55
about their own views on foreign policy.

00:50:54 --> 00:50:56
In other words,

00:50:55 --> 00:50:59
we like to be isolationist until it comes time for us

00:50:58 --> 00:51:00
to be defeated and humiliated.

00:50:59 --> 00:51:04
When that happens, we tend not to like it very much.

00:51:03 --> 00:51:07
- You mentioned Biden being a good father.

00:51:06 --> 00:51:09
Can you make the case for and against

00:51:08 --> 00:51:12
he Hunter Biden laptop story?

00:51:11 --> 00:51:14
For it being a big deal

00:51:13 --> 00:51:16
and against it being a big deal?

00:51:15 --> 00:51:19
- Sure, the case for being a big deal is basically twofold.

00:51:18 --> 00:51:21
One, is that it is clearly relevant

00:51:20 --> 00:51:24
if the president's son is running around

00:51:23 --> 00:51:26
to foreign countries picking up bags of cash

00:51:25 --> 00:51:28
because his last name is Biden,

00:51:27 --> 00:51:32
while his father is vice president of the United States.

00:51:31 --> 00:51:34
And, it raises questions as to influence peddling

00:51:33 --> 00:51:36
for either the vice president

00:51:35 --> 00:51:38
or the former vice president using political connections.

00:51:37 --> 00:51:39
Did he make any money?

00:51:38 --> 00:51:40
Who was the big guy?

00:51:39 --> 00:51:41
All these open questions,

00:51:40 --> 00:51:45
that obviously implicates the questions to be asked.

00:51:44 --> 00:51:47
And then, the secondary reason that the story is big

00:51:46 --> 00:51:48
is actually 'cause the reaction of the story,

00:51:47 --> 00:51:52
the banning of the story is in and of itself a major story.

00:51:51 --> 00:51:53
If there's any story

00:51:52 --> 00:51:55
that implicates a presidential candidate

00:51:54 --> 00:51:56
in the last month of an election,

00:51:55 --> 00:51:58
and there is a media blackout,

00:51:57 --> 00:52:00
including a social media blackout,

00:51:59 --> 00:52:02
that obviously raises some very serious questions

00:52:01 --> 00:52:03
about informational flow and dissemination

00:52:02 --> 00:52:04
in the United States.

00:52:03 --> 00:52:07
- No matter how big of a deal the story is,

00:52:06 --> 00:52:09
it is a big deal that there's a censorship

00:52:08 --> 00:52:11
of any relevant story.

00:52:10 --> 00:52:13
- When there's a coordinated collusive blackout, yeah.

00:52:12 --> 00:52:15
That's a serious and major problem.

00:52:14 --> 00:52:17
So those are the two reasons why it would be a big story.

00:52:16 --> 00:52:19
The two reasons,

00:52:18 --> 00:52:21
a reason why it would not be a big story perhaps,

00:52:20 --> 00:52:25
is if it turns out, and we don't really know this yet.

00:52:24 --> 00:52:29
But let's say that Hunter Biden was basically off on his own

00:52:28 --> 00:52:30
doing what he was doing,

00:52:29 --> 00:52:34
being a derelict or drug addict or acting badly,

00:52:33 --> 00:52:35
and his dad had nothing to do with it,

00:52:34 --> 00:52:37
and Joe was telling the truth and he really knew.

00:52:36 --> 00:52:38
But, the problem is

00:52:37 --> 00:52:39
we never actually got those questions answered.

00:52:38 --> 00:52:41
If it had turned out to be nothing of a story,

00:52:40 --> 00:52:43
the nice thing about stories that turn out to be nothing

00:52:42 --> 00:52:44
is that after they turned out

00:52:43 --> 00:52:45
o be nothing, they're nothing.

00:52:44 --> 00:52:47
The biggest problem with this story

00:52:46 --> 00:52:48
is that it wasn't allowed

00:52:47 --> 00:52:51
to take the normal life cycle of a story;

00:52:50 --> 00:52:52
which is original story breaks,

00:52:51 --> 00:52:54
follow on questions are asked,

00:52:53 --> 00:52:55
follow on questions are answered.

00:52:54 --> 00:52:58
Story is either now a big story or it's nothing.

00:52:57 --> 00:53:01
One, when the life cycle of a story is cut off

00:53:00 --> 00:53:03
right at the very beginning, right when it's born,

00:53:02 --> 00:53:05
then that allows you to speculate in any direction you want.

00:53:04 --> 00:53:06
You can speculate, "It means nothing.

00:53:05 --> 00:53:07
"It's it's nonsense.

00:53:06 --> 00:53:10
"It's Russian, it's a Russian laptop,

00:53:09 --> 00:53:11
"It's disinformation."

00:53:10 --> 00:53:12
Or on the other hand,

00:53:11 --> 00:53:14
this means that Joe Biden was personally calling Hunter

00:53:13 --> 00:53:16
and telling him to pick up a sack of cash

00:53:15 --> 00:53:18
over in Beijing and then he became president

00:53:17 --> 00:53:19
and he's influenced pedaling.

00:53:18 --> 00:53:21
This is why it's important to allow these stories

00:53:20 --> 00:53:22
to go forward.

00:53:21 --> 00:53:23
So this is why actually the bigger story

00:53:22 --> 00:53:25
for the moment is not the laptop,

00:53:24 --> 00:53:27
it's the reaction to the laptop

00:53:26 --> 00:53:29
'cause it cut off that life cycle of the story.

00:53:28 --> 00:53:31
And then, at some point

00:53:30 --> 00:53:33
I would assume that there will be some follow on questions

00:53:32 --> 00:53:34
that are actually answered.

00:53:33 --> 00:53:36
The house is pledging, if it goes Republican,

00:53:35 --> 00:53:37
to investigate all of this.

00:53:36 --> 00:53:39
Again, I wouldn't be supremely surprised

00:53:38 --> 00:53:40
if it turns out

00:53:39 --> 00:53:44
hat there was no direct involvement of Joe

00:53:43 --> 00:53:44
in this sort of stuff.

00:53:43 --> 00:53:46
Because it turns out, as I said before,

00:53:45 --> 00:53:47
that all of politics is Veep.

00:53:46 --> 00:53:49
And, this is always the story with half the scandals

00:53:48 --> 00:53:51
that you see is that everybody assumes

00:53:50 --> 00:53:55
that there's some sort of deep and abiding clever plan

00:53:54 --> 00:53:56
that some politician is implementing.

00:53:55 --> 00:53:58
And then, you look at it and it turns out,

00:53:57 --> 00:54:00
now it's just something dumb, right.

00:53:59 --> 00:54:02
This is sort of, perfect example of this,

00:54:01 --> 00:54:04
President Trump with the classified documents in Mar Lago.

00:54:03 --> 00:54:07
So people on the left lane, it's probably nuclear codes,

00:54:06 --> 00:54:08
probably he's taking secret documents

00:54:07 --> 00:54:10
and selling them to the Russians or the Chinese.

00:54:09 --> 00:54:12
And, the real most obvious explanation

00:54:11 --> 00:54:14
is Trump looked at the papers and he said,

00:54:13 --> 00:54:15
"I like these papers."

00:54:14 --> 00:54:17
And then, he just decided to keep them.

00:54:16 --> 00:54:18
And then, people came to him and said,

00:54:17 --> 00:54:19
"Mr. President, you're not allowed to keep those papers."

00:54:18 --> 00:54:20
He said, "Who are those people?

00:54:19 --> 00:54:21
"I don't care about what they have to say,

00:54:20 --> 00:54:23
"I'm putting them in the other room in a box."

00:54:25 --> 00:54:28
It is highly likely that that is what happened.

00:54:27 --> 00:54:30
And it's very disappointing to people, I think,

00:54:29 --> 00:54:32
when they realize it.

00:54:31 --> 00:54:32
The human brain, I mean,

00:54:31 --> 00:54:33
you know this better than I do,

00:54:32 --> 00:54:35
but the human brain is built to find patterns.

00:54:34 --> 00:54:36
It's what we like to do.

00:54:35 --> 00:54:37
We like to find plans and patterns

00:54:36 --> 00:54:39
because this is how we survived in the wild,

00:54:38 --> 00:54:40
is you found a plan,

00:54:39 --> 00:54:42
you found a pattern, you crack the code of the universe.

00:54:41 --> 00:54:43
When it comes to politics,

00:54:42 --> 00:54:46
the conspiracy theories that we see so often,

00:54:45 --> 00:54:48
it's largely because we're seeing inexplicable events.

00:54:48 --> 00:54:50
Unless, you just assume everyone's a moron.

00:54:49 --> 00:54:52
If you assume that there's a lot of stupidity going on,

00:54:51 --> 00:54:54
everything becomes quickly explicable.

00:54:53 --> 00:54:57
If you assume that there must be some rationale behind it.

00:54:56 --> 00:55:00
You have to come up with increasingly convoluted conspiracy

00:54:59 --> 00:55:01
theories to explain just why people are acting the way

00:55:00 --> 00:55:03
that they're acting.

00:55:02 --> 00:55:04
And, I find that,

00:55:03 --> 00:55:06
I don't say a 100% of the time, but 90,

00:55:05 --> 00:55:08
94% of the time,

00:55:07 --> 00:55:13
the conspiracy theory turns out just to be people being dumb

00:55:12 --> 00:55:14
and then other people reacting in dumb ways

00:55:14 --> 00:55:16
to the original people being dumb.

00:55:15 --> 00:55:19
- But it's also, to me, in that same way, very possible,

00:55:19 --> 00:55:25
very likely that Hunter Biden getting money in Ukraine,

00:55:24 --> 00:55:27
I guess for consulting and all that kind of stuff

00:55:26 --> 00:55:29
is a nothing burger.

00:55:28 --> 00:55:31
He's qualified, he's getting money as he should.

00:55:30 --> 00:55:34
There's a lot of influence pedaling in general

00:55:33 --> 00:55:36
that's not corrupt.

00:55:35 --> 00:55:38
- The most obvious explanation there probably is

00:55:37 --> 00:55:40
that he was fake influence pedaling;

00:55:39 --> 00:55:41
meaning he went to Ukraine.

00:55:40 --> 00:55:42
And he is like, "Guess what?

00:55:41 --> 00:55:43
"My dad's Joe."

00:55:42 --> 00:55:44
And they're like,

00:55:43 --> 00:55:46
"Well, you don't have any qualifications in oil

00:55:45 --> 00:55:48
"And natural gas and you don't really have a great resume

00:55:47 --> 00:55:49
"But your dad is Joe.

00:55:48 --> 00:55:50
And then, that was kinda the end of it.

00:55:49 --> 00:55:52
They gave him a bag of cash, hoping you would do something.

00:55:51 --> 00:55:52
He never did anything.

00:55:51 --> 00:55:54
- I think you're making it sound worse than it is.

00:55:53 --> 00:55:55
I think this in general,

00:55:54 --> 00:55:56
consulting is done in that way.

00:55:55 --> 00:55:57
Your name, it's not like you're-

00:55:56 --> 00:55:58
I agree with you.

00:55:57 --> 00:55:59
- You're not,

00:55:58 --> 00:56:02
it's not like he is some rare case

00:56:01 --> 00:56:04
and this is an illustration of corruption.

00:56:03 --> 00:56:06
If you can criticize consulting,

00:56:05 --> 00:56:07
which I would.

00:56:06 --> 00:56:08
- [Ben] That's fair.

00:56:07 --> 00:56:10
- Which they're basically not providing...

00:56:09 --> 00:56:13
You look at a resume and who's who.

00:56:12 --> 00:56:14
If you went to Harvard,

00:56:13 --> 00:56:16
I can criticize the same thing.

00:56:15 --> 00:56:18
If you have Harvard on your resume,

00:56:17 --> 00:56:21
you're more likely to be hired as a consultant.

00:56:20 --> 00:56:24
Maybe there's a network there of people that you know

00:56:23 --> 00:56:25
and you hire them in that same way.

00:56:24 --> 00:56:27
If your last name is Biden,

00:56:26 --> 00:56:29
there's a lot of last names that sound pretty good at it.

00:56:28 --> 00:56:30
- That's for sure.

00:56:29 --> 00:56:31
For sure, for sure.

00:56:30 --> 00:56:32
- It's not like-

00:56:31 --> 00:56:33
And, Hunter Biden admitted that much, by the way.

00:56:32 --> 00:56:34
In an open interview.

00:56:33 --> 00:56:35
He was like,

00:56:34 --> 00:56:36
"If your last name weren't Biden,

00:56:35 --> 00:56:36
"Would you have got that job?"

00:56:35 --> 00:56:38
And he is like, "Probably not."

00:56:37 --> 00:56:39
And you're right.

00:56:38 --> 00:56:39
- That's an honest, that's-

00:56:38 --> 00:56:40
I agree with you.

00:56:39 --> 00:56:43
- It's not like he's getting a ridiculous amount of money.

00:56:42 --> 00:56:45
He was getting like a pretty standard consulting

00:56:44 --> 00:56:46
kind of money;

00:56:45 --> 00:56:48
which I also would criticize

00:56:47 --> 00:56:50
'cause they get a ridiculous amount of money.

00:56:49 --> 00:56:52
But, even to push back on the life cycle

00:56:51 --> 00:56:57
or to steel man the side that was concerned

00:56:56 --> 00:56:59
about the Hunter Biden laptop story.

00:56:58 --> 00:57:02
I don't know if there is a natural life cycle of a story

00:57:01 --> 00:57:05
because there's something about the virality of the internet

00:57:04 --> 00:57:09
hat we can't predict that a story can just take hold

00:57:08 --> 00:57:12
and the conspiracy around it builds.

00:57:11 --> 00:57:16
Especially around politics, where the interpretation,

00:57:15 --> 00:57:18
some popular sexy interpretation of a story

00:57:17 --> 00:57:21
that might not be connected to reality at all,

00:57:20 --> 00:57:22
will become viral.

00:57:21 --> 00:57:23
And that, from Facebook's perspective,

00:57:22 --> 00:57:25
is probably what they're worried about,

00:57:24 --> 00:57:30
is organized misinformation campaign

00:57:29 --> 00:57:34
that makes up a sexy story or sexy interpretation

00:57:33 --> 00:57:37
of the vague story that we have.

00:57:36 --> 00:57:39
And that, has an influence on the populace.

00:57:38 --> 00:57:41
- I mean, I think that's true,

00:57:40 --> 00:57:42
but I think the question becomes

00:57:41 --> 00:57:43
who's the great adjudicator there.

00:57:42 --> 00:57:46
Who adjudicates when the story ought to be allowed

00:57:45 --> 00:57:48
to go through even a bad life cycle

00:57:47 --> 00:57:50
or allowed to go viral as opposed to not?

00:57:49 --> 00:57:52
Now, that's one thing if you wanna say,

00:57:51 --> 00:57:53
"Okay, we can spot the Russian accounts

00:57:52 --> 00:57:54
"That are actually promoting this stuff,

00:57:53 --> 00:57:55
"They belong to the Russian government.

00:57:54 --> 00:57:56
"Gotta shut that down."

00:57:55 --> 00:57:57
I think everybody agrees.

00:57:56 --> 00:57:59
This is actually one of the slides

00:57:58 --> 00:58:00
that's happened linguistically

00:57:59 --> 00:58:01
that I really object to,

00:58:00 --> 00:58:04
is the slide between disinformation and misinformation.

00:58:03 --> 00:58:06
You notice there was this evolution.

00:58:05 --> 00:58:08
In 2017, there was a lot of talk about disinformation.

00:58:07 --> 00:58:09
It was Russian disinformation.

00:58:08 --> 00:58:11
The Russians were putting out deliberately false information

00:58:10 --> 00:58:13
in order to skew election results, was the accusation.

00:58:12 --> 00:58:14
And then, people started

00:58:13 --> 00:58:17
using disinformation or misinformation.

00:58:16 --> 00:58:19
And misinformation is either mistaken information

00:58:18 --> 00:58:21
or information that is quote unquote out of context.

00:58:20 --> 00:58:23
That becomes very subjective very quickly

00:58:22 --> 00:58:25
as to what out of context means.

00:58:24 --> 00:58:27
And it doesn't necessarily have to be from a foreign source,

00:58:26 --> 00:58:28
it can be from a domestic source, right.

00:58:27 --> 00:58:30
It could be somebody misinterpreting something here.

00:58:29 --> 00:58:32
It could be somebody interpreting something correctly,

00:58:31 --> 00:58:34
but PolitiFact thinks that it's out of context.

00:58:33 --> 00:58:37
That sort of stuff gets very murky very quickly.

00:58:36 --> 00:58:39
And so, I'm deeply uncomfortable with the idea

00:58:38 --> 00:58:40
that Facebook, I mean Zuckerberg

00:58:39 --> 00:58:43
was on with Rogan and talking about

00:58:42 --> 00:58:46
how the FBI had basically set lookout

00:58:45 --> 00:58:48
for Russian interference in the election,

00:58:47 --> 00:58:50
and then all of these people were out there saying

00:58:49 --> 00:58:52
that the laptop was Russian disinformation.

00:58:51 --> 00:58:53
So, he basically shut it down.

00:58:52 --> 00:58:54
That sort of stuff is frightening,

00:58:54 --> 00:58:56
especially 'cause it wasn't Russian disinformation.

00:58:55 --> 00:58:57
I mean the laptop was real.

00:58:57 --> 00:59:02
And so, the fact that you have people who seemed to,

00:59:02 --> 00:59:04
let's put it this way,

00:59:03 --> 00:59:05
it seems as though, maybe this is wrong.

00:59:04 --> 00:59:07
It seems as though when a story gets killed,

00:59:06 --> 00:59:08
preemptively like this,

00:59:07 --> 00:59:11
it is almost universally a story that negatively

00:59:10 --> 00:59:13
affects one side of the political aisle.

00:59:12 --> 00:59:16
I can't remember the last time there was a story

00:59:15 --> 00:59:19
on the right that was disinformation or misinformation,

00:59:18 --> 00:59:20
where social media stepped in and they went,

00:59:19 --> 00:59:21
"We cannot have this,

00:59:20 --> 00:59:22
"This cannot be be distributed.

00:59:21 --> 00:59:24
"We're going to all collude so that this,

00:59:23 --> 00:59:26
"Information is not distributed."

00:59:25 --> 00:59:28
Maybe in response to the story being proved false,

00:59:27 --> 00:59:29
it gets taken down.

00:59:28 --> 00:59:31
But, what made the Hunter Biden thing so amazing

00:59:30 --> 00:59:33
is that it wasn't even a response to anything.

00:59:32 --> 00:59:34
It was, the story got posted,

00:59:33 --> 00:59:38
there were no actual doubts expressed as to the verified

00:59:37 --> 00:59:39
falsity the story.

00:59:38 --> 00:59:41
It was just supposition that it had to be false

00:59:40 --> 00:59:42
and everybody jumped in.

00:59:41 --> 00:59:44
So I think that confirmed a lot of the conspiracy theories

00:59:43 --> 00:59:47
people had about social media and how it works.

00:59:46 --> 00:59:51
- Yeah, if the reason you want to slow down the viral spread

00:59:50 --> 00:59:56
of a thing is at all grounded in partisanship,

00:59:55 --> 00:59:57
that's a problem.

00:59:56 --> 00:59:59
You should be very honest with yourself

00:59:58 --> 01:00:01
and ask yourself that question.

01:00:00 --> 01:00:03
Is it because I'm on the left or on the right,

01:00:02 --> 01:00:05
that I want to slow this down?

01:00:04 --> 01:00:06
Versus is it hate,

01:00:07 --> 01:00:10
bipartisan hate speech?

01:00:12 --> 01:00:14
It's really tricky.

01:00:13 --> 01:00:17
But, like you, I'm very uncomfortable in general,

01:00:16 --> 01:00:18
with any kind of slowing down,

01:00:17 --> 01:00:19
with any kind of censorship.

01:00:18 --> 01:00:22
But, if there's something like a conspiracy theory

01:00:21 --> 01:00:25
that spreads hate, that becomes viral,

01:00:26 --> 01:00:31
I still lean to let that conspiracy theory spread

01:00:30 --> 01:00:35
'cause the alternative is dangerous, more dangerous.

01:00:34 --> 01:00:36
- It's sort like the ring of power.

01:00:35 --> 01:00:37
Everybody wants the ring.

01:00:36 --> 01:00:39
'Cause with the ring you can stop the bad guys

01:00:38 --> 01:00:40
from going forward.

01:00:39 --> 01:00:42
But, it turns out that the ring gives you enormous power

01:00:41 --> 01:00:45
and that power can be used in the wrong ways too.

01:00:44 --> 01:00:47
- You had The Daily Wire;

01:00:46 --> 01:00:50
which I'm a member of.

01:00:49 --> 01:00:51
(hearty laughing)

01:00:50 --> 01:00:52
- I appreciate that, thank you.

01:00:51 --> 01:00:53
- I recommend everybody sign up to it,

01:00:52 --> 01:00:55
it should be part of your regular diet;

01:00:54 --> 01:00:56
whether you're on the left and the right,

01:00:55 --> 01:00:58
the far left or the far right,

01:00:57 --> 01:01:00
everybody, should be part of your regular diet.

01:00:59 --> 01:01:01
Okay, that said,

01:01:01 --> 01:01:05
do you worry about the audience capture aspect of it

01:01:04 --> 01:01:08
because it is a platform for conservatives

01:01:07 --> 01:01:11
and you have a powerful voice on there.

01:01:12 --> 01:01:17
It might be difficult for you to go against

01:01:16 --> 01:01:20
he talking points or against the stream of ideas

01:01:19 --> 01:01:24
that is usually connected to conservative thought.

01:01:23 --> 01:01:25
Do you worry about that?

01:01:24 --> 01:01:26
- Well, I mean,

01:01:25 --> 01:01:28
the audience would obviously be upset with me

01:01:27 --> 01:01:29
and would have a right to be upset with me

01:01:28 --> 01:01:32
if I suddenly flipped all of my positions on a dime.

01:01:31 --> 01:01:34
I have enough faith in my audience that I can say things

01:01:33 --> 01:01:36
that I think are true and that may disagree

01:01:35 --> 01:01:40
with the audience on a fairly regular basis, I would say.

01:01:39 --> 01:01:42
But they understand that on the deeper principle

01:01:41 --> 01:01:44
or on the same side,

01:01:43 --> 01:01:46
or at least I hope that much from the audience.

01:01:45 --> 01:01:49
It's also why we provide a number of different views

01:01:48 --> 01:01:51
on the platforms, many of which I disagree with,

01:01:50 --> 01:01:53
but are sort of within the generalized range

01:01:52 --> 01:01:54
of conservative thought.

01:01:55 --> 01:01:58
It's something I do have to think about every day though.

01:01:57 --> 01:01:59
Yeah, I mean you have to, you have to think about ,

01:01:58 --> 01:02:01
"Am I saying this because I'm afraid

01:02:00 --> 01:02:02
"Of ticking off my audience

01:02:01 --> 01:02:05
"Or am I saying this because I actually believe this?"

01:02:04 --> 01:02:08
And you know, that's a delicate dance a little bit.

01:02:07 --> 01:02:11
You have to be sort of honest with yourself.

01:02:10 --> 01:02:15
- Yeah, somebody like Sam Harris is pretty good at this,

01:02:14 --> 01:02:17
at fighting, at saying

01:02:16 --> 01:02:20
the most outrageous thing that he knows.

01:02:19 --> 01:02:21
He almost leans into it,

01:02:21 --> 01:02:25
he knows will piss off a lot of his audience.

01:02:24 --> 01:02:27
Sometimes you almost have to test the system.

01:02:27 --> 01:02:29
It's like if you feel,

01:02:28 --> 01:02:33
you almost exaggerate your feelings just to make sure

01:02:32 --> 01:02:34
to send a signal to the audience

01:02:33 --> 01:02:36
that you're not captured by them.

01:02:36 --> 01:02:39
So speaking of people you disagree with,

01:02:39 --> 01:02:43
what is your favorite thing about Candace Owens,

01:02:42 --> 01:02:47
and what is one thing you disagree with her on?

01:02:46 --> 01:02:48
- Well, my favorite thing about Candace

01:02:47 --> 01:02:51
is that she will say things that nobody else will say.

01:02:50 --> 01:02:53
My least favorite thing about Candace

01:02:52 --> 01:02:55
is that she will say things that nobody else will say.

01:02:54 --> 01:02:57
(hearty laughing)

01:02:56 --> 01:02:58
I mean, listen,

01:02:57 --> 01:02:59
she says things that are audacious

01:02:58 --> 01:03:00
and I think need to be said sometimes.

01:02:59 --> 01:03:03
Sometimes, I think that she is morally wrong,

01:03:02 --> 01:03:04
I think the way she responded to Kanye,

01:03:03 --> 01:03:08
I've said this clearly, was dead wrong and morally wrong.

01:03:07 --> 01:03:09
- What was her response?

01:03:08 --> 01:03:12
- Her original response was that she proffered confusion

01:03:11 --> 01:03:15
of what Ye was actually talking about.

01:03:14 --> 01:03:18
And then, she was defending her friend.

01:03:17 --> 01:03:20
I wish that the way that she had responded was by saying,

01:03:19 --> 01:03:21
"He's my friend.

01:03:20 --> 01:03:24
"And also he said something bad and antisemitic."

01:03:23 --> 01:03:26
I wish that she had said that.

01:03:25 --> 01:03:27
- [Lex] Right away.

01:03:26 --> 01:03:28
- Right away.

01:03:27 --> 01:03:31
- Yeah, I think you can also,

01:03:30 --> 01:03:32
this is the interesting human thing.

01:03:31 --> 01:03:35
You can be friends with people that you disagree with

01:03:34 --> 01:03:36
and you can be friends with people

01:03:35 --> 01:03:38
that actually say hateful stuff.

01:03:37 --> 01:03:41
And, one of the ways to help alleviate hate

01:03:40 --> 01:03:42
is being friends with people

01:03:41 --> 01:03:45
that say hateful things.

01:03:44 --> 01:03:48
- Yeah, and then calling them out on a personal level,

01:03:47 --> 01:03:51
when they do say wrong or hateful things.

01:03:50 --> 01:03:54
- Yeah, from a place of love and respect and privately.

01:03:53 --> 01:03:55
- Privately is also a big thing, right?

01:03:54 --> 01:03:59
I mean, the public demand for denunciation,

01:03:58 --> 01:04:03
from friends to friends, is difficult.

01:04:02 --> 01:04:06
And, I certainly have compassion for Candace,

01:04:05 --> 01:04:08
given the fact that she's so close with Ye.

01:04:07 --> 01:04:09
- Yeah, it breaks my heart sometimes,

01:04:08 --> 01:04:13
the public fights between friends and broken friendships.

01:04:12 --> 01:04:18
I've seen quite a few friendships publicly break over COVID.

01:04:17 --> 01:04:22
COVID made people behave their worst, in many cases.

01:04:21 --> 01:04:27
Which yeah, breaks my heart a little bit.

01:04:26 --> 01:04:30
'Cause like the human connection

01:04:29 --> 01:04:33
is a prerequisite for effective debate

01:04:32 --> 01:04:35
and discussion and battles over ideas.

01:04:35 --> 01:04:39
Has there been any argument from the opposite political

01:04:38 --> 01:04:41
isle that has made you change your mind about something?

01:04:41 --> 01:04:44
If you look back.

01:04:45 --> 01:04:47
- So I will say that,

01:04:50 --> 01:04:52
I'm thinking it through

01:04:51 --> 01:04:55
because I think that my views probably on foreign policy,

01:04:54 --> 01:04:57
morphed somewhat.

01:04:56 --> 01:04:58
I would say that I was much more interventionist

01:04:57 --> 01:05:00
when I was younger.

01:04:59 --> 01:05:02
I'm significantly less interventionist now.

01:05:01 --> 01:05:03
I'd probably-

01:05:02 --> 01:05:04
Can you give an example?

01:05:03 --> 01:05:05
- Sure I was a big backer of the Iraq War.

01:05:04 --> 01:05:07
I think now, in retrospect,

01:05:06 --> 01:05:08
I might not be a backer of the Iraq War,

01:05:07 --> 01:05:10
if the same situation arose again,

01:05:10 --> 01:05:14
based on the amount of evidence that had been presented

01:05:13 --> 01:05:18
or based on the sort of willingness of the American public

01:05:17 --> 01:05:20
to go it, right.

01:05:19 --> 01:05:21
If you're gonna get involved in a war,

01:05:20 --> 01:05:22
you have to know what the end point looks like,

01:05:21 --> 01:05:24
and you have to know what the American people really are

01:05:23 --> 01:05:25
willing to bear.

01:05:24 --> 01:05:26
The American people are not willing

01:05:25 --> 01:05:29
to bear open-ended occupations.

01:05:28 --> 01:05:33
And so knowing that, you have to consider that going in.

01:05:32 --> 01:05:34
So, on foreign policy,

01:05:33 --> 01:05:36
I've become a lot more of,

01:05:35 --> 01:05:38
I'd say almost Henry Kissinger realist

01:05:37 --> 01:05:39
in some ways.

01:05:39 --> 01:05:44
When it comes to social policy,

01:05:43 --> 01:05:47
I would say that, I'm fairly strong

01:05:46 --> 01:05:48
where I was.

01:05:48 --> 01:05:51
I may have become slightly convinced

01:05:50 --> 01:05:52
actually by more of the conservative side

01:05:51 --> 01:05:54
of the isle and things like drug legalization.

01:05:53 --> 01:05:55
I think when I was younger,

01:05:54 --> 01:05:58
I was much more pro-drug legalization then I am now,

01:05:57 --> 01:05:58
at least on the local level.

01:05:57 --> 01:05:59
On a federal level,

01:05:58 --> 01:06:02
I think the federal government can't really do much,

01:06:01 --> 01:06:03
other than close the borders

01:06:02 --> 01:06:05
with regard to fentanyl trafficking, for example.

01:06:04 --> 01:06:07
But, when it comes to how drugs were in local communities,

01:06:06 --> 01:06:08
you can see

01:06:07 --> 01:06:10
how drugs were in local communities pretty easily.

01:06:09 --> 01:06:13
- Which is weird because I saw you smoke a joint

01:06:12 --> 01:06:14
right before this conversation.

01:06:13 --> 01:06:15
- It's my biggest thing.

01:06:14 --> 01:06:16
I mean, I try to keep that secret.

01:06:16 --> 01:06:20
- Well that's interesting about intervention.

01:06:19 --> 01:06:22
Can you comment about the war in Ukraine?

01:06:21 --> 01:06:25
So for me, it's a deeply personal thing,

01:06:25 --> 01:06:28
but I think you're able to look at it

01:06:27 --> 01:06:30
from a geopolitics perspective.

01:06:29 --> 01:06:32
What is the role of the United States in this conflict

01:06:31 --> 01:06:33
before the conflict,

01:06:32 --> 01:06:34
during the conflict,

01:06:33 --> 01:06:39
and right now, in helping achieve peace?

01:06:38 --> 01:06:40
- I think before the conflict,

01:06:39 --> 01:06:43
the big problem is that the West took

01:06:42 --> 01:06:44
almost the worst possible view;

01:06:43 --> 01:06:48
which was encourage Ukraine to keep trying to join NATO

01:06:47 --> 01:06:49
and the EU, but don't let them in.

01:06:48 --> 01:06:52
And so, what that does is it achieves the purpose

01:06:51 --> 01:06:54
of getting Russia really, really, really ticked off

01:06:53 --> 01:06:55
and feeling threatened.

01:06:54 --> 01:06:58
But also, does not give any of the protections of NATO

01:06:57 --> 01:07:00
or the EU to Ukraine.

01:06:59 --> 01:07:03
Zelensky is on film when he was a comedy actor

01:07:02 --> 01:07:05
making that exact joke.

01:07:04 --> 01:07:06
He has Merkel on the other line and she's like,

01:07:05 --> 01:07:08
"Oh welcome to NATO."

01:07:07 --> 01:07:09
And he's like, "Great."

01:07:08 --> 01:07:10
And she's like,

01:07:09 --> 01:07:11
"Wait, is this Ukraine on the line?

01:07:11 --> 01:07:13
"And, oops."

01:07:12 --> 01:07:15
But so, that sort of policy

01:07:14 --> 01:07:16
is sort of nonsensical.

01:07:15 --> 01:07:18
If you're gonna offer alliance to somebody,

01:07:17 --> 01:07:19
offer alliance to them.

01:07:18 --> 01:07:20
And if you're going to guarantee their security,

01:07:19 --> 01:07:21
guarantee their security.

01:07:20 --> 01:07:24
And, the West failed signally to do that.

01:07:23 --> 01:07:27
So, that was mistakes in the run up to the war.

01:07:26 --> 01:07:28
Once the war began,

01:07:27 --> 01:07:33
then the responsibility of the West became to give Ukraine

01:07:32 --> 01:07:37
as much material as is necessary to repel the invasion.

01:07:36 --> 01:07:40
And, the West did really well with that.

01:07:39 --> 01:07:42
I think we were late on the ball in the United States.

01:07:41 --> 01:07:43
It seemed like Europe led the way a little bit more

01:07:42 --> 01:07:45
than the United States did there.

01:07:44 --> 01:07:48
But, in terms of effectuating American interests

01:07:47 --> 01:07:49
in the region;

01:07:48 --> 01:07:50
which being an American,

01:07:49 --> 01:07:53
is what I'm chiefly concerned about.

01:07:52 --> 01:07:55
The American interests were several fold.

01:07:54 --> 01:07:57
One is preserve borders.

01:07:56 --> 01:08:00
Two is degrade the Russian aggressive military

01:07:59 --> 01:08:02
because Russia's military has been aggressive,

01:08:01 --> 01:08:05
and they are geopolitical rival of the United States.

01:08:04 --> 01:08:08
Three, recalibrate the European balance with China.

01:08:07 --> 01:08:10
Europe was sort of balancing with Russia and China.

01:08:09 --> 01:08:13
And then, because of the war they sort of rebalanced away

01:08:12 --> 01:08:14
from China and Russia;

01:08:13 --> 01:08:16
which is a real geostrategic opportunity

01:08:15 --> 01:08:18
for the United States.

01:08:17 --> 01:08:20
It seemed like most of those goals

01:08:19 --> 01:08:21
have already been achieved at this point

01:08:20 --> 01:08:22
for the United States.

01:08:21 --> 01:08:25
And so then, the question becomes, what's the off ramp here,

01:08:24 --> 01:08:26
and what is the thing you're trying to prevent?

01:08:25 --> 01:08:27
So what's the best opportunity?

01:08:26 --> 01:08:28
What's the best case scenario?

01:08:27 --> 01:08:29
What's the worst case scenario?

01:08:28 --> 01:08:30
And then, what's realistic?

01:08:29 --> 01:08:34
So best case scenario is Ukraine forces Russia entirely out

01:08:33 --> 01:08:36
of Ukraine; including Luhansk, Donetsk, and Crimea, right.

01:08:35 --> 01:08:37
That's the best case scenario.

01:08:36 --> 01:08:39
Virtually no one thinks that's accomplishable;

01:08:38 --> 01:08:40
including the United States.

01:08:39 --> 01:08:42
The White House has basically said as much.

01:08:41 --> 01:08:44
It's difficult to imagine, particularly Crimea,

01:08:43 --> 01:08:47
the Russians being forced out of Crimea.

01:08:46 --> 01:08:50
The Ukrainians have been successful in pushing the Russians

01:08:49 --> 01:08:52
out of certain parts of Luhansk and Donetsk.

01:08:51 --> 01:08:53
But, the idea they're gonna be able to push

01:08:52 --> 01:08:54
the entire Russian army

01:08:53 --> 01:08:57
completely back to the Russian borders.

01:08:56 --> 01:08:58
That would be at best a very,

01:08:57 --> 01:09:00
very long and difficult slog,

01:08:59 --> 01:09:02
in the middle of a collapsing Ukrainian economy.

01:09:01 --> 01:09:04
Which is a point that Zelensky has made.

01:09:03 --> 01:09:06
It's not enough for you guys to give us military aid.

01:09:05 --> 01:09:06
We're in the middle of a war,

01:09:05 --> 01:09:07
we're gonna need economic aid as well.

01:09:06 --> 01:09:10
So it's a pretty open-ended and strong commitment.

01:09:09 --> 01:09:11
- Can I take a small tangent on that?

01:09:10 --> 01:09:12
- [Ben] Sure.

01:09:11 --> 01:09:13
- And best case scenario?

01:09:12 --> 01:09:17
If that does militarily happen, including Crimea,

01:09:16 --> 01:09:20
do you think there's a world in which Vladimir Putin,

01:09:19 --> 01:09:25
would be able to convince the Russian people

01:09:24 --> 01:09:27
that this was a good conclusion to the war?

01:09:26 --> 01:09:30
- Right, the problem is that the best case scenario,

01:09:29 --> 01:09:31
might also be the worst case scenario.

01:09:30 --> 01:09:33
Meaning that there are a couple of scenarios

01:09:32 --> 01:09:35
that are sort of the worst case scenario.

01:09:34 --> 01:09:37
And this, is sort of the puzzlement of the situation.

01:09:36 --> 01:09:39
One, is that Putin feels so boxed in,

01:09:38 --> 01:09:41
so unable to go back to his own people and say,

01:09:40 --> 01:09:43
"We just wasted tens of thousands of lives here

01:09:42 --> 01:09:44
"For no reason."

01:09:43 --> 01:09:47
That he unleashes a tactical nuclear weapon

01:09:46 --> 01:09:48
the battlefield.

01:09:47 --> 01:09:49
Nobody knows what happens after that.

01:09:48 --> 01:09:52
So we put NATO planes in the air to take out Russian assets.

01:09:51 --> 01:09:55
Do Russians start shooting down planes?

01:09:54 --> 01:09:57
Does Russia then threaten to escalate even further

01:09:56 --> 01:09:59
by attacking an actual NATO civilian center,

01:09:58 --> 01:10:02
or even a Ukrainian civilian center with nuclear weapons?

01:10:01 --> 01:10:03
Like where it goes from there, nobody knows.

01:10:02 --> 01:10:06
'Cause nuclear weapons haven't been used since 1945.

01:10:05 --> 01:10:09
So, that is a worst case scenario.

01:10:08 --> 01:10:10
It's an unpredictable scenario that could devolve

01:10:09 --> 01:10:13
into really, really significant problems.

01:10:12 --> 01:10:15
The other worst case scenario,

01:10:14 --> 01:10:15
could be a best case scenario,

01:10:14 --> 01:10:16
it could be a worse,

01:10:15 --> 01:10:18
we just don't know is Putin falls.

01:10:17 --> 01:10:20
What happens after that?

01:10:19 --> 01:10:21
Who takes over for Putin?

01:10:20 --> 01:10:23
Is that person more moderate than Putin?

01:10:22 --> 01:10:25
Is that person a liberalizer?

01:10:24 --> 01:10:26
It probably won't (indistinct),

01:10:25 --> 01:10:28
if he's gonna be ousted.

01:10:27 --> 01:10:31
It'll probably somebody who's a top member of Putin's brass

01:10:30 --> 01:10:34
right now, and has capacity to control the military.

01:10:33 --> 01:10:36
Or it's possible the entire regime breaks down,

01:10:35 --> 01:10:38
and what you end up with is Syria in Russia.

01:10:37 --> 01:10:41
Where you just have an entirely out of control region

01:10:40 --> 01:10:43
with no centralizing power;

01:10:42 --> 01:10:44
which is also a disaster area.

01:10:43 --> 01:10:48
And so, in the nature of risk mitigation,

01:10:47 --> 01:10:50
in sort of an attempt at risk mitigation,

01:10:49 --> 01:10:54
what actually should be happening right now is some off ramp

01:10:53 --> 01:10:56
has to be offered to Putin.

01:10:55 --> 01:10:58
The off ramp likely is going to be him maintaining Crimea

01:10:57 --> 01:10:59
nd parts of Luhansk and Donetsk.

01:10:58 --> 01:11:02
It's probably gonna be a commitment by Ukraine,

01:11:01 --> 01:11:05
not to join NATO formally,

01:11:04 --> 01:11:08
but a guarantee by the West to defend Ukraine

01:11:07 --> 01:11:12
in case of an invasion of its borders, again, by Russia.

01:11:11 --> 01:11:13
Like an actual treaty obligation, not like,

01:11:12 --> 01:11:16
the BS treaty obligation and win-win Ukraine gave up

01:11:15 --> 01:11:19
its nuclear weapons in the 90s.

01:11:18 --> 01:11:22
And, that is likely how this is gonna have to go.

01:11:21 --> 01:11:24
The problem is that requires political courage,

01:11:23 --> 01:11:26
not from Zelensky.

01:11:25 --> 01:11:28
It requires courage from probably Biden

01:11:27 --> 01:11:31
because the only, Zelensky is not in a political position,

01:11:30 --> 01:11:32
where he can go back to his own people,

01:11:31 --> 01:11:34
who have made unbelievable sacrifices on behalf

01:11:33 --> 01:11:36
of their nation and freedom and say to them,

01:11:35 --> 01:11:38
"Guys, now I'm calling it quits.

01:11:37 --> 01:11:39
"We're gonna have to give them Luhansk and Donetsk

01:11:38 --> 01:11:40
"And give Putin an off ramp."

01:11:39 --> 01:11:42
I don't think that's an acceptable answer to most Ukrainians

01:11:41 --> 01:11:43
at this point in time,

01:11:42 --> 01:11:45
from the polling data and from the available data we have

01:11:44 --> 01:11:46
on the ground.

01:11:45 --> 01:11:49
It's gonna actually take Biden biting the bullet,

01:11:48 --> 01:11:50
and being the bad guy and saying to Zelensky,

01:11:49 --> 01:11:54
"Listen, we've made a commitment of material aid.

01:11:53 --> 01:11:55
"We're offering you all these things,

01:11:54 --> 01:11:58
"Including essentially a defense pact.

01:11:57 --> 01:11:59
"We're offering you all this stuff,

01:11:58 --> 01:12:01
"But, if you don't come to the table,

01:12:00 --> 01:12:03
"Then we're gonna have to start weaning you all."

01:12:02 --> 01:12:04
There will have to be a stick there,

01:12:03 --> 01:12:06
it can't just be a carrot.

01:12:05 --> 01:12:08
And so, that will allow Zelensky, if Biden were to do that,

01:12:08 --> 01:12:11
it allows Zelensky to blame Biden for the solution

01:12:10 --> 01:12:11
everybody knows has to happen.

01:12:10 --> 01:12:13
Zelensky can go back to his own people and he can say,

01:12:12 --> 01:12:16
"Listen, this is the way it has to go.

01:12:15 --> 01:12:18
"I don't want it to go this way, but it's not my,

01:12:17 --> 01:12:20
"I'm signing other people's checks.

01:12:19 --> 01:12:23
"I mean, it's not my money."

01:12:22 --> 01:12:24
And Biden would take the hit

01:12:23 --> 01:12:27
because he wouldn't then be able to blame Ukraine

01:12:26 --> 01:12:28
for whatever happens next.

01:12:27 --> 01:12:29
Which has been the easy road off,

01:12:28 --> 01:12:30
I think for a lot of politicians in the West.

01:12:29 --> 01:12:32
Is for them to just say,

01:12:31 --> 01:12:33
Well, this is up to the Ukrainians to decide.

01:12:32 --> 01:12:34
It's up to the Ukrainians to decide.

01:12:33 --> 01:12:37
Well, is it totally up to the Ukrainians to decide?

01:12:36 --> 01:12:39
Because it seems like the West is signing an awful lot

01:12:39 --> 01:12:42
of checks and all of Europe is gonna freeze this winter.

01:12:42 --> 01:12:46
- This is the importance of great leadership by the way.

01:12:45 --> 01:12:48
That's why the people we elect is very important.

01:12:48 --> 01:12:54
Do you think there's power to just one on one conversation?

01:12:55 --> 01:12:57
Where Biden sits down with Zelensky,

01:12:56 --> 01:13:01
and Biden sits down with Putin, almost in person.

01:13:00 --> 01:13:04
'Cause I, or maybe I'm romanticizing the notion,

01:13:03 --> 01:13:05
but having done these podcasts in person,

01:13:04 --> 01:13:07
I think there's something fundamentally different

01:13:06 --> 01:13:10
han through a remote call,

01:13:09 --> 01:13:15
and also a distant kind of recorded political

01:13:14 --> 01:13:17
type speak versus like man to man.

01:13:16 --> 01:13:21
- So I'm deeply afraid that Putin outplays people

01:13:20 --> 01:13:22
in the one-on-one scenarios

01:13:21 --> 01:13:24
because he's done it to multiple presidents already.

01:13:23 --> 01:13:27
He gets in one-on-one scenarios with Bush, with Obama,

01:13:26 --> 01:13:28
with Trump, with Biden;

01:13:27 --> 01:13:30
and he seems to be a very canny operator

01:13:29 --> 01:13:34
and a very sort of hard-nosed operator in those situations.

01:13:33 --> 01:13:36
I think that if you were gonna do something like that,

01:13:35 --> 01:13:37
an actual political face-to-face summit,

01:13:36 --> 01:13:38
what you would need is for Biden

01:13:37 --> 01:13:40
to first have a conversation with Zelensky.

01:13:39 --> 01:13:41
Where Zelensky knows what's going on.

01:13:40 --> 01:13:43
So he's aware,

01:13:42 --> 01:13:47
and then Biden walks in and he says to Putin on camera,

01:13:46 --> 01:13:51
"Here's the offer, let's get it together,

01:13:50 --> 01:13:53
"Let's make peace.

01:13:52 --> 01:13:55
"You get to keep this stuff."

01:13:54 --> 01:13:57
And then, let Putin respond,

01:13:56 --> 01:13:58
how Putin is going to respond.

01:13:59 --> 01:14:02
But, the big problem for Putin I think,

01:14:01 --> 01:14:04
and the problem with public facing forum,

01:14:03 --> 01:14:05
maybe it's a private meeting.

01:14:04 --> 01:14:05
If it's a private meeting,

01:14:04 --> 01:14:06
maybe that's the best thing.

01:14:05 --> 01:14:07
If it's a public facing forum,

01:14:06 --> 01:14:09
I think it's a problem 'cause Putin's afraid

01:14:08 --> 01:14:10
of being humiliated at this point.

01:14:09 --> 01:14:15
If it's a private meeting, then sure, except that again,

01:14:14 --> 01:14:17
I just wonder whether,

01:14:17 --> 01:14:22
when it comes to a person as canny as Putin,

01:14:21 --> 01:14:25
and to a politician that I really don't think

01:14:24 --> 01:14:28
is a particularly sophisticated player in Joe Biden.

01:14:27 --> 01:14:30
And again, this is not unique to Biden.

01:14:29 --> 01:14:33
I think that most of our presidents for the last 30,

01:14:33 --> 01:14:36
40 years have not been particularly sophisticated players.

01:14:36 --> 01:14:39
I think that's a risky scenario.

01:14:40 --> 01:14:43
- Yeah, I still believe in the power of that.

01:14:42 --> 01:14:44
'Cause otherwise,

01:14:45 --> 01:14:48
I don't think stuff on paper

01:14:47 --> 01:14:51
and political speak will solve these kinds of problems.

01:14:50 --> 01:14:53
Because from the Zelensky perspective,

01:14:52 --> 01:14:56
nothing but complete victory will do.

01:14:55 --> 01:14:57
- [Ben] Right.

01:14:56 --> 01:15:00
- As a nation has people sacrificed way too much

01:14:59 --> 01:15:01
and they're all in.

01:15:00 --> 01:15:03
And if you look at,

01:15:02 --> 01:15:05
'cause I traveled to Ukraine, I spent time there,

01:15:04 --> 01:15:06
I'll be going back there hopefully.

01:15:06 --> 01:15:11
Also, going back to Russia, just speaking to Ukrainians.

01:15:10 --> 01:15:14
They're all in, they're all in.

01:15:13 --> 01:15:15
- Yeah.

01:15:14 --> 01:15:16
- Nothing, but don't complete victory.

01:15:15 --> 01:15:18
- Yep, don't blame them, that's right.

01:15:17 --> 01:15:19
- And so for that,

01:15:18 --> 01:15:22
the only way to achieve peace

01:15:21 --> 01:15:27
is through honest human to human conversation.

01:15:26 --> 01:15:30
Giving both people a way to off ramp,

01:15:29 --> 01:15:32
to walk away victorious.

01:15:31 --> 01:15:36
And some of that requires speaking honestly

01:15:35 --> 01:15:37
as a human being,

01:15:36 --> 01:15:40
but also for America to actually,

01:15:39 --> 01:15:41
not even America, honestly,

01:15:40 --> 01:15:45
just the president, be able to eat their own ego a bit,

01:15:44 --> 01:15:47
and be the punching bag a little,

01:15:46 --> 01:15:50
just enough for both presidents to be able to walk away

01:15:49 --> 01:15:54
and say, "Listen, we got the American president

01:15:53 --> 01:15:55
"To come to us.

01:15:54 --> 01:15:58
"And, I think that makes the President

01:15:57 --> 01:15:59
"Look strong, not weak."

01:15:58 --> 01:16:00
- I mean, I agree with you.

01:16:00 --> 01:16:03
I think it would also require some people on the right,

01:16:02 --> 01:16:05
people like me, if it's Joe Biden to say,

01:16:04 --> 01:16:06
"If Biden does that, I see what he's doing.

01:16:05 --> 01:16:08
"It's the right move."

01:16:07 --> 01:16:09
I think one of the things that he's afraid of,

01:16:08 --> 01:16:10
to steel man him.

01:16:09 --> 01:16:12
I think one of the things he's afraid of is,

01:16:11 --> 01:16:13
he goes and he makes that sort of deal,

01:16:12 --> 01:16:15
and the right says "You just coward in front of Russia,

01:16:14 --> 01:16:17
"You just gave away Ukraine."

01:16:16 --> 01:16:18
Whatever it is.

01:16:17 --> 01:16:20
But, it's gonna require some people on the right

01:16:19 --> 01:16:22
o say that that move is the right move,

01:16:21 --> 01:16:23
and then hold by it.

01:16:22 --> 01:16:25
If Biden actually performs that move.

01:16:24 --> 01:16:27
- You're exceptionally good at debate.

01:16:27 --> 01:16:33
You wrote How to Debate the Leftist and Destroy Them.

01:16:32 --> 01:16:34
You're kind of known for this kind of stuff,

01:16:33 --> 01:16:36
just exceptionally skilled at conversation,

01:16:35 --> 01:16:40
at debate, at getting to the facts of the matter

01:16:39 --> 01:16:44
and using logic to get to the conclusion in the debate.

01:16:43 --> 01:16:46
Do you ever worry that this power,

01:16:45 --> 01:16:48
you talked about the ring,

01:16:48 --> 01:16:53
this power you were given has corrupted you

01:16:52 --> 01:16:56
and your ability to see what's...

01:16:55 --> 01:16:59
To pursue the truth versus just winning debates?

01:16:58 --> 01:17:00
- I hope not.

01:16:59 --> 01:17:01
So I think one of the things

01:17:00 --> 01:17:04
that's kind of funny about the branding versus the reality,

01:17:03 --> 01:17:06
is that most of the things that get characterized

01:17:05 --> 01:17:09
as destroying in debates with facts and logic,

01:17:08 --> 01:17:12
most of those things are basically me having a conversation

01:17:11 --> 01:17:14
with somebody on a college campus.

01:17:13 --> 01:17:16
It actually isn't like a formal debate where we sit there

01:17:15 --> 01:17:18
and we critique each other's positions,

01:17:17 --> 01:17:20
or it's not me insulting anybody.

01:17:19 --> 01:17:21
A lot of the clips that have gone very viral

01:17:20 --> 01:17:22
is me making an argument,

01:17:21 --> 01:17:24
and then there not being an amazing counter argument.

01:17:23 --> 01:17:25
- [Lex] Yep.

01:17:24 --> 01:17:27
- Many of the debates that I've held

01:17:26 --> 01:17:28
have been extremely cordial.

01:17:27 --> 01:17:30
To take the latest example like about a year ago

01:17:29 --> 01:17:32
I debated Ana Kasparian from Young Turks.

01:17:31 --> 01:17:33
It was very cordial,

01:17:32 --> 01:17:33
it was very nice.

01:17:34 --> 01:17:37
Yeah, that's sort of the way that I like to debate.

01:17:36 --> 01:17:41
My rule when it comes to debate and or discussion,

01:17:40 --> 01:17:43
is that my opponent actually gets to pick the mode

01:17:42 --> 01:17:44
in which we work.

01:17:43 --> 01:17:47
So if it's going to be a debate of ideas

01:17:46 --> 01:17:50
and we're just going to discuss and critique and clarify,

01:17:49 --> 01:17:51
then we can do that.

01:17:50 --> 01:17:52
If somebody comes loaded for bear,

01:17:51 --> 01:17:56
then I will respond in kind.

01:17:55 --> 01:17:58
Because one of the big problems,

01:17:57 --> 01:18:01
I think in sort of the debate slash discussion sphere

01:18:00 --> 01:18:03
is very often misdiagnosis

01:18:02 --> 01:18:04
of what exactly is going on.

01:18:03 --> 01:18:06
People who think that a discussion is debate

01:18:05 --> 01:18:07
and vice versa.

01:18:06 --> 01:18:10
And that can be a real problem.

01:18:09 --> 01:18:11
And there are people

01:18:10 --> 01:18:15
who will treat what ought to be a discussion,

01:18:14 --> 01:18:16
as for example,

01:18:15 --> 01:18:18
an exercise in performance art.

01:18:17 --> 01:18:19
And so what that is,

01:18:18 --> 01:18:20
is mugging or trolling,

01:18:19 --> 01:18:21
or saying trolley things

01:18:20 --> 01:18:23
in order to just get,

01:18:22 --> 01:18:24
that's something I actually don't do during debate.

01:18:23 --> 01:18:26
If you actually watch me talk to people,

01:18:25 --> 01:18:27
I don't actually do the trolling thing.

01:18:26 --> 01:18:30
The trolling thing is almost solely relegated to Twitter

01:18:29 --> 01:18:31
and me making jokes on my show.

01:18:30 --> 01:18:33
When it comes to actually debating people,

01:18:32 --> 01:18:36
that sounds actually a lot like what we're doing right now

01:18:35 --> 01:18:37
is just the person

01:18:36 --> 01:18:40
maybe taking just an adverse position to mine.

01:18:39 --> 01:18:41
And so, that's fine.

01:18:40 --> 01:18:43
Usually half the debate or discussion

01:18:42 --> 01:18:45
is me just asking for clarification of terms.

01:18:44 --> 01:18:47
What exactly do you mean by this?

01:18:46 --> 01:18:49
So I can drill down on

01:18:48 --> 01:18:50
where the actual disagreement may lie.

01:18:49 --> 01:18:52
Because some of the time people think they're disagreeing

01:18:51 --> 01:18:54
and they're actually not disagreeing.

01:18:53 --> 01:18:56
When I'm talking with Ana Kasparian,

01:18:55 --> 01:18:57
and she's talking about how corporate and government

01:18:56 --> 01:18:58
have too much power together, I'm like,

01:18:57 --> 01:19:00
"Well, you sound like a tea party."

01:18:59 --> 01:19:01
Like you and I are on the same page about that.

01:19:00 --> 01:19:02
That sort of stuff does tend to happen

01:19:01 --> 01:19:04
a lot in discussion.

01:19:03 --> 01:19:07
I think that when discussion gets termed debate,

01:19:06 --> 01:19:08
it's a problem.

01:19:07 --> 01:19:09
When debate gets term discussion,

01:19:08 --> 01:19:10
it's even more problematic

01:19:09 --> 01:19:12
because debate is a different thing.

01:19:11 --> 01:19:14
- And I find that your debate and your conversation

01:19:13 --> 01:19:15
is often in good faith,

01:19:14 --> 01:19:16
you're able to steel man on the other side.

01:19:15 --> 01:19:18
You're actually listening,

01:19:17 --> 01:19:20
you're considering the other side.

01:19:19 --> 01:19:22
The times when I see that you,

01:19:21 --> 01:19:24
venture pure destroys leftist,

01:19:23 --> 01:19:25
it's usually just like you said,

01:19:24 --> 01:19:27
the other side is doing the trolling

01:19:27 --> 01:19:30
because they didn't...

01:19:29 --> 01:19:35
I mean the people that do criticize you for that interaction

01:19:34 --> 01:19:37
is the people that usually get destroyed

01:19:36 --> 01:19:39
are like 20 years old,

01:19:38 --> 01:19:41
and they're usually not sophisticated

01:19:40 --> 01:19:43
in any kind of degree,

01:19:42 --> 01:19:45
in terms of being able to use logic and reason,

01:19:44 --> 01:19:46
and facts and so on.

01:19:45 --> 01:19:48
- And, that's totally fine by the way.

01:19:47 --> 01:19:49
If people wanna criticize me for speaking

01:19:48 --> 01:19:50
on college campuses,

01:19:49 --> 01:19:52
where a lot of political conversation happens,

01:19:51 --> 01:19:54
both right and left, that's fine.

01:19:53 --> 01:19:56
I've had lots of conversations with people on the other side

01:19:55 --> 01:19:57
of the aisle too.

01:19:56 --> 01:19:58
I've done podcasts with Sam Harris,

01:19:57 --> 01:19:59
and we've talked about atheism.

01:19:58 --> 01:20:01
Or I've done debates with Anna Kasparian,

01:20:00 --> 01:20:03
or I've done debate with Cenk Uygur,

01:20:02 --> 01:20:05
or I've had conversations with lots of people

01:20:04 --> 01:20:06
on the other side of the aisle.

01:20:05 --> 01:20:08
In fact, I believe I'm the only person on the right

01:20:07 --> 01:20:10
who recommends that people listen to his shows

01:20:09 --> 01:20:11
on the other side of the aisle.

01:20:10 --> 01:20:13
I say on my show on a fairly regular basis,

01:20:12 --> 01:20:15
that people should listen to Positive America.

01:20:14 --> 01:20:17
Now, no one on Positive America will ever say

01:20:16 --> 01:20:18
that somebody should listen to my show.

01:20:17 --> 01:20:19
That is verboten.

01:20:18 --> 01:20:21
That is not something that can be had.

01:20:20 --> 01:20:23
It's one of the strangeness of our politics.

01:20:22 --> 01:20:25
It's what I've called the happy birthday problem;

01:20:24 --> 01:20:27
which is, I have a lot of friends who are of the left,

01:20:26 --> 01:20:29
and are publicly of the left.

01:20:28 --> 01:20:31
And, on my birthday they'll send you a text message,

01:20:30 --> 01:20:32
"Happy birthday."

01:20:31 --> 01:20:33
But, they will never tweet happy birthday,

01:20:32 --> 01:20:35
less they be acknowledging

01:20:34 --> 01:20:38
that you were born of woman and that this can't be allowed.

01:20:37 --> 01:20:39
So, on the Sunday Special,

01:20:38 --> 01:20:40
I've had a bevy of people

01:20:39 --> 01:20:42
who are on the other side of the aisle,

01:20:41 --> 01:20:45
a lot of them ranging from people in Hollywood,

01:20:44 --> 01:20:48
like Jason Blum to Larry Wilmore, to Sam,

01:20:47 --> 01:20:51
to just a lot of people on the left.

01:20:50 --> 01:20:53
I think, we're in the near future

01:20:52 --> 01:20:54
probably gonna do a Sunday Special

01:20:53 --> 01:20:55
with Ro Khanna in California.

01:20:54 --> 01:20:56
The California Congress person.

01:20:55 --> 01:20:58
Very nice guy, I had him on the show.

01:20:57 --> 01:21:01
That kinda stuff is fun and interesting.

01:21:00 --> 01:21:03
But, I think that the easy way out

01:21:02 --> 01:21:05
for a clip that people don't like,

01:21:04 --> 01:21:07
is to either immediately clip the clip.

01:21:06 --> 01:21:08
They'll take a two minute clip

01:21:07 --> 01:21:09
and clip it down to 15 seconds.

01:21:08 --> 01:21:11
Where somebody insults me and then that viral;

01:21:10 --> 01:21:13
which is, welcome to the internet.

01:21:12 --> 01:21:16
Or to say, "Well you're only debating in colleges,

01:21:15 --> 01:21:17
"You're only talking to 20."

01:21:16 --> 01:21:19
I talked to a lot more people than that.

01:21:18 --> 01:21:21
That's just not the stuff you're watching.

01:21:20 --> 01:21:22
- You lost your cool in an interview

01:21:21 --> 01:21:25
ith BBC's Andrew Neil,

01:21:24 --> 01:21:27
and you are really honest about it after;

01:21:26 --> 01:21:30
which was kind of refreshing and enjoyable.

01:21:29 --> 01:21:31
As the internet said,

01:21:30 --> 01:21:34
"They've never seen anyone lose an interview."

01:21:34 --> 01:21:37
So to me, honestly,

01:21:36 --> 01:21:40
it was like seeing like Floyd Mayweather Jr.

01:21:39 --> 01:21:41
Or somebody, knock down.

01:21:43 --> 01:21:45
Can you take me through that experience?

01:21:44 --> 01:21:46
- Here's that day.

01:21:45 --> 01:21:48
That day is, I have a book released,

01:21:47 --> 01:21:49
didn't get a lot of sleep the night before.

01:21:48 --> 01:21:51
And this is the last interview of the day,

01:21:50 --> 01:21:52
and it's an interview with BBC.

01:21:51 --> 01:21:53
I don't know anything about BBC,

01:21:52 --> 01:21:55
I don't watch BBC, I don't know any of the hosts.

01:21:54 --> 01:21:57
So we get on the interview

01:21:56 --> 01:21:59
and it's supposed to be about the book,

01:21:58 --> 01:22:02
and the host Andrew Neil,

01:22:01 --> 01:22:05
doesn't ask virtually a single question about the book.

01:22:04 --> 01:22:07
He just starts reading me battled tweets,

01:22:06 --> 01:22:08
which I hate.

01:22:07 --> 01:22:09
I mean, it's annoying and it's stupid,

01:22:08 --> 01:22:10
and it's the worst form of an interview.

01:22:09 --> 01:22:11
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:22:10 --> 01:22:12
- When somebody just reads you battled tweets,

01:22:11 --> 01:22:15
especially when I've acknowledged battled tweets before.

01:22:14 --> 01:22:17
And so, I'm going through the list with him

01:22:16 --> 01:22:18
and this interview was solidly 20 minutes.

01:22:17 --> 01:22:20
It was a long interview.

01:22:19 --> 01:22:21
And we get to,

01:22:20 --> 01:22:25
and I make a couple of particularly annoyed mistakes

01:22:24 --> 01:22:26
in the interview.

01:22:25 --> 01:22:30
So annoyed mistake number one, is the ego play,

01:22:29 --> 01:22:31
There's a point in the middle of the interview

01:22:30 --> 01:22:32
here I say,

01:22:31 --> 01:22:33
"I don't even know who you are."

01:22:32 --> 01:22:34
Which was true, I didn't know who he was.

01:22:33 --> 01:22:36
It turns out he was a very famous person in Britain.

01:22:35 --> 01:22:38
And so you can't make that ego play.

01:22:37 --> 01:22:40
- But even if he's not famous, that's not-

01:22:39 --> 01:22:41
It doesn't, it's a dumb thing to do

01:22:40 --> 01:22:42
and it's an ass thing to do.

01:22:41 --> 01:22:47
So saying that was more just kind of peak in silliness.

01:22:46 --> 01:22:49
And so, that was mistake number one.

01:22:48 --> 01:22:50
- I enjoyed watching that.

01:22:49 --> 01:22:52
It was like, oh Ben is human.

01:22:51 --> 01:22:53
(hearty laughing)

01:22:52 --> 01:22:55
- Glad somebody enjoyed it.

01:22:54 --> 01:22:56
So there was that.

01:22:55 --> 01:22:57
And then, the other mistake

01:22:56 --> 01:23:00
was that I just don't watch enough British TV.

01:22:59 --> 01:23:02
So the way that interviews are done there,

01:23:01 --> 01:23:04
are much more adversarial than American TV.

01:23:03 --> 01:23:06
In American TV, if somebody is adversarial with you,

01:23:05 --> 01:23:07
you assume that they're a member of the other side.

01:23:06 --> 01:23:09
That's typically how it is.

01:23:08 --> 01:23:11
And so, I'm critiquing some of his questions

01:23:10 --> 01:23:11
at the beginning

01:23:10 --> 01:23:13
and I thought that the critique of some of his questions

01:23:12 --> 01:23:14
is actually fair.

01:23:13 --> 01:23:15
He was asking me about abortion,

01:23:14 --> 01:23:17
and I thought he was asking it from a way

01:23:16 --> 01:23:19
of framing the question that wasn't accurate.

01:23:18 --> 01:23:20
And so, I assumed that he was on the left.

01:23:19 --> 01:23:22
'Cause again, I'd never heard of him.

01:23:21 --> 01:23:24
And so, I mischaracterized him,

01:23:23 --> 01:23:27
and I apologize later for mischaracterizing him.

01:23:26 --> 01:23:29
We finally go through the interview.

01:23:28 --> 01:23:32
It's 20 minutes, he just keeps going with the battle tweets.

01:23:31 --> 01:23:33
And finally, I got up and I took off the microphone.

01:23:32 --> 01:23:34
I walked out.

01:23:33 --> 01:23:36
And immediately, I knew it was a mistake.

01:23:35 --> 01:23:38
Like within 30 seconds of the end of the interview,

01:23:37 --> 01:23:40
I knew it was a mistake.

01:23:39 --> 01:23:43
And, that's why even before the interview came out,

01:23:42 --> 01:23:45
I believe I corrected the record that Andrew Neil

01:23:44 --> 01:23:46
is not on the left.

01:23:45 --> 01:23:47
That's a mistake by me.

01:23:47 --> 01:23:52
And then, took the hit for a bad interview.

01:23:51 --> 01:23:56
And so as far as, what I wish I had done differently,

01:23:55 --> 01:23:57
I wish I'd known who he was.

01:23:57 --> 01:23:59
I wish I'd done my research.

01:23:58 --> 01:24:01
I wish that I had treated it as though

01:24:00 --> 01:24:02
there was a possibility

01:24:01 --> 01:24:03
that it was gonna be more adversarial than it was.

01:24:02 --> 01:24:06
I think I was incautious about the interview

01:24:05 --> 01:24:07
because it was pitched as,

01:24:06 --> 01:24:08
it's just another book interview,

01:24:07 --> 01:24:10
and it wasn't just another book interview.

01:24:09 --> 01:24:12
It was treated much more adversarially than that.

01:24:11 --> 01:24:14
So I wish that, that's on me.

01:24:13 --> 01:24:16
I gotta research the people who are talking to me,

01:24:15 --> 01:24:19
and watch their shows, and learn about that.

01:24:18 --> 01:24:23
And then obviously, the kind of gut level appeal to ego

01:24:22 --> 01:24:25
r arrogance, that a bad look,

01:24:24 --> 01:24:26
and shouldn't have done that.

01:24:25 --> 01:24:28
And losing your cool is always a bad look.

01:24:28 --> 01:24:32
- The fact that that sort of became somewhat viral

01:24:31 --> 01:24:33
and stood out,

01:24:32 --> 01:24:36
just shows that it happens so rarely to you.

01:24:36 --> 01:24:40
So just to look at the day in the life of Ben Shapiro,

01:24:39 --> 01:24:45
you speak a lot, very eloquently, about difficult topics.

01:24:46 --> 01:24:49
What goes into the research, the mental,

01:24:48 --> 01:24:52
and you always look pretty like energetic,

01:24:51 --> 01:24:56
and like you're not exhausted by the burden,

01:24:55 --> 01:24:59
the heaviness of the topics you're covering day after day

01:24:58 --> 01:25:01
after day.

01:25:00 --> 01:25:06
What goes through the preparation mentally, diet-wise,

01:25:05 --> 01:25:07
anything like that?

01:25:06 --> 01:25:08
When do you wake up?

01:25:07 --> 01:25:10
- Okay, so I wake up when my kids wake me up,

01:25:09 --> 01:25:13
usually that's my baby daughter who's two and a half.

01:25:12 --> 01:25:14
She usually, we hear her on the monitor,

01:25:13 --> 01:25:17
usually about 6:15, 6:20AM.

01:25:16 --> 01:25:18
So I get up,

01:25:17 --> 01:25:19
my wife sleeps in a little bit.

01:25:18 --> 01:25:21
I go get the baby,

01:25:20 --> 01:25:22
and then my son gets up,

01:25:21 --> 01:25:24
and then my oldest daughter gets up.

01:25:23 --> 01:25:25
I have eight, six, and two.

01:25:24 --> 01:25:27
The boy's the middle child.

01:25:26 --> 01:25:29
- Is that both a source of stress and happiness?

01:25:28 --> 01:25:31
- Oh yeah, it's the height of both, right.

01:25:30 --> 01:25:33
I mean, it's the source of the greatest happiness.

01:25:32 --> 01:25:35
So the way that I characterize it is this,

01:25:34 --> 01:25:37
when it comes to sort of kids and life.

01:25:36 --> 01:25:38
When you're single,

01:25:37 --> 01:25:40
your boundaries of happiness and unhappiness,

01:25:39 --> 01:25:41
you can be a zero in terms of happiness,

01:25:40 --> 01:25:43
you can be like a 10 in terms of happiness.

01:25:42 --> 01:25:44
Then, you get married and it goes up to like a 20

01:25:43 --> 01:25:45
and a negative 20;

01:25:44 --> 01:25:47
because your happiest stuff is with your wife.

01:25:46 --> 01:25:48
And then, the most unhappy stuff

01:25:47 --> 01:25:49
is when something happens to your spouse,

01:25:48 --> 01:25:51
it's the worst thing in the entire world.

01:25:50 --> 01:25:52
Then, you have kids and all limits are removed.

01:25:51 --> 01:25:54
So the best things that have ever happened to me

01:25:53 --> 01:25:56
are things where I'm watching my kids

01:25:55 --> 01:25:57
and they're playing together

01:25:56 --> 01:25:58
and they're being wonderful and sweet and cute,

01:25:57 --> 01:25:59
and I love them so much.

01:25:58 --> 01:26:02
And, the worst things are when my son is screaming at me

01:26:01 --> 01:26:04
for no reason because he's being insane,

01:26:03 --> 01:26:06
and I have to deal with that.

01:26:05 --> 01:26:08
Or something bad happens to my daughter at school

01:26:07 --> 01:26:09
or something like that.

01:26:08 --> 01:26:10
That stuff is really it.

01:26:09 --> 01:26:11
So yes, the source of my greatest happiness,

01:26:10 --> 01:26:12
the source of my greatest stress.

01:26:11 --> 01:26:14
They get me up at about 6:15 in the morning.

01:26:13 --> 01:26:15
I feed them breakfast.

01:26:14 --> 01:26:16
I'm kind of scrolling the news;

01:26:15 --> 01:26:17
while I'm making the eggs.

01:26:19 --> 01:26:21
Just updating myself on anything

01:26:20 --> 01:26:23
that may have happened overnight.

01:26:22 --> 01:26:25
I go into the office,

01:26:24 --> 01:26:28
put on the makeup and the wardrobe or whatever,

01:26:27 --> 01:26:30
and then I sit down and do the show.

01:26:29 --> 01:26:32
A lot of the prep is actually done the night before

01:26:31 --> 01:26:34
because the news cycle doesn't change all that much

01:26:33 --> 01:26:36
between kind of late at night,

01:26:35 --> 01:26:37
and in the morning.

01:26:36 --> 01:26:38
So, I can supplement in the morning.

01:26:37 --> 01:26:40
So, I do the show.

01:26:39 --> 01:26:41
- So a lot of the preparation,

01:26:40 --> 01:26:44
like thinking through what are the big issues in the world

01:26:43 --> 01:26:45
is done the night before?

01:26:44 --> 01:26:48
- Yeah, and that's reading pretty much all the legacy media.

01:26:47 --> 01:26:49
I rip on legacy media a lot,

01:26:48 --> 01:26:51
but that's because a lot of what they do is really good

01:26:50 --> 01:26:52
and a lot of what they do is really bad.

01:26:51 --> 01:26:54
I cover a lot of legacy media.

01:26:53 --> 01:26:56
So that's probably covering, Wall Street Journal,

01:26:55 --> 01:26:59
New York Times, Washington Post, Boston Globe, Daily Mail.

01:26:58 --> 01:27:01
And then, I'll look over at some of the alternative media.

01:27:00 --> 01:27:03
I'll look at my own website, Daily Wire,

01:27:02 --> 01:27:03
I'll look at Breitbart.

01:27:02 --> 01:27:04
I'll look at The Blaze.

01:27:03 --> 01:27:07
I'll look at maybe The Intercept,

01:27:06 --> 01:27:09
I'll look at a bunch of different sources

01:27:08 --> 01:27:12
and then I will look at different clips online.

01:27:11 --> 01:27:14
So media comes in handy here,

01:27:13 --> 01:27:17
(indistinct) comes in handy here, that sort of stuff.

01:27:16 --> 01:27:18
'Cause my show relies very heavily

01:27:17 --> 01:27:19
on being able to play people,

01:27:18 --> 01:27:21
so you can hear them in their own words.

01:27:20 --> 01:27:23
And, that's sort of the media diet.

01:27:22 --> 01:27:25
So I sit down,

01:27:25 --> 01:27:28
I do the show and then once I'm done with the show,

01:27:27 --> 01:27:31
I usually have between,

01:27:30 --> 01:27:33
now it's like 11:15 in the morning maybe,

01:27:32 --> 01:27:35
'cause sometimes I'll prerecord the show.

01:27:34 --> 01:27:37
It's 11:15 in the morning,

01:27:36 --> 01:27:39
I'll go home and if my wife's available,

01:27:38 --> 01:27:41
I'll grab lunch with her.

01:27:40 --> 01:27:43
If not, then I will go and work out.

01:27:42 --> 01:27:47
I try to work out five times a week with a trainer,

01:27:46 --> 01:27:48
something like that.

01:27:47 --> 01:27:50
And then, I will-

01:27:49 --> 01:27:51
Just regular gym stuff?

01:27:50 --> 01:27:53
Just going to the gym?

01:27:52 --> 01:27:55
- Yeah, weights and plyometrics,

01:27:54 --> 01:27:58
and some CrossFit kind of stuff.

01:27:59 --> 01:28:04
Beneath this mild astute lies a hulking monster, yeah.

01:28:03 --> 01:28:07
And, so I'll do that.

01:28:06 --> 01:28:11
Then, I will do reading and writing.

01:28:12 --> 01:28:14
I'm usually working on a book at any given time.

01:28:13 --> 01:28:17
- Do you shut off the rest of the world?

01:28:16 --> 01:28:19
- Yes, I put some music in my ears.

01:28:18 --> 01:28:20
Usually Brahms or Bach,

01:28:19 --> 01:28:22
sometimes Beethoven or Mozart.

01:28:21 --> 01:28:24
Those four it's usually, those are on rotation.

01:28:23 --> 01:28:24
- No rap?

01:28:23 --> 01:28:26
- No rap, no rap.

01:28:25 --> 01:28:28
Despite my extraordinary rendition of WAP.

01:28:27 --> 01:28:30
I'm not in fact a rap fan.

01:28:29 --> 01:28:33
- Do you still hate WAP, the song?

01:28:32 --> 01:28:34
- I will say,

01:28:33 --> 01:28:36
I do not think that it is the peak of western civilized art.

01:28:35 --> 01:28:37
- [Lex] Okay.

01:28:36 --> 01:28:38
- I don't think that a hundred years from now,

01:28:37 --> 01:28:40
people will be gluing their faces to a WAP

01:28:39 --> 01:28:42
and protest at the environment.

01:28:41 --> 01:28:45
- But, Brahms and the rest will be still around?

01:28:44 --> 01:28:46
- Yes, I would assume.

01:28:45 --> 01:28:48
If people still have a functioning prefrontal

01:28:47 --> 01:28:49
cortex in any sort of taste.

01:28:48 --> 01:28:51
- Strong words from Ben Shapiro.

01:28:50 --> 01:28:54
All right, so you got some classical music in your ears

01:28:53 --> 01:28:55
and you're focusing.

01:28:54 --> 01:28:57
Are you at the computer when you're writing?

01:28:56 --> 01:29:00
- Yeah, I'm at the computer, usually.

01:28:59 --> 01:29:02
We have a kind of a room that has some sun coming in,

01:29:01 --> 01:29:03
so it's nice in there.

01:29:02 --> 01:29:06
Or I'll go up to a library that we just completed for me.

01:29:05 --> 01:29:07
I'll go up there and I'll write and read.

01:29:06 --> 01:29:09
- Like with physical books?

01:29:08 --> 01:29:11
- Yeah, I love physical books

01:29:10 --> 01:29:12
and because I keep Sabbath.

01:29:11 --> 01:29:14
I don't use Kindle.

01:29:13 --> 01:29:16
Because when I'm reading a book,

01:29:15 --> 01:29:17
and I hit Sabbath,

01:29:16 --> 01:29:17
I have to turn off the Kindle.

01:29:16 --> 01:29:20
So that means that I have tons and tons and tons

01:29:19 --> 01:29:21
of physical books.

01:29:20 --> 01:29:22
When we moved from Los Angeles to Florida,

01:29:21 --> 01:29:24
I had about 7,000 volumes.

01:29:23 --> 01:29:27
I had to discard probably 4,000 of them.

01:29:26 --> 01:29:28
And then, I've built that back up now.

01:29:27 --> 01:29:30
I'm probably gonna have to go through another round,

01:29:29 --> 01:29:31
where I put them somewhere else.

01:29:30 --> 01:29:33
I tend to tab books rather than highlighting them

01:29:32 --> 01:29:35
because I can't highlight on Sabbath.

01:29:34 --> 01:29:38
So I have the little stickers and I put them in the book.

01:29:37 --> 01:29:40
A typical book from me, you can see it on the book club,

01:29:39 --> 01:29:42
will be filled with tabs on the side.

01:29:41 --> 01:29:43
Things that I wanna take notes, I actually,

01:29:42 --> 01:29:48
I've gotten a person who I pay to go through

01:29:47 --> 01:29:51
and write down in files the quotes

01:29:50 --> 01:29:52
that I like from the book.

01:29:51 --> 01:29:53
So I have those handy.

01:29:53 --> 01:29:55
Which is a good way for me to remember

01:29:54 --> 01:29:57
what it is that I've read.

01:29:56 --> 01:30:00
'Cause I read probably somewhere

01:29:59 --> 01:30:02
between three and five books a week.

01:30:01 --> 01:30:05
And then, in a good week five.

01:30:04 --> 01:30:08
And then, I write, I read.

01:30:07 --> 01:30:11
And then, I go pick up my kids from school at 3:30.

01:30:10 --> 01:30:13
According to my kids, I have no job.

01:30:12 --> 01:30:15
I'm there in the mornings until they leave for school.

01:30:14 --> 01:30:16
I pick them up from school,

01:30:15 --> 01:30:18
I hang out with them until they go to bed;
