01:30:17 --> 01:30:20
which is usually 7:30 or so.

01:30:19 --> 01:30:21
I'm helping them with their homework,

01:30:20 --> 01:30:22
and I'm playing with them.

01:30:21 --> 01:30:26
And, I'm taking them on rides in the brand new Tesla;

01:30:25 --> 01:30:28
which my son is obsessed with.

01:30:27 --> 01:30:30
And then, I put them to bed and then I sit back down,

01:30:29 --> 01:30:31
I prep for the next day.

01:30:30 --> 01:30:32
Go through all those media sources

01:30:31 --> 01:30:33
I was talking about,

01:30:32 --> 01:30:34
compile kind of a schedule

01:30:33 --> 01:30:35
for what I want the show to look like,

01:30:34 --> 01:30:37
and run a show.

01:30:36 --> 01:30:38
It's very detail oriented.

01:30:37 --> 01:30:39
Nobody writes anything for me.

01:30:38 --> 01:30:41
I write all my own stuff.

01:30:40 --> 01:30:43
So every word that comes outta my mouth, is my fault.

01:30:42 --> 01:30:46
And then, hopefully I have a couple hours

01:30:45 --> 01:30:49
or an hour to hang out with my wife before we go to bed.

01:30:48 --> 01:30:50
- The words you write,

01:30:49 --> 01:30:54
do you edit a lot or does it just come out, you're thinking?

01:30:53 --> 01:30:55
What are the key ideas I want to express?

01:30:54 --> 01:30:57
- No, I don't tend to edit a lot,

01:30:57 --> 01:31:00
I thank God I'm able to write extraordinarily quickly.

01:30:59 --> 01:31:02
I write very, very fast.

01:31:01 --> 01:31:03
In fact, in a previous life I was.

01:31:02 --> 01:31:05
- You also speak fast, so similarly.

01:31:04 --> 01:31:06
- Yeah, exactly.

01:31:05 --> 01:31:07
And I speak in paragraphs,

01:31:06 --> 01:31:09
it's exactly the same thing.

01:31:08 --> 01:31:11
In a previous life, I was a ghost writer.

01:31:10 --> 01:31:13
So, I used to be sort of known as a turnaround specialist

01:31:12 --> 01:31:14
in the publishing industry.

01:31:13 --> 01:31:17
There'd be somebody who came to the publisher and says,

01:31:16 --> 01:31:19
"I have three weeks to get this book done.

01:31:18 --> 01:31:20
"I don't have a word done."

01:31:19 --> 01:31:22
And they would call me up and be like,

01:31:21 --> 01:31:24
"This person needs a book written."

01:31:23 --> 01:31:26
And so, in three weeks, I'd knock out 60,000 words or so.

01:31:25 --> 01:31:28
- Is there something you can say to the process

01:31:27 --> 01:31:31
that you follow to think?

01:31:30 --> 01:31:33
How you think about ideas,

01:31:32 --> 01:31:37
stuff is going on in the world and trying to understand

01:31:36 --> 01:31:37
what is happening.

01:31:36 --> 01:31:39
What are the explanations?

01:31:38 --> 01:31:40
What are the forces behind this?

01:31:39 --> 01:31:44
Do you have a process or just you wait for the news

01:31:43 --> 01:31:45
to give you the interpretation?

01:31:44 --> 01:31:48
- Well, I don't think it's a formal process,

01:31:47 --> 01:31:50
but because I read...

01:31:50 --> 01:31:52
There's two ways to do it.

01:31:51 --> 01:31:57
One is sometimes, the daily grind of the news

01:31:56 --> 01:32:00
is going to refer back to core principles

01:31:59 --> 01:32:01
that are broader and deeper.

01:32:01 --> 01:32:04
I thank God because I've read so much

01:32:03 --> 01:32:06
on so many different things

01:32:05 --> 01:32:08
of a lot of different point of views.

01:32:07 --> 01:32:10
Then, if something breaks, and a piece of news breaks,

01:32:09 --> 01:32:12
I can immediately sort of channel that into,

01:32:11 --> 01:32:14
in the mental Rolodex.

01:32:13 --> 01:32:18
These three big ideas that I think are really important.

01:32:17 --> 01:32:19
And then, I can talk at length

01:32:18 --> 01:32:20
about what those ideas are

01:32:19 --> 01:32:21
and I can explicate those.

01:32:20 --> 01:32:23
And so, for example,

01:32:22 --> 01:32:26
when we were talking about Musk taking over Twitter

01:32:25 --> 01:32:28
before and I immediately go to the history of media.

01:32:27 --> 01:32:31
That's me tying it into a broader theme.

01:32:30 --> 01:32:32
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:32:31 --> 01:32:35
- And, I do that, I would say fairly frequently.

01:32:34 --> 01:32:40
We're talking about, say subsidization of industry,

01:32:39 --> 01:32:41
and I can immediately tie that into,

01:32:40 --> 01:32:42
okay, what's the history of subsidization

01:32:41 --> 01:32:43
in the United States,

01:32:42 --> 01:32:45
going all the way back to Woodrow Wilson

01:32:44 --> 01:32:47
and forward through FDR's industrial policy.

01:32:46 --> 01:32:48
And, how does that tie into

01:32:47 --> 01:32:51
sort of broader economic policy internationally?

01:32:50 --> 01:32:53
It allows me to tie into bigger themes

01:32:52 --> 01:32:55
because what I tend to read is mostly not news.

01:32:54 --> 01:32:57
What I tend to read is mostly books.

01:32:56 --> 01:32:58
I would say most of my media diet

01:32:57 --> 01:32:59
is actually not the stuff.

01:32:58 --> 01:33:01
Like, that's the icing on the cake.

01:33:00 --> 01:33:05
But, the actual cake is the hundreds of pages in history,

01:33:04 --> 01:33:10
econ, geography, social science,

01:33:09 --> 01:33:12
that I'm reading every week.

01:33:11 --> 01:33:14
And so, that sort of stuff allows me

01:33:13 --> 01:33:16
to think more deeply about these things.

01:33:15 --> 01:33:17
So that's one way of doing it.

01:33:16 --> 01:33:19
The other way of doing it is, Russia breaks in the news.

01:33:18 --> 01:33:20
I don't know anything about Russia.

01:33:19 --> 01:33:22
I immediately go and I purchase five books about Russia,

01:33:21 --> 01:33:23
and I read all of them.

01:33:27 --> 01:33:29
The fortunate thing for me,

01:33:28 --> 01:33:30
and the unfortunate thing about the world

01:33:29 --> 01:33:33
is you read two books on a subject.

01:33:33 --> 01:33:35
You are now considered by the media,

01:33:34 --> 01:33:36
an expert on the subject.

01:33:36 --> 01:33:38
That's sad and shallow.

01:33:37 --> 01:33:40
But, that is the way that it is.

01:33:39 --> 01:33:41
The good news for me is that my job

01:33:40 --> 01:33:43
isn't to be a full expert on any of these subjects.

01:33:42 --> 01:33:44
I don't claim to be.

01:33:43 --> 01:33:45
I'm not a Russia expert.

01:33:44 --> 01:33:48
I know enough on Russia to be able to understand

01:33:47 --> 01:33:49
when people talk about Russia,

01:33:48 --> 01:33:51
what the system looks like, how it works, and all of that.

01:33:50 --> 01:33:53
And then, to explicate that for the common man;

01:33:52 --> 01:33:56
which a lot of people who are infused

01:33:55 --> 01:33:57
with the expertise can't really do.

01:33:56 --> 01:33:58
If you're so deep in the weeds,

01:33:57 --> 01:34:00
that you're like a full on academic expert on a thing;

01:33:59 --> 01:34:01
sometimes it's hard to translate that over

01:34:00 --> 01:34:03
to a massive audience; which is really my job.

01:34:02 --> 01:34:05
- Well, I think it can actually,

01:34:04 --> 01:34:06
it's funny with the two books,

01:34:05 --> 01:34:09
you can actually get a pretty deep understanding

01:34:08 --> 01:34:11
if you read and also think deeply about it.

01:34:10 --> 01:34:14
It allows you to approach a thing from first principles.

01:34:13 --> 01:34:17
A lot of times if you're a quote unquote expert,

01:34:16 --> 01:34:22
you get carried away by the momentum of what the field

01:34:22 --> 01:34:26
has been thinking about versus like stepping back,

01:34:25 --> 01:34:28
"All right, what is really going on?"

01:34:27 --> 01:34:30
The challenge is to pick the right two books.

01:34:29 --> 01:34:32
- Right, usually what I'll try to find

01:34:31 --> 01:34:33
is somebody who knows the topic pretty well

01:34:32 --> 01:34:34
and have them recommend,

01:34:33 --> 01:34:36
or a couple people, and have them recommend books.

01:34:35 --> 01:34:38
So a couple years ago I knew nothing about Bitcoin.

01:34:37 --> 01:34:41
I was at a conference and a couple of people

01:34:40 --> 01:34:43
who you've had on your show, actually,

01:34:42 --> 01:34:44
were there and I asked them,

01:34:43 --> 01:34:46
"Gimme your top three books on Bitcoin."

01:34:45 --> 01:34:50
And so then, I went and I read like nine books on Bitcoin.

01:34:49 --> 01:34:51
And so, if you're nine books on Bitcoin,

01:34:50 --> 01:34:52
you at least know enough to get by.

01:34:51 --> 01:34:53
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:34:52 --> 01:34:55
- And so, I can actually explain what Bitcoin is

01:34:54 --> 01:34:56
and why it works,

01:34:55 --> 01:34:58
or why it doesn't work in some cases,

01:34:57 --> 01:35:00
and what's happening in the markets that way.

01:34:59 --> 01:35:03
So that's very, very helpful.

01:35:02 --> 01:35:05
- Well, Putin as an example.

01:35:04 --> 01:35:08
That's a difficult one to find the right books on.

01:35:07 --> 01:35:11
I think The New Czar is the one I read,

01:35:10 --> 01:35:12
where it was the most objective.

01:35:11 --> 01:35:13
- One I read, I think, about Putin was,

01:35:12 --> 01:35:16
it was one called Strong Man.

01:35:15 --> 01:35:18
And, it was very highly critical of Putin,

01:35:17 --> 01:35:21
but it gave a good background on him at the very least.

01:35:20 --> 01:35:23
- Yeah, so I'm very skeptical sort of things

01:35:22 --> 01:35:26
that are very critical of Putin

01:35:25 --> 01:35:29
because it feels like there's activism injected

01:35:28 --> 01:35:30
into the history.

01:35:29 --> 01:35:32
Like, the way The Rise and Fall of the Third Reich

01:35:31 --> 01:35:33
is written about Hitler,

01:35:32 --> 01:35:37
I like because there's almost not a criticism of Hitler.

01:35:36 --> 01:35:40
It's a description of Hitler, which is very,

01:35:40 --> 01:35:43
it's easier to do about a historical figure;

01:35:42 --> 01:35:45
which with William Shirer

01:35:44 --> 01:35:46
with The Rise and Fall of the Third Reich,

01:35:45 --> 01:35:49
it's impressive because he lived through it.

01:35:48 --> 01:35:52
But, it's very tough to find objective descriptions

01:35:51 --> 01:35:55
about the history of the man and a country of Putin,

01:35:54 --> 01:35:58
of Zelensky, of any difficult,

01:35:57 --> 01:35:59
Trump was the same.

01:35:58 --> 01:36:01
And, I feel like-

01:36:00 --> 01:36:02
That's the hero villain archetype, right?

01:36:01 --> 01:36:03
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:36:02 --> 01:36:04
- It's like, either somebody's completely a hero

01:36:03 --> 01:36:06
r completely a villain.

01:36:05 --> 01:36:10
And, the truth is pretty much no one is completely a hero

01:36:09 --> 01:36:11
r completely a villain.

01:36:10 --> 01:36:12
People, in fact,

01:36:11 --> 01:36:13
I'm not sure that I love descriptions of people

01:36:12 --> 01:36:15
as heroes or villains, generally.

01:36:14 --> 01:36:17
I think that people tend to do heroic things

01:36:16 --> 01:36:18
or do villainous things.

01:36:17 --> 01:36:18
In the same way that I'm not sure

01:36:17 --> 01:36:20
I love descriptions of people as a genius.

01:36:19 --> 01:36:21
My dad used to say this when I was growing up,

01:36:20 --> 01:36:24
he used to say he didn't believe that there were geniuses.

01:36:23 --> 01:36:25
He said he believed that there were people with a genius

01:36:24 --> 01:36:28
for something because people, yes,

01:36:27 --> 01:36:29
there are people who have very high IQ

01:36:28 --> 01:36:30
and we call them geniuses,

01:36:29 --> 01:36:33
but does that mean that they're good at EQ stuff?

01:36:32 --> 01:36:34
Not necessarily,

01:36:33 --> 01:36:35
but there are people who are geniuses at EQ stuff.

01:36:34 --> 01:36:36
In other words,

01:36:35 --> 01:36:39
it would be more specific to say that somebody's a genius

01:36:38 --> 01:36:41
at engineering than to say just broad spectrum,

01:36:40 --> 01:36:42
they're a genius.

01:36:41 --> 01:36:43
And that, does avoid the problem of thinking

01:36:42 --> 01:36:45
that they're good at something that they're not good at.

01:36:44 --> 01:36:47
It's a little more specific.

01:36:46 --> 01:36:48
- So because you read a lot of books,

01:36:48 --> 01:36:51
can you look back, and it's always a tough question,

01:36:50 --> 01:36:53
'cause so many, it's like your favorite song,

01:36:52 --> 01:36:56
but are there books that have been influential in your life

01:36:55 --> 01:36:57
that are impacting your thinking?

01:36:56 --> 01:36:59
Or maybe ones you go back to that

01:37:00 --> 01:37:03
still carry insight for you?

01:37:02 --> 01:37:04
- The Federalist Paper is a big one

01:37:03 --> 01:37:07
in terms of sort of how American politics works.

01:37:07 --> 01:37:10
The first econ book that I thought was really great

01:37:09 --> 01:37:11
'cause it was written for teenagers,

01:37:10 --> 01:37:12
essentially, is one called

01:37:11 --> 01:37:14
Economics in One Lesson by Henry Hazlitt.

01:37:13 --> 01:37:15
It's like 150 pages.

01:37:14 --> 01:37:18
I recommend it to everybody sort of 15 and up.

01:37:17 --> 01:37:20
It's easier than say Thomas Sowell's Basic Econ;

01:37:19 --> 01:37:21
which is four or 500 pages.

01:37:20 --> 01:37:23
- And it's looking what, at like macroeconomics,

01:37:22 --> 01:37:25
microeconomics, that kind of stuff?

01:37:24 --> 01:37:26
- Macro, macro.

01:37:25 --> 01:37:27
And, then,

01:37:28 --> 01:37:30
there's a great book

01:37:29 --> 01:37:32
by Carl Trueman called Rise and Triumph for the Modern Self,

01:37:31 --> 01:37:35
which I think is the best book in the last 10 years.

01:37:34 --> 01:37:37
That's been sort of impactful on some of the thoughts

01:37:36 --> 01:37:38
I've been having lately.

01:37:37 --> 01:37:39
- What's the key idea in there?

01:37:38 --> 01:37:41
- The key idea is that we've shifted the nature

01:37:40 --> 01:37:43
of how identity is done in the West,

01:37:42 --> 01:37:45
from how it was historically done.

01:37:44 --> 01:37:47
That basically, for nearly all of human history,

01:37:46 --> 01:37:51
the way that we identify as human beings is as a mix

01:37:50 --> 01:37:52
of our biological drives,

01:37:51 --> 01:37:53
and then how that interacts

01:37:52 --> 01:37:55
with the social institutions around us.

01:37:54 --> 01:37:56
And so, when you're a child,

01:37:55 --> 01:37:58
you're a bunch of unfettered biological drives,

01:37:57 --> 01:38:00
and it's your parents' job to civilize you.

01:37:59 --> 01:38:01
And, civilize you

01:38:00 --> 01:38:03
literally means bring you into civilization, right.

01:38:02 --> 01:38:04
You learn the rules of the road,

01:38:03 --> 01:38:06
you learn how to integrate into institutions

01:38:05 --> 01:38:08
that already exist and are designed to shape you.

01:38:07 --> 01:38:10
And it's how you interact with those institutions

01:38:09 --> 01:38:11
that makes you, you.

01:38:10 --> 01:38:13
It's not just a set of biological drives.

01:38:12 --> 01:38:15
And then, in the modern world,

01:38:14 --> 01:38:18
we've really driven toward the idea that what we are

01:38:17 --> 01:38:19
is how we feel on the inside

01:38:18 --> 01:38:20
without reference to the outside world.

01:38:19 --> 01:38:22
And it's the job of the outside world

01:38:21 --> 01:38:23
to celebrate and reflect

01:38:22 --> 01:38:25
what we think about ourselves on the inside.

01:38:24 --> 01:38:28
And so, what that means is that we are driven now

01:38:27 --> 01:38:30
toward fighting institutions

01:38:29 --> 01:38:32
'cause institutions are in positions.

01:38:31 --> 01:38:34
So everything around us, societal institutions,

01:38:33 --> 01:38:35
these are things that are crimping our style.

01:38:34 --> 01:38:37
They're making us not feel the way that we wanna feel.

01:38:36 --> 01:38:39
And, if we just destroy those things,

01:38:38 --> 01:38:41
then we'll be freer and more liberated.

01:38:40 --> 01:38:43
It's, I think, much deeper model

01:38:42 --> 01:38:46
of how to think about why our social politics

01:38:45 --> 01:38:48
particular are moving in a particular direction.

01:38:47 --> 01:38:49
Is that a ground shift has happened

01:38:48 --> 01:38:51
in how people think about themselves.

01:38:50 --> 01:38:56
And, this has had some somewhat kind of shocking effect

01:38:55 --> 01:38:57
in terms of social politics.

01:38:56 --> 01:39:00
- So there's negative consequences in your view of that.

01:38:59 --> 01:39:04
But, is there also a positive consequence of more power,

01:39:03 --> 01:39:06
more agency to the individual?

01:39:05 --> 01:39:07
- I think that you can make the argument

01:39:06 --> 01:39:09
hat institutions were weighing too heavily

01:39:08 --> 01:39:10
in how people form their identity.

01:39:09 --> 01:39:12
But, I think that what we've done

01:39:11 --> 01:39:14
is gone significantly too far on the other side.

01:39:13 --> 01:39:16
We basically decided to blow up the institutions

01:39:15 --> 01:39:21
in favor of unfettered feeling slash identity.

01:39:20 --> 01:39:23
And I think that that is not only a large mistake,

01:39:22 --> 01:39:25
I think it's gonna have dire ramifications for everything

01:39:24 --> 01:39:29
from suicidal ideation to institutional longevity

01:39:28 --> 01:39:32
in politics and in society more broadly.

01:39:31 --> 01:39:34
- So speaking about the nature of self,

01:39:33 --> 01:39:37
you've been an outspoken proponent of pro-life.

01:39:38 --> 01:39:43
Can we start by you trying to steel man,

01:39:42 --> 01:39:45
the case for pro-choice?

01:39:44 --> 01:39:47
That abortion is not murder,

01:39:46 --> 01:39:49
and a woman's right to choose

01:39:48 --> 01:39:52
is a fundamental human right, freedom.

01:39:51 --> 01:39:55
- So I think that the only way to steel man,

01:39:54 --> 01:40:00
the pro-choice case is to, and be ideologically consistent,

01:39:59 --> 01:40:03
is to suggest that there is no interest in the life

01:40:02 --> 01:40:05
of the unborn,

01:40:05 --> 01:40:10
that counter weighs at all, freedom of choice.

01:40:09 --> 01:40:14
So, what that means is we can take the full example,

01:40:13 --> 01:40:16
we can sort of the partial example.

01:40:15 --> 01:40:17
If we take the full example.

01:40:16 --> 01:40:19
What that would mean is that up until point of birth;

01:40:18 --> 01:40:21
which is sort of the democratic party platform position,

01:40:22 --> 01:40:25
that a woman's right to choose ought to extend

01:40:24 --> 01:40:27
for any reason whatsoever up to point of birth.

01:40:26 --> 01:40:28
The only way to argue that is that bodily autonomy

01:40:27 --> 01:40:29
is the only factor.

01:40:28 --> 01:40:31
There is no countervailing factor

01:40:30 --> 01:40:33
that would ever outweigh bodily autonomy.

01:40:32 --> 01:40:36
That would be the strongest version of the argument.

01:40:35 --> 01:40:39
Another version of that argument would be that the reason

01:40:38 --> 01:40:41
that bodily autonomy ought to weigh so heavily

01:40:40 --> 01:40:44
is because women can't be

01:40:43 --> 01:40:46
the equals of men,

01:40:45 --> 01:40:48
if the vicissitudes of biology are allowed

01:40:47 --> 01:40:50
to decide their futures.

01:40:49 --> 01:40:53
If pregnancy changes women

01:40:52 --> 01:40:53
in a way that it doesn't change men,

01:40:52 --> 01:40:55
it's a form of sex discrimination for women

01:40:54 --> 01:40:57
to ever have to go through with pregnancy;

01:40:56 --> 01:40:58
which is an argument

01:40:57 --> 01:41:00
hat was made by Ruth Bader Ginsburg, kind of.

01:40:59 --> 01:41:01
Those are the arguments.

01:41:00 --> 01:41:05
The kind of softer version is the more,

01:41:04 --> 01:41:08
I would say emotionally resonant version of the argument;

01:41:07 --> 01:41:11
which is that bodily autonomy ought to outweigh

01:41:10 --> 01:41:15
the interests of the fetus up till point x.

01:41:14 --> 01:41:15
And then, people have different feelings

01:41:15 --> 01:41:16
about what point X looks like.

01:41:15 --> 01:41:18
Is it up to the point of viability?

01:41:17 --> 01:41:19
Is it up to the point of the heartbeat?

01:41:18 --> 01:41:20
Is it up to 12 weeks or 15 weeks?

01:41:19 --> 01:41:22
And, that really is where the American public is.

01:41:21 --> 01:41:24
Where the American public is broadly speaking,

01:41:23 --> 01:41:25
not state by state,

01:41:24 --> 01:41:27
where there are various, really varied opinions.

01:41:26 --> 01:41:29
But, like broadly speaking,

01:41:28 --> 01:41:30
it seems like the American public by polling data

01:41:29 --> 01:41:31
wants somewhere between

01:41:30 --> 01:41:34
a 12 and 15 week abortion restriction.

01:41:33 --> 01:41:36
And, they believe that up until 12 or 15 weeks,

01:41:35 --> 01:41:39
there's not enough there for, to not be specific,

01:41:38 --> 01:41:41
but to be kind of how people feel about it,

01:41:40 --> 01:41:43
to outweigh a woman's bodily autonomy.

01:41:42 --> 01:41:45
And then, beyond that point,

01:41:44 --> 01:41:47
then there's enough of an interest in the life

01:41:46 --> 01:41:50
of the pre-born child, it's developed enough.

01:41:49 --> 01:41:51
Then now, we care about it enough

01:41:50 --> 01:41:53
that it outweighs a woman's bodily autonomy.

01:41:52 --> 01:41:58
- What's the strongest case for pro-life in your mind?

01:41:57 --> 01:41:59
- I mean, the strongest case for pro-life

01:41:58 --> 01:42:01
is that from conception,

01:42:00 --> 01:42:03
a human life has been created.

01:42:02 --> 01:42:05
It is a human life with potential.

01:42:04 --> 01:42:07
That human life potential with potential,

01:42:06 --> 01:42:10
now has an independent interest in its own existence.

01:42:09 --> 01:42:13
- If I may just ask a quick question.

01:42:12 --> 01:42:16
So conception is when a sperm fertilizes an egg?

01:42:15 --> 01:42:18
- Yes.

01:42:17 --> 01:42:20
- Okay, just to to clarify the biological beginning

01:42:19 --> 01:42:21
of what conception means.

01:42:20 --> 01:42:24
- I mean, because that is the beginning of human life.

01:42:23 --> 01:42:25
Now, there are other standards

01:42:24 --> 01:42:26
that people have drawn.

01:42:25 --> 01:42:29
Some people say implantation in the uterus,

01:42:28 --> 01:42:32
some people will suggest viability,

01:42:31 --> 01:42:33
some brain development or heart development.

01:42:33 --> 01:42:35
But, the clear dividing line

01:42:34 --> 01:42:37
between a human life exists

01:42:36 --> 01:42:38
and a human life does not exist,

01:42:37 --> 01:42:40
is the biological creation of an independent human life

01:42:39 --> 01:42:42
with own DNA strands and et cetera;

01:42:41 --> 01:42:44
which happens at conception.

01:42:43 --> 01:42:45
Once you acknowledge that

01:42:44 --> 01:42:49
here is that independent human life with potential,

01:42:48 --> 01:42:50
and I keep calling it that

01:42:49 --> 01:42:51
because people sometimes say potential human life,

01:42:50 --> 01:42:52
it's not a potential human life.

01:42:51 --> 01:42:54
It's a human life that is not developed yet

01:42:53 --> 01:42:56
o the full extent that it will develop.

01:42:55 --> 01:42:57
Once you say that,

01:42:56 --> 01:42:59
and once you say that it has its own interest.

01:42:58 --> 01:43:02
Now, the burden of proof is to explain

01:43:01 --> 01:43:05
why bodily autonomy ought to allow

01:43:04 --> 01:43:07
for the snuffing out of that human life.

01:43:06 --> 01:43:09
If we believe that human life ought not to be killed

01:43:08 --> 01:43:12
for quote unquote, no good reason,

01:43:11 --> 01:43:14
you have to come up with a good reason.

01:43:13 --> 01:43:15
The burden of proof is now shifted.

01:43:14 --> 01:43:17
Now, we'll find people who will say,

01:43:16 --> 01:43:19
"Well, the good reason is that

01:43:18 --> 01:43:20
"It's not sufficiently developed to outweigh

01:43:19 --> 01:43:22
"The mental trauma or emotional trauma

01:43:21 --> 01:43:23
"That a woman goes through.

01:43:22 --> 01:43:26
"If, for example, she was raped or the victim of incest."

01:43:25 --> 01:43:29
Okay, and that is a fairly emotionally resonant argument,

01:43:28 --> 01:43:31
but it's not necessarily dispositive.

01:43:30 --> 01:43:32
You can make the argument

01:43:31 --> 01:43:34
hat just because something horrific

01:43:33 --> 01:43:36
and horrible happened to a woman,

01:43:35 --> 01:43:41
does not rob the human life of its interest in life.

01:43:40 --> 01:43:43
One of the big problems in trying to draw any line

01:43:42 --> 01:43:46
for the self-interest of life,

01:43:45 --> 01:43:50
in the human life is that it's very difficult to draw

01:43:49 --> 01:43:53
any other line that doesn't seem somewhat arbitrary.

01:43:52 --> 01:43:54
You say that independent heartbeat,

01:43:55 --> 01:43:57
people have pacemakers.

01:43:56 --> 01:43:59
If you say brain function,

01:43:58 --> 01:44:01
people have various levels of brain function as adults.

01:44:00 --> 01:44:03
If you say viability,

01:44:02 --> 01:44:04
babies are not viable after they're born.

01:44:03 --> 01:44:06
If I left a newborn baby on a table,

01:44:05 --> 01:44:07
and did not take care of it,

01:44:06 --> 01:44:08
it would be dead in two days.

01:44:07 --> 01:44:11
So, once you start getting into sort of these lines,

01:44:10 --> 01:44:13
it starts to get very fuzzy very quickly.

01:44:12 --> 01:44:16
And so, if you're looking for sort of a bright line

01:44:15 --> 01:44:19
moral rule, that would be the bright line moral rule.

01:44:18 --> 01:44:20
And, that's sort of the pro-life case.

01:44:19 --> 01:44:22
- Well, there's still mysterious

01:44:21 --> 01:44:26
difficult scientific questions of things like consciousness.

01:44:25 --> 01:44:30
- So what does the question of consciousness,

01:44:30 --> 01:44:34
how does it come into play into this debate?

01:44:33 --> 01:44:36
- So I don't believe that consciousness

01:44:35 --> 01:44:38
is the sole criterion

01:44:37 --> 01:44:41
by which we judge the self-interest in human life.

01:44:40 --> 01:44:46
So we are unconscious a good deal of our lives, right?

01:44:45 --> 01:44:48
That does not, we will be conscious again.

01:44:47 --> 01:44:50
When you're unconscious, when you're asleep,

01:44:49 --> 01:44:53
for example, presumably your life is still worth living.

01:44:52 --> 01:44:54
If somebody came in and killed you,

01:44:53 --> 01:44:57
that'd be a serious moral quandary at the very least.

01:44:56 --> 01:44:59
- But the birth of consciousness,

01:44:58 --> 01:45:02
the lighting up of the flame,

01:45:01 --> 01:45:04
the initial lighting up of the flame,

01:45:03 --> 01:45:06
there does seem to be something special about that.

01:45:05 --> 01:45:09
And, it's a mystery of when that happens.

01:45:08 --> 01:45:11
- Well, I mean, Peter Singer makes the case

01:45:10 --> 01:45:12
that basically self-consciousness doesn't exist

01:45:11 --> 01:45:14
until you're two and a half.

01:45:13 --> 01:45:16
He says that even infanticide should be okay.

01:45:15 --> 01:45:19
He's the bioethicist over at Princeton.

01:45:18 --> 01:45:20
You get into some real dicey territory

01:45:19 --> 01:45:21
once you get into consciousness.

01:45:20 --> 01:45:24
Also, the truth is that consciousness is more of a spectrum

01:45:23 --> 01:45:27
than it is a dividing line.

01:45:26 --> 01:45:30
Meaning, that there are people with various degrees

01:45:29 --> 01:45:31
of brain function.

01:45:30 --> 01:45:33
We don't actually know how conscious they are.

01:45:32 --> 01:45:36
And you can get into eugenic territory pretty quickly,

01:45:35 --> 01:45:38
when we start dividing between lives that are worth living

01:45:37 --> 01:45:39
based on levels of consciousness,

01:45:38 --> 01:45:40
and lives that are not worth living

01:45:39 --> 01:45:42
based on levels of consciousness.

01:45:41 --> 01:45:44
- Do you find it,

01:45:43 --> 01:45:48
the aspect of women's freedom,

01:45:47 --> 01:45:51
do you feel the tension between that ability

01:45:50 --> 01:45:54
to choose the trajectory of your own life

01:45:53 --> 01:45:59
versus the rights of the unborn child?

01:45:59 --> 01:46:01
- In one situation, yes.

01:46:00 --> 01:46:02
In one situation, no.

01:46:01 --> 01:46:05
If you've had sex with a person voluntarily,

01:46:04 --> 01:46:09
and as a product of that, you are now pregnant, no.

01:46:08 --> 01:46:11
You've taken an action with a perfectly predictable result.

01:46:10 --> 01:46:12
Even if you took birth control.

01:46:11 --> 01:46:14
This is the way that human beings have procreated

01:46:13 --> 01:46:15
for literally all of human existence.

01:46:14 --> 01:46:16
And by the way,

01:46:15 --> 01:46:18
also how all mammals appropriate.

01:46:17 --> 01:46:20
So the idea that this was an entirely unforeseen consequence

01:46:19 --> 01:46:21
of your activity,

01:46:20 --> 01:46:25
I find, I have less sympathy for you

01:46:24 --> 01:46:26
in that particular situation

01:46:25 --> 01:46:28
because you could have made decisions

01:46:27 --> 01:46:30
that would not lead you to this particular impasse.

01:46:29 --> 01:46:32
In fact, this used to be the basis of marriage.

01:46:31 --> 01:46:36
Was when we were a, apparently more terrible society,

01:46:35 --> 01:46:37
we used to say that people should wait

01:46:36 --> 01:46:39
until they get married to have sex,

01:46:38 --> 01:46:41
a position that I still hold.

01:46:40 --> 01:46:42
And, the reason for that

01:46:41 --> 01:46:44
was because then if you have sex and you produce a child,

01:46:43 --> 01:46:45
then the child will grow up

01:46:44 --> 01:46:47
in a two parent family with stability.

01:46:46 --> 01:46:50
So, not a ton of sympathy there.

01:46:49 --> 01:46:51
When it comes to rape and incest,

01:46:50 --> 01:46:52
obviously heavy, heavy sympathy.

01:46:51 --> 01:46:55
And so, that's why I think you see, statistically speaking,

01:46:54 --> 01:46:56
a huge percentage of Americans,

01:46:55 --> 01:46:58
including many pro-life Americans,

01:46:57 --> 01:46:59
people who consider themselves pro-life,

01:46:58 --> 01:47:02
would consider exceptions for rape and incest.

01:47:01 --> 01:47:04
One of the sort of dishonest things that I think happens

01:47:03 --> 01:47:07
in abortion debates is arguing from the fringes.

01:47:06 --> 01:47:08
This tends to happen a lot,

01:47:07 --> 01:47:11
is pro-choice activists will argue from rape and incest

01:47:10 --> 01:47:14
o the other 99.8% of abortions.

01:47:13 --> 01:47:15
Or you'll see people on the pro-life side argue

01:47:14 --> 01:47:17
from partial birth abortion

01:47:16 --> 01:47:20
to all of abortion that you actually have to take

01:47:19 --> 01:47:21
on sort of the mainstream case,

01:47:20 --> 01:47:23
and then decide whether or not that's acceptable or not.

01:47:22 --> 01:47:24
- But to you, the exception,

01:47:23 --> 01:47:26
just ethically without generalizing it,

01:47:26 --> 01:47:30
that is a valid ethically exception.

01:47:29 --> 01:47:33
- I don't hold that there should be an exception for rape

01:47:32 --> 01:47:34
or incest because again,

01:47:33 --> 01:47:36
I hold by the bright line rule that once a human life

01:47:35 --> 01:47:37
with potential exists,

01:47:36 --> 01:47:40
then it has its own interest in life that cannot be curbed

01:47:39 --> 01:47:42
by your self-interest.

01:47:42 --> 01:47:45
The only exception that I hold by is,

01:47:44 --> 01:47:47
the same exception that literally all pro-lifers hold by;

01:47:46 --> 01:47:49
which is the life of the mother is put in danger.

01:47:48 --> 01:47:51
- Such a tough, tough topic.

01:47:50 --> 01:47:54
'Cause if you believe that that's the line,

01:47:53 --> 01:47:56
then we're committing mass murder.

01:47:55 --> 01:47:57
- Well, or at least mass killing.

01:47:56 --> 01:48:00
So I would say that murder typically requires a level

01:47:59 --> 01:48:03
of mens rea that may be absent

01:48:02 --> 01:48:04
and in many cases of abortion.

01:48:03 --> 01:48:06
Because the usual follow on question is,

01:48:05 --> 01:48:07
"Well if to murder, why don't prosecute the woman?"

01:48:06 --> 01:48:10
And the answer is because the vast majority of people

01:48:09 --> 01:48:12
who are having abortions, don't actually believe

01:48:11 --> 01:48:13
that they're killing a person.

01:48:12 --> 01:48:16
They have a very different view

01:48:15 --> 01:48:17
of what is exactly happening.

01:48:18 --> 01:48:21
I would say that there are all sorts of interesting

01:48:20 --> 01:48:22
hypotheticals that come in to play

01:48:21 --> 01:48:24
when it comes to abortion.

01:48:23 --> 01:48:27
And you can play them any which way.

01:48:26 --> 01:48:29
But, let's put it this way,

01:48:28 --> 01:48:31
there are gradations of wrongs.

01:48:30 --> 01:48:34
I don't think that all abortions are equally blameworthy.

01:48:33 --> 01:48:39
Even if I would ban virtually all of them.

01:48:39 --> 01:48:42
I think that there are mitigating circumstances

01:48:41 --> 01:48:44
that make, while being wrong,

01:48:43 --> 01:48:47
some abortions less morally blameworthy than others.

01:48:46 --> 01:48:48
I think that,

01:48:49 --> 01:48:52
I can admit a difference between killing

01:48:52 --> 01:48:56
a two week old embryo in the womb,

01:48:55 --> 01:48:58
and stabbing a seven year old in the face.

01:48:57 --> 01:48:59
I can recognize all that while still saying,

01:48:58 --> 01:49:01
I think that it would be wrong to terminate a pregnancy.

01:49:00 --> 01:49:03
- Do you think the question when life begins,

01:49:02 --> 01:49:05
which I think is a fascinating question,

01:49:06 --> 01:49:09
is a question of science or a question of religion?

01:49:08 --> 01:49:11
- I mean when life begins, is a question of science.

01:49:10 --> 01:49:13
When that life becomes valuable enough for people

01:49:12 --> 01:49:17
to wanna protect it is is gonna be a question

01:49:16 --> 01:49:18
that is beyond science.

01:49:17 --> 01:49:19
Science doesn't have moral judgements

01:49:18 --> 01:49:20
to make about the value of human life.

01:49:19 --> 01:49:22
This is one of the problems that,

01:49:21 --> 01:49:23
Sam Harrison and I have had this argument many times,

01:49:22 --> 01:49:25
and it's always kind of interesting,

01:49:24 --> 01:49:27
'cause Sam is of the opinion

01:49:26 --> 01:49:29
that you can get to ought from is.

01:49:28 --> 01:49:31
That science says "Is, therefore we can learn ought."

01:49:30 --> 01:49:33
So human flourishing is the goal of life.

01:49:32 --> 01:49:34
And I always say to him,

01:49:33 --> 01:49:37
I don't see where you get that from evolutionary biology.

01:49:36 --> 01:49:39
You can assume it,

01:49:38 --> 01:49:40
just say you're assuming it,

01:49:39 --> 01:49:42
but don't pretend that that is a conclusion

01:49:41 --> 01:49:45
that you can draw straight from biological

01:49:44 --> 01:49:48
reality itself because obviously that doesn't exist

01:49:47 --> 01:49:49
in the animal world, for example.

01:49:48 --> 01:49:52
Nobody assumes the innate value of every ant.

01:49:51 --> 01:49:54
- I think I know your answer to this,

01:49:53 --> 01:49:54
but, let's test it

01:49:53 --> 01:49:57
'cause I think you're going to be wrong.

01:49:56 --> 01:49:58
(hearty laughing)

01:49:57 --> 01:50:00
So there's a robot behind you.

01:49:59 --> 01:50:02
Do you think there will be a time in the future

01:50:01 --> 01:50:04
when it will be unethical

01:50:03 --> 01:50:06
and illegal to kill a robot,

01:50:05 --> 01:50:08
because they will have sentience?

01:50:08 --> 01:50:11
My guess is you would say,

01:50:10 --> 01:50:14
"No, Lex, 'cause there's a fundamental difference

01:50:13 --> 01:50:15
"Between humans and robots."

01:50:14 --> 01:50:17
And I just want to get you on record

01:50:16 --> 01:50:19
'cause I think you'll be wrong.

01:50:18 --> 01:50:21
- It depends on the level of development

01:50:20 --> 01:50:23
I would assume of the robots.

01:50:22 --> 01:50:25
You're assuming a complexity in the robots

01:50:24 --> 01:50:29
that eventually imitates what we in the religious life

01:50:28 --> 01:50:30
would call the human soul.

01:50:29 --> 01:50:31
- [Lex] Yes.

01:50:30 --> 01:50:32
- The ability to choose freely, for example.

01:50:31 --> 01:50:33
- [Lex] Yes.

01:50:32 --> 01:50:34
- Which I believe is sort of the capacity

01:50:33 --> 01:50:36
for human beings.

01:50:35 --> 01:50:37
- The ability to suffer.

01:50:36 --> 01:50:42
- Yeah, if all of that could be

01:50:42 --> 01:50:45
proved and not programmed,

01:50:44 --> 01:50:48
meaning the freely willed capacity of a machine

01:50:47 --> 01:50:51
to do X, Y, or Z.

01:50:50 --> 01:50:53
- You, could not pinpoint exactly

01:50:52 --> 01:50:55
where it happens in the program.

01:50:54 --> 01:50:56
- Right.

01:50:55 --> 01:50:56
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:50:55 --> 01:50:57
- It's not deterministic.

01:50:56 --> 01:50:58
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:50:57 --> 01:51:01
- Then it would raise serious moral issues, for sure.

01:51:00 --> 01:51:03
I'm not sure I know to answer to that question.

01:51:02 --> 01:51:04
- Are you afraid of that time?

01:51:03 --> 01:51:06
- I'm not sure I'm afraid of that time.

01:51:05 --> 01:51:09
I mean, any more than I'd be afraid if aliens

01:51:08 --> 01:51:10
arrived in the world,

01:51:09 --> 01:51:12
and had these characteristics.

01:51:11 --> 01:51:13
- Well, there's just a lot of moral complexities

01:51:12 --> 01:51:15
and they don't necessarily have to be in the physical space.

01:51:14 --> 01:51:17
They can be in the digital space.

01:51:16 --> 01:51:20
There's an increased sophistication and number of bots

01:51:19 --> 01:51:22
on the internet, including on Twitter.

01:51:22 --> 01:51:25
As they become more and more intelligent,

01:51:24 --> 01:51:26
there's going to be serious questions

01:51:25 --> 01:51:29
about what is our moral duty

01:51:28 --> 01:51:32
to protect ones that have or claim

01:51:31 --> 01:51:33
to have an identity.

01:51:32 --> 01:51:34
- And that'll be really interesting.

01:51:33 --> 01:51:36
Actually what I'm afraid of is the opposite happening,

01:51:35 --> 01:51:37
meaning that people,

01:51:37 --> 01:51:40
the worst that should happen is that we develop robots

01:51:39 --> 01:51:42
o sophisticated that they appear to have free will

01:51:41 --> 01:51:45
and then we treat them with human dignity.

01:51:44 --> 01:51:46
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:51:45 --> 01:51:46
- That should be the worst that happens.

01:51:45 --> 01:51:48
What I'm afraid of is the opposite, is that,

01:51:47 --> 01:51:51
if we're talking about this particular hypothetical,

01:51:50 --> 01:51:52
that we develop robots

01:51:51 --> 01:51:53
that have all of these apparent abilities

01:51:52 --> 01:51:55
and then we dehumanize them,

01:51:54 --> 01:51:57
which leads us to also dehumanize the other humans

01:51:56 --> 01:52:00
around us; which you could easily see happening.

01:51:59 --> 01:52:02
And the devaluation of life to the point where

01:52:01 --> 01:52:03
it doesn't really matter.

01:52:02 --> 01:52:05
I mean people have always treated,

01:52:04 --> 01:52:09
unfortunately, newly discovered other humans this way.

01:52:08 --> 01:52:11
I don't think there's actually a new problem.

01:52:10 --> 01:52:13
I think it's a pretty old problem.

01:52:12 --> 01:52:15
It'll just be interesting when it's made of human hands.

01:52:14 --> 01:52:17
- Yeah, it's an opportunity

01:52:16 --> 01:52:18
to either celebrate humanity

01:52:17 --> 01:52:23
or to bring out the worst in humanity.

01:52:22 --> 01:52:24
The derision that naturally happens,

01:52:23 --> 01:52:27
like you said with pointing out the other,

01:52:26 --> 01:52:29
Let me ask you about climate change.

01:52:29 --> 01:52:32
Let's go from the meme

01:52:32 --> 01:52:35
to the profound philosophy.

01:52:34 --> 01:52:36
The meme is,

01:52:35 --> 01:52:38
there's a clip of you talking about climate change

01:52:37 --> 01:52:39
and saying that-

01:52:38 --> 01:52:40
Ah, the Aquaman meme.

01:52:39 --> 01:52:41
- You said that, "For the sake of argument,

01:52:40 --> 01:52:44
"And if the water level arises five to 10 feet

01:52:43 --> 01:52:46
"In the next hundred years,

01:52:45 --> 01:52:49
"People will just sell their homes and move."

01:52:48 --> 01:52:52
And then, the meme is sell to who?

01:52:51 --> 01:52:53
Can you argue both sides of that?

01:52:52 --> 01:52:55
- The argument that they're making as a straw man,

01:52:54 --> 01:52:57
the argument that I'm making is over time.

01:52:56 --> 01:52:59
I don't mean that if a tsunami is about to hit your house,

01:52:58 --> 01:53:00
you can list it on eBay.

01:52:59 --> 01:53:01
That's not what I mean, obviously.

01:53:00 --> 01:53:02
What I mean is that human beings

01:53:01 --> 01:53:04
have an extraordinary ability to adapt.

01:53:03 --> 01:53:06
It's actually our best quality.

01:53:05 --> 01:53:08
And that as water levels rise,

01:53:07 --> 01:53:10
real estate prices in those areas tends to fall.

01:53:09 --> 01:53:14
That over time people tend to abandon those areas.

01:53:13 --> 01:53:15
They tend to leave,

01:53:14 --> 01:53:16
they tend to, right now,

01:53:15 --> 01:53:17
sell their houses.

01:53:16 --> 01:53:18
And then, they tend to move.

01:53:17 --> 01:53:20
And eventually, those houses will be worthless

01:53:19 --> 01:53:21
and you won't have anybody to sell to.

01:53:20 --> 01:53:22
But presumably not that many people

01:53:21 --> 01:53:24
will be living there by that point,

01:53:23 --> 01:53:26
which is one of the reasons why the price would be low

01:53:25 --> 01:53:27
because there's no demand.

01:53:26 --> 01:53:28
- So it's over a hundred years.

01:53:27 --> 01:53:32
So all of these price dynamics are very gradual relative

01:53:31 --> 01:53:33
to the other price dynamics.

01:53:32 --> 01:53:35
- Correct, that's why the joke of it,

01:53:34 --> 01:53:37
of course is that like I'm saying that tomorrow

01:53:36 --> 01:53:38
there's a tsunami on your so step and you're like,

01:53:37 --> 01:53:40
"Oh, Bob will buy my house."

01:53:39 --> 01:53:41
Bob ain't gonna buy your house.

01:53:40 --> 01:53:43
Like we all get that.

01:53:42 --> 01:53:44
But it's a funny meme, I'll admit,

01:53:43 --> 01:53:45
I laughed at.

01:53:44 --> 01:53:47
- How's your view on climate change,

01:53:46 --> 01:53:51
the human contribution to climate change?

01:53:50 --> 01:53:53
What should we do in terms of policy to respond

01:53:52 --> 01:53:53
to climate change?

01:53:52 --> 01:53:55
How has that changed over the years?

01:53:54 --> 01:53:56
- I would say the truth

01:53:55 --> 01:53:58
is for years and years,

01:53:57 --> 01:54:00
I've believed that climate change was a reality

01:53:59 --> 01:54:04
and that anthropogenic climate change is a reality.

01:54:03 --> 01:54:06
I don't argue with the IPCC estimates.

01:54:05 --> 01:54:09
I know climatologists at places like MIT or Caltech,

01:54:08 --> 01:54:11
and they know this stuff better than I do.

01:54:11 --> 01:54:14
The notion that climate change is just not happening

01:54:13 --> 01:54:16
or that human beings have not contributed to climate change,

01:54:15 --> 01:54:17
I find doubtful.

01:54:16 --> 01:54:20
The question is to what extent human beings are contributing

01:54:19 --> 01:54:21
to climate change, that 50%, is it 70%?

01:54:20 --> 01:54:23
Is it 90%?

01:54:22 --> 01:54:25
I think there's a little bit more play in the joints there,

01:54:24 --> 01:54:26
so it's not totally clear.

01:54:25 --> 01:54:27
The one thing I do know,

01:54:26 --> 01:54:29
and this I know with factual accuracy,

01:54:28 --> 01:54:30
is that all of the measures that are

01:54:29 --> 01:54:33
currently being proposed are unworkable and will not happen.

01:54:32 --> 01:54:36
So when people say, Paris Climate Accords,

01:54:35 --> 01:54:37
even if those were imposed,

01:54:36 --> 01:54:40
you're talking about lowering the potential trajectory

01:54:39 --> 01:54:42
of climate change by a fraction of a degree.

01:54:41 --> 01:54:44
If you're talking about

01:54:45 --> 01:54:49
he Green New Deal, net zero by 2050,

01:54:48 --> 01:54:51
the carbon is up there in the air and the climate change

01:54:50 --> 01:54:52
is going to happen.

01:54:51 --> 01:54:53
Also, you're assuming

01:54:52 --> 01:54:56
that geopolitical dynamics don't exist.

01:54:55 --> 01:54:58
Everybody's gonna magically get on the same page

01:54:57 --> 01:55:01
and we're all gonna be imposing massive carbon taxes

01:55:00 --> 01:55:04
to get to net zero by 2050.

01:55:03 --> 01:55:05
I mean like hundreds of times higher

01:55:04 --> 01:55:06
than they currently are.

01:55:05 --> 01:55:08
And that's not me saying that, that's Klaus Schwab saying

01:55:07 --> 01:55:09
this at the World Economic Forum;

01:55:08 --> 01:55:12
who's a big advocate of exactly this sort of policy.

01:55:11 --> 01:55:14
And the reality is that we're gonna have to accept

01:55:13 --> 01:55:16
hat at least 1.5 degrees Celsius of climate change

01:55:15 --> 01:55:17
is baked into the cake by the end of the century.

01:55:16 --> 01:55:18
Again, not me talking William Nordhaus,

01:55:17 --> 01:55:20
the economist who just won the Nobel Prize

01:55:19 --> 01:55:21
in this stuff, talking.

01:55:20 --> 01:55:24
And so, what that suggests to me is what we've always known

01:55:23 --> 01:55:25
human beings are crap at mitigation

01:55:24 --> 01:55:27
and excellent in adaptation.

01:55:26 --> 01:55:30
We are very bad at mitigating our own fault.

01:55:29 --> 01:55:32
We are very good at adapting to the problems as they exist.

01:55:31 --> 01:55:35
Which means that all of the estimates that billions

01:55:34 --> 01:55:37
will die, that there will be mass starvation,

01:55:36 --> 01:55:40
that we'll see the migration in just a few years

01:55:39 --> 01:55:43
of hundreds of millions of people, those are wrong.

01:55:42 --> 01:55:45
What you'll see is a gradual change of living.

01:55:44 --> 01:55:47
People will move away from areas that are inundated

01:55:46 --> 01:55:48
on the coast.

01:55:47 --> 01:55:49
You'll see people building sea walls,

01:55:48 --> 01:55:52
you'll see people adapting new technologies to suck carbon

01:55:51 --> 01:55:53
out of the air.

01:55:52 --> 01:55:55
You'll see geoengineering,

01:55:54 --> 01:55:57
this is the sort of stuff that we should be focused on

01:55:56 --> 01:55:59
and the sort of bizarre focus

01:55:58 --> 01:56:00
on what if we just keep tossing

01:55:59 --> 01:56:03
hundreds of billions of dollars at the same three

01:56:02 --> 01:56:05
technologies over and over in the hopes

01:56:04 --> 01:56:06
that if we subsidize it,

01:56:05 --> 01:56:08
this will magically make it more efficient.

01:56:07 --> 01:56:10
I've seen no evidence whatsoever that

01:56:09 --> 01:56:13
is going to be the way that we get ourselves

01:56:12 --> 01:56:15
out of this, necessity being the mother invention.

01:56:14 --> 01:56:17
I think human beings will adapt because we have adapted

01:56:16 --> 01:56:18
and we'll continue to adapt.

01:56:17 --> 01:56:22
- So to the degree we invest in the threat of this,

01:56:21 --> 01:56:25
it should be into policies that help with the adaptation

01:56:24 --> 01:56:26
versus the mitigation?

01:56:25 --> 01:56:28
- Right, sea walls, geoengineering,

01:56:27 --> 01:56:30
developing technology to suck carbon out of the air.

01:56:29 --> 01:56:32
Again, if I thought that there was more sort of hope

01:56:31 --> 01:56:34
for the green technologies currently in play,

01:56:33 --> 01:56:36
than subsidization of those technologies,

01:56:35 --> 01:56:37
I might be a little bit more for,

01:56:36 --> 01:56:39
but I haven't seen tremendous progress

01:56:38 --> 01:56:40
over the course of the last 30 years

01:56:39 --> 01:56:43
in the reliability of, for example, wind energy.

01:56:42 --> 01:56:47
Or the ability to store solar energy to the extent necessary

01:56:46 --> 01:56:49
to actually power a grid.

01:56:48 --> 01:56:51
- What's your thoughts on nuclear energy?

01:56:50 --> 01:56:52
- Nuclear energy is great.

01:56:51 --> 01:56:53
Nuclear energy is a proven source of energy

01:56:52 --> 01:56:58
and we should be radically extending the use

01:56:57 --> 01:56:59
of nuclear energy.

01:56:58 --> 01:57:02
To me, honestly, this is like a litmus test question

01:57:01 --> 01:57:03
as to whether you take

01:57:02 --> 01:57:03
climate change seriously.

01:57:02 --> 01:57:05
If you're on right or left and you take climate change

01:57:04 --> 01:57:07
seriously, you should be in favor of nuclear energy.

01:57:06 --> 01:57:09
If you're not, I know that you're just,

01:57:08 --> 01:57:10
you have other priorities.

01:57:09 --> 01:57:11
- Yeah, the fascinating thing

01:57:10 --> 01:57:13
about the climate change debate is the dynamics

01:57:12 --> 01:57:16
of the fear mongering over the past few decades,

01:57:15 --> 01:57:18
'cause some of the nuclear energy

01:57:17 --> 01:57:19
was tied up into that somehow.

01:57:18 --> 01:57:22
There's a lot of fear about nuclear energy.

01:57:21 --> 01:57:25
It seems like there's a lot of social phenomena,

01:57:24 --> 01:57:29
social dynamics involved versus dealing with just science.

01:57:28 --> 01:57:33
It's interesting to watch and if, on my darker days,

01:57:32 --> 01:57:36
it makes me cynical about our ability to use reason

01:57:35 --> 01:57:40
and science to deal with the threats of the world.

01:57:39 --> 01:57:42
- I think that our ability to use reason and science

01:57:41 --> 01:57:43
to deal with threats of the world,

01:57:42 --> 01:57:46
is almost a timeframe question.

01:57:45 --> 01:57:48
So I think, we're very bad at looking down the road

01:57:47 --> 01:57:51
and saying, because people can't handle, for example,

01:57:50 --> 01:57:52
even things like compound interest.

01:57:51 --> 01:57:53
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:57:52 --> 01:57:54
- Right, the idea that if I put a dollar in the bank today,

01:57:53 --> 01:57:56
that 15 years from now,

01:57:55 --> 01:57:58
that's gonna be worth a lot more than a dollar.

01:57:57 --> 01:57:59
People can't actually see that.

01:57:58 --> 01:58:01
And so the idea of let's foresee a problem then we'll deal

01:58:00 --> 01:58:02
with it right now,

01:58:01 --> 01:58:04
as opposed to 30 years down the road.

01:58:03 --> 01:58:05
Typically, we let the problem happen

01:58:04 --> 01:58:06
and then we solve it,

01:58:05 --> 01:58:08
and it's bloodier and worse than it would've been,

01:58:07 --> 01:58:09
if we had solved it 30 years ago.

01:58:08 --> 01:58:12
But, it is in fact effective and sometimes it turns out

01:58:11 --> 01:58:14
he solution that we're proposing 30 years

01:58:13 --> 01:58:16
in advance is not effective.

01:58:15 --> 01:58:18
And that, can be a major problem as well.

01:58:17 --> 01:58:19
- Well that's then to steel man,

01:58:18 --> 01:58:23
the case for fear mongering, for irrational fear mongering,

01:58:22 --> 01:58:25
we need to be scared shitless

01:58:24 --> 01:58:26
in order for us to do anything.

01:58:27 --> 01:58:30
I'm generally against that.

01:58:29 --> 01:58:32
But, maybe on a population scale,

01:58:31 --> 01:58:36
maybe some of that is necessary for us to respond

01:58:35 --> 01:58:39
appropriate to long-term threats.

01:58:39 --> 01:58:41
We should be scared shitless.

01:58:40 --> 01:58:42
- But, I don't think think

01:58:41 --> 01:58:43
that we can actually do that though.

01:58:43 --> 01:58:46
First of all,

01:58:45 --> 01:58:47
platonic lives are generally bad.

01:58:46 --> 01:58:48
And then second of all,

01:58:47 --> 01:58:50
I don't think that we actually have the capacity to do this.

01:58:49 --> 01:58:52
I think that the people who are,

01:58:51 --> 01:58:55
the sort of elites of our society who get together in rooms

01:58:54 --> 01:58:56
and talk about this sort of stuff,

01:58:55 --> 01:58:57
and I've been in some of those meetings.

01:58:56 --> 01:58:59
At my synagogues Friday nights,

01:58:58 --> 01:59:00
actually no, but...

01:59:00 --> 01:59:04
- I was gonna make the joke, but I'm glad you did.

01:59:03 --> 01:59:07
- Yeah, I've been in rooms, Davos like rooms.

01:59:06 --> 01:59:10
And, when people discuss these sorts of topics,

01:59:09 --> 01:59:11
and they're like,

01:59:10 --> 01:59:12
"What if we just tell people that it's gonna be a disaster

01:59:11 --> 01:59:13
"With tsunamis and the day after tomorrow?"

01:59:12 --> 01:59:15
It's like, you guys don't have that power.

01:59:14 --> 01:59:17
You don't, and by the way,

01:59:16 --> 01:59:19
you dramatically undercut your own power because of COVID

01:59:18 --> 01:59:20
to do this sort of stuff.

01:59:19 --> 01:59:22
Because a lot of the,

01:59:21 --> 01:59:23
what if we scare the living hell out of you

01:59:22 --> 01:59:25
to the point where you stay in your own house

01:59:24 --> 01:59:26
for two years,

01:59:25 --> 01:59:28
and we tell you you can't send your kids to school,

01:59:27 --> 01:59:30
and then we tell you that the vaccine

01:59:29 --> 01:59:32
is gonna prevent transmission,

01:59:31 --> 01:59:35
and then we also tell you that we need to spend $7 trillion

01:59:34 --> 01:59:36
in one year,

01:59:35 --> 01:59:37
and it won't have any inflationary effect.

01:59:36 --> 01:59:38
And it turns out,

01:59:37 --> 01:59:40
you're wrong on literally all of those things.

01:59:39 --> 01:59:42
The last two years have done more to undermine

01:59:41 --> 01:59:44
institutional trust than any time

01:59:43 --> 01:59:45
in probably American history.

01:59:44 --> 01:59:46
It's pretty, pretty amazing.

01:59:45 --> 01:59:48
- Yeah, I tend to agree with the only thing we have to fear

01:59:47 --> 01:59:50
is fear itself.

01:59:49 --> 01:59:54
Let me ask you, back to the question of God

01:59:54 --> 01:59:56
in a big ridiculous question.

01:59:55 --> 01:59:58
Who's God?

01:59:57 --> 01:59:59
- Who is God?

01:59:58 --> 02:00:03
I'm gonna use sort of the Aquinas formulation

02:00:02 --> 02:00:06
of what God is.

02:00:05 --> 02:00:11
That, if there is a cause of all things not physical things,

02:00:10 --> 02:00:15
if there is a cause underlying the reason of the universe,

02:00:14 --> 02:00:17
then that is the thing we call God.

02:00:16 --> 02:00:20
So not a big guy in the sky with the beard,.

02:00:20 --> 02:00:25
He is the force underlying the logic of the universe,

02:00:24 --> 02:00:27
if there is a logic to the universe.

02:00:26 --> 02:00:32
And He is the creator in the Judea view of that universe.

02:00:33 --> 02:00:37
And He, does have an interest in us

02:00:36 --> 02:00:40
living in accordance with the laws of the universe

02:00:39 --> 02:00:43
that, if you're a religious Jew, are encoded in the Torah.

02:00:42 --> 02:00:44
But, if you're not religious Jew,

02:00:43 --> 02:00:46
it would be encoded in the natural law

02:00:45 --> 02:00:48
by sort of Catholic theology.

02:00:47 --> 02:00:50
- Why do you think God created the universe

02:00:49 --> 02:00:53
or as is popularly asked,

02:00:52 --> 02:00:56
what do you think is the meaning behind it?

02:00:55 --> 02:00:57
What's the meaning of life?

02:00:56 --> 02:00:59
- What's the meaning of life?

02:00:58 --> 02:01:00
So I think that the meaning of life

02:01:00 --> 02:01:04
is to fulfill what God made you to do.

02:01:03 --> 02:01:07
And that is a series of roles.

02:01:06 --> 02:01:08
I think that human beings,

02:01:07 --> 02:01:10
and here you have to look to sort of human nature

02:01:09 --> 02:01:13
rather than looking kind of to big questions.

02:01:13 --> 02:01:15
I've evolved something

02:01:14 --> 02:01:17
that I've really been working on.

02:01:16 --> 02:01:18
I'm writing a book about this actually,

02:01:17 --> 02:01:22
that I call colloquially role theory.

02:01:21 --> 02:01:25
And basically the idea is that the way that we interact

02:01:24 --> 02:01:27
with the world is through a series of roles.

02:01:26 --> 02:01:30
And those are also the things we find most important

02:01:29 --> 02:01:32
and most implementable.

02:01:31 --> 02:01:34
There's sort of virtue ethics,

02:01:33 --> 02:01:37
which suggests that if we act in accordance with virtue,

02:01:36 --> 02:01:39
like Aristotle, then we will be living

02:01:38 --> 02:01:42
the most fulfilled and meaningful life.

02:01:41 --> 02:01:44
And then, you have sort of deontological ethics,

02:01:43 --> 02:01:47
continent ethics, that it's a rule-based ethic.

02:01:46 --> 02:01:48
You follow the rules,

02:01:47 --> 02:01:50
then you'll then you'll find the meaning of life.

02:01:49 --> 02:01:53
And then, what I'm proposing is that there's something

02:01:52 --> 02:01:54
that I would call role ethics,

02:01:53 --> 02:01:55
which is there are a series of roles

02:01:54 --> 02:01:57
that we play across our lives;

02:01:56 --> 02:01:57
which are also the things

02:01:56 --> 02:01:59
that we tend to put on our tombstones,

02:01:58 --> 02:02:00
and find the most meaningful.

02:01:59 --> 02:02:02
So when you go to a cemetery,

02:02:01 --> 02:02:04
you can see what people found the most meaningful

02:02:03 --> 02:02:05
because it's the stuff they put on the stone

02:02:04 --> 02:02:07
that has like four words on it.

02:02:06 --> 02:02:11
Like beloved father, beloved mother, sister, brother,

02:02:10 --> 02:02:13
you might have a job once in a while;

02:02:13 --> 02:02:16
a creator, a religious person.

02:02:15 --> 02:02:18
These are all roles that have existed across societies

02:02:17 --> 02:02:19
and across humanity.

02:02:18 --> 02:02:22
And those are the things where we actually find meaning.

02:02:21 --> 02:02:25
The way that we navigate those roles brings us meaning.

02:02:24 --> 02:02:30
And I think that God created us in order to fulfill those

02:02:29 --> 02:02:32
roles for purposes that I can't begin to understand

02:02:31 --> 02:02:33
'cause I ain't him.

02:02:32 --> 02:02:37
And, the more we recognize those roles,

02:02:36 --> 02:02:39
and the more we live those roles,

02:02:38 --> 02:02:41
and then we can express freedom within those roles.

02:02:40 --> 02:02:43
I think that liberty exists inside each of those roles,

02:02:42 --> 02:02:45
and that's what makes all of our lives

02:02:44 --> 02:02:45
different and fun.

02:02:44 --> 02:02:47
We all parent in different ways,

02:02:46 --> 02:02:48
but being a parent is a meaningful role.

02:02:48 --> 02:02:51
We all have spouses, but you know,

02:02:50 --> 02:02:53
how you interact with that relationship

02:02:52 --> 02:02:55
is what makes your life meaningful and interesting.

02:02:54 --> 02:02:58
That is what we are put on earth to do.

02:02:57 --> 02:03:00
And, if we perform those roles properly,

02:02:59 --> 02:03:01
and those roles do include things like being a creator,

02:03:00 --> 02:03:03
like we have a creative instinct as human beings,

02:03:02 --> 02:03:06
being a creator, being an innovator,

02:03:05 --> 02:03:08
being a defender of your family,

02:03:07 --> 02:03:09
being somebody who builds up,

02:03:08 --> 02:03:10
being a social member of your community;

02:03:09 --> 02:03:12
which is something that we're built to do.

02:03:11 --> 02:03:13
If we fulfill those roles properly,

02:03:12 --> 02:03:14
then we will have made the world a better place

02:03:13 --> 02:03:16
then we inherited it.

02:03:15 --> 02:03:20
And, we'll also have had the joy of experiencing

02:03:19 --> 02:03:24
the sort of flow they talk about in psychology.

02:03:23 --> 02:03:25
Where, when you engage in these roles,

02:03:24 --> 02:03:27
you actually do feel a flow.

02:03:26 --> 02:03:29
- So these roles are a fundamental part

02:03:28 --> 02:03:30
of the human condition.

02:03:29 --> 02:03:30
- [Ben] Yes.

02:03:29 --> 02:03:33
- So, the book you're working on

02:03:32 --> 02:03:37
is constructing a system to help us understand.

02:03:37 --> 02:03:41
- It's looking at, let's assume that all that's true.

02:03:40 --> 02:03:43
The real question in the book is how do you construct

02:03:42 --> 02:03:47
a flourishing and useful society in politics?

02:03:46 --> 02:03:50
- Ah, so a society level.

02:03:49 --> 02:03:52
If this is our understanding of a human being,

02:03:51 --> 02:03:53
how do we construct a good society?

02:03:52 --> 02:03:55
- Right, exactly, because I think that a lot

02:03:54 --> 02:03:57
of political theory is right now based in

02:03:56 --> 02:04:00
either J.S. Mill kind of thought,

02:03:59 --> 02:04:02
which is all that a good politics does,

02:04:01 --> 02:04:03
allows you wave your hand around

02:04:02 --> 02:04:04
until you hit somebody in the face.

02:04:03 --> 02:04:06
Or a Rawlsian thought,

02:04:05 --> 02:04:08
which is what if we constructed society

02:04:07 --> 02:04:11
in order to achieve the most for the least, essentially.

02:04:10 --> 02:04:14
What if we constructed society around what actually makes

02:04:13 --> 02:04:16
humans the most fulfilled?

02:04:15 --> 02:04:21
And that is the fulfillment of these particular roles.

02:04:20 --> 02:04:22
And where does liberty come into that?

02:04:21 --> 02:04:25
How do you avoid the idea of a tyranny in that?

02:04:24 --> 02:04:26
"You have to be a mother, you must be a father,

02:04:25 --> 02:04:27
"You must be a..."

02:04:26 --> 02:04:29
Where does freedom come into that?

02:04:28 --> 02:04:32
Can you reject those roles totally as a society and be okay?

02:04:31 --> 02:04:33
The answer probably is not.

02:04:32 --> 02:04:37
So you need a society that actually promotes

02:04:36 --> 02:04:38
and protects those roles,

02:04:37 --> 02:04:40
but also protects the freedom inside those roles.

02:04:39 --> 02:04:42
And that raises a more fundamental question

02:04:41 --> 02:04:44
of what exactly liberty is for.

02:04:43 --> 02:04:45
And I think that both the right and the left

02:04:44 --> 02:04:48
actually tend to make a mistake when they discuss liberty.

02:04:47 --> 02:04:51
The left tends to think that liberty is an ultimate good,

02:04:50 --> 02:04:53
that simple choice makes a bad thing good,

02:04:52 --> 02:04:55
which is not true.

02:04:54 --> 02:04:56
And I think the right talks about liberty

02:04:55 --> 02:04:58
in almost the same terms sometimes.

02:04:57 --> 02:05:00
And, I think that's not true either.

02:04:59 --> 02:05:03
The question is whether liberty is of inherent value

02:05:02 --> 02:05:04
or instrumental value.

02:05:03 --> 02:05:07
Is liberty good in and of itself or is liberty good

02:05:06 --> 02:05:10
because it allows you to achieve X, Y or Z?

02:05:09 --> 02:05:12
And, I've thought about this one a lot,

02:05:11 --> 02:05:14
and I tend to come down on the latter side of the aisle.

02:05:13 --> 02:05:15
I mean this is,

02:05:14 --> 02:05:15
you asked me areas where I moved,

02:05:14 --> 02:05:16
this may be an area where I've moved.

02:05:15 --> 02:05:19
Is that I think when you think more shallowly about politics

02:05:18 --> 02:05:22
or maybe more quickly because this is how we talk in America

02:05:21 --> 02:05:23
is about liberties and rights.

02:05:22 --> 02:05:24
We tend to think that the right

02:05:23 --> 02:05:26
is what make, not like the political right,

02:05:25 --> 02:05:27
rights make things good,

02:05:26 --> 02:05:29
liberties make things good.

02:05:28 --> 02:05:29
The question really is

02:05:28 --> 02:05:31
what are those rights and liberties for?

02:05:30 --> 02:05:32
- [Lex] Yeah.

02:05:31 --> 02:05:33
- Now, you have to be careful,

02:05:32 --> 02:05:35
so that that doesn't shade into tyranny.

02:05:34 --> 02:05:36
You can only have liberty to do the thing

02:05:35 --> 02:05:38
that I say that you can do.

02:05:37 --> 02:05:41
But, there have to be spheres of liberty that are roiling

02:05:40 --> 02:05:44
and interesting and filled with debate,

02:05:43 --> 02:05:47
but without threatening the chief institutions

02:05:46 --> 02:05:48
that surround those liberties.

02:05:47 --> 02:05:49
Because if you destroy the institutions,

02:05:48 --> 02:05:51
liberties will go too.

02:05:50 --> 02:05:52
If you knock down the pillars of the society,

02:05:51 --> 02:05:54
the liberties that are on top of those pillars

02:05:53 --> 02:05:55
are going to collapse.

02:05:54 --> 02:05:57
And, I think if people are feeling as though

02:05:56 --> 02:06:00
we're on the verge tyranny, I think that's why.

02:05:59 --> 02:06:01
- This is fascinating by the way.

02:06:00 --> 02:06:05
It's an instrumental perspective on liberty

02:06:04 --> 02:06:08
that's gonna give me a lot to think about.

02:06:07 --> 02:06:10
Let me ask a personal question.

02:06:09 --> 02:06:13
Was there ever a time that you had a crisis of faith,

02:06:12 --> 02:06:15
where you questioned your belief in God?

02:06:14 --> 02:06:17
- Sure, and I would,

02:06:16 --> 02:06:18
less call it a crisis of faith,

02:06:17 --> 02:06:20
and an ongoing question of faith;

02:06:19 --> 02:06:21
which I think is,

02:06:20 --> 02:06:22
I hope most religious people.

02:06:23 --> 02:06:26
The word Israel, right, in Hebrew,

02:06:25 --> 02:06:28
Israel means to struggle with God.

02:06:27 --> 02:06:30
That's literally what the word means.

02:06:29 --> 02:06:34
And so, the idea of struggling with God.

02:06:33 --> 02:06:36
Where if you're Jewish or Bene Israel,

02:06:35 --> 02:06:38
the idea of struggling with God I think is endemic

02:06:37 --> 02:06:40
to the human condition.

02:06:39 --> 02:06:41
If you understand what God's doing,

02:06:40 --> 02:06:43
then I think you're wrong.

02:06:42 --> 02:06:46
And, if you think that that question doesn't matter,

02:06:45 --> 02:06:47
then I think you're also wrong.

02:06:46 --> 02:06:50
I think that God is a very necessary hypothesis.

02:06:49 --> 02:06:53
- So struggle, the struggle with God is life.

02:06:52 --> 02:06:54
That is the process of life?

02:06:53 --> 02:06:56
- That's right, because you're never gonna get to that

02:06:55 --> 02:06:58
answer otherwise your God, and you aren't.

02:06:57 --> 02:07:01
- Why does God allow cruelty and suffering in the world?

02:07:00 --> 02:07:03
One of the tough questions.

02:07:02 --> 02:07:04
- So we're going deep here.

02:07:03 --> 02:07:08
There's two types of cruelty and suffering.

02:07:07 --> 02:07:10
So if we're talking about human cruelty and suffering,

02:07:09 --> 02:07:12
because God does not intervene to prevent people

02:07:11 --> 02:07:15
from exercising their free will

02:07:14 --> 02:07:17
because to do so would be to deprive human beings

02:07:16 --> 02:07:19
of the choice that makes them human.

02:07:18 --> 02:07:22
This is the sin of the garden of Eden, basically.

02:07:21 --> 02:07:24
Is that God could make you an angel,

02:07:23 --> 02:07:25
in which case you wouldn't have the choice

02:07:24 --> 02:07:26
to do the wrong thing.

02:07:25 --> 02:07:29
But, so long as we are going to allow for cause and effect

02:07:28 --> 02:07:32
in a universe shaped by your choice,

02:07:31 --> 02:07:34
cruelty and evil are going to exist.

02:07:33 --> 02:07:37
And then, there's the question of just the natural cruelty

02:07:36 --> 02:07:38
and vicissitudes of life.

02:07:38 --> 02:07:41
And, the answer there is I think that God obscures himself.

02:07:40 --> 02:07:44
I think that if God were to appear in all of his glory

02:07:43 --> 02:07:46
to people on a regular basis,

02:07:45 --> 02:07:48
I think that would make faith,

02:07:47 --> 02:07:49
you wouldn't need it.

02:07:48 --> 02:07:51
There'd be no such thing as faith.

02:07:50 --> 02:07:53
It would just be reality.

02:07:52 --> 02:07:56
Nobody has to prove to you that the sun rises every day.

02:07:55 --> 02:07:59
But, if God is to allow us the choice to believe in Him,

02:07:58 --> 02:08:02
which is the ultimate choice from a religious point of view,

02:08:01 --> 02:08:04
then He's gonna have to obscure himself behind tragedy

02:08:03 --> 02:08:06
and horror and all those other things.

02:08:05 --> 02:08:09
I mean, this is a fairly well known capitalistic concept

02:08:08 --> 02:08:10
called tzimtzum in Judaism;

02:08:09 --> 02:08:12
which is the idea that when God created the universe,

02:08:11 --> 02:08:15
He sort of withdrew in order to make space

02:08:14 --> 02:08:17
for all of these things to happen.

02:08:16 --> 02:08:20
- So God doesn't have a instrumental perspective on liberty?

02:08:20 --> 02:08:23
- Right, in a chief sense,

02:08:22 --> 02:08:25
He does because the best use of liberty

02:08:24 --> 02:08:27
is going to be belief in Him.

02:08:26 --> 02:08:28
And you can misuse your liberty, right?

02:08:27 --> 02:08:30
There will be consequences

02:08:30 --> 02:08:32
if you believe in an afterlife

02:08:31 --> 02:08:35
or if you believe in sort of a generalized better version

02:08:34 --> 02:08:38
of life led by faith, then liberty does have a purpose.

02:08:37 --> 02:08:40
But, He also believes that you have to give people,

02:08:39 --> 02:08:41
from a cosmic perspective,

02:08:40 --> 02:08:45
the liberty to do wrong without threatening

02:08:44 --> 02:08:47
all the institutions of society.

02:08:46 --> 02:08:49
I mean, that's why it does say in the Bible

02:08:48 --> 02:08:50
that if man sheds blood by man,

02:08:49 --> 02:08:51
shall his blood be shed, right.

02:08:50 --> 02:08:52
That there are punishments,

02:08:51 --> 02:08:56
that are in biblical thought for doing things

02:08:55 --> 02:08:57
that are wrong.

02:08:56 --> 02:09:00
- So for a human being who lacks the faith in God.

02:08:59 --> 02:09:03
So if you're an atheist, can you still be a good person?

02:09:02 --> 02:09:05
- Of course, 100%.

02:09:04 --> 02:09:06
And there are a lot of religious people

02:09:05 --> 02:09:07
who are crappy people.

02:09:06 --> 02:09:09
- How do I understand that tension?

02:09:08 --> 02:09:10
- Well, from a religious perspective,

02:09:09 --> 02:09:14
what you would say is that it is perfectly plausible to live

02:09:13 --> 02:09:16
in accordance with a set of rules that don't damage

02:09:15 --> 02:09:18
other people without believing in God.

02:09:17 --> 02:09:20
You just might be understanding the reason

02:09:19 --> 02:09:22
for doing that wrong, is what a religious person would say.

02:09:22 --> 02:09:26
This is the conversation, again, that I had with Sam.

02:09:25 --> 02:09:28
Basically, is you and I agree, I said this to Sam,

02:09:27 --> 02:09:29
"You and I agree nearly everything

02:09:28 --> 02:09:30
"When it comes to morality.

02:09:29 --> 02:09:32
"Like we probably disagree on 15 to 20% of things.

02:09:31 --> 02:09:34
"The other 80% is because you grew up

02:09:33 --> 02:09:35
"In a Judeo Christian society and so do I.

02:09:34 --> 02:09:37
"And we grew up 10 miles from each other,

02:09:36 --> 02:09:38
"Around the turn of the millennium."

02:09:37 --> 02:09:40
So there's that.

02:09:39 --> 02:09:43
So you can perfectly well be an atheist

02:09:42 --> 02:09:46
living a good moral decent life

02:09:45 --> 02:09:48
because you can live a good moral decent life

02:09:47 --> 02:09:49
with regard to other people

02:09:48 --> 02:09:50
without believing in God.

02:09:49 --> 02:09:51
I don't think you can build a society on that

02:09:50 --> 02:09:53
because I think that that relies on

02:09:52 --> 02:09:54
the sort of

02:09:55 --> 02:09:57
goodness of mankind.

02:09:56 --> 02:09:58
Natural goodness of mankind.

02:09:57 --> 02:09:59
I don't believe in the natural goodness of mankind.

02:09:58 --> 02:10:00
- [Lex] You don't?

02:09:59 --> 02:10:02
- No, I believe that man was created

02:10:01 --> 02:10:04
both sinful and with the capacity for sin,

02:10:03 --> 02:10:05
and the capacity for good.

02:10:04 --> 02:10:10
- But, if you let them be on their own, doesn't-

02:10:09 --> 02:10:11
Without social institutions to shape them,

02:10:10 --> 02:10:14
I think that that's very likely to go poorly.

02:10:13 --> 02:10:15
- Oh, interesting.

02:10:14 --> 02:10:18
Well, we came to something we disagree on, but that may be,

02:10:18 --> 02:10:21
that might reflect itself in our approach

02:10:20 --> 02:10:22
to Twitter as well.

02:10:21 --> 02:10:23
(hearty laughing)

02:10:22 --> 02:10:25
I think if humans are left on their own,

02:10:26 --> 02:10:29
they tend towards good.

02:10:28 --> 02:10:32
They definitely have the capacity for good and evil.

02:10:31 --> 02:10:34
But, when left on their own,

02:10:33 --> 02:10:37
I tend to believe they're good.

02:10:36 --> 02:10:39
- I think they might be good with limits.

02:10:38 --> 02:10:40
What I mean by that is that what the evidence

02:10:39 --> 02:10:41
I think tends to show

02:10:40 --> 02:10:43
is that human beings are quite tribal.

02:10:42 --> 02:10:46
So what you'll end up with is people who are good

02:10:45 --> 02:10:47
with their immediate family,

02:10:46 --> 02:10:48
and maybe their immediate neighbors.

02:10:47 --> 02:10:50
And, then when they're threatened by an outside tribe,

02:10:49 --> 02:10:52
then they kill everyone.

02:10:51 --> 02:10:54
Which is sort of the history of civilization

02:10:53 --> 02:10:55
in the pre-civilizational era;

02:10:54 --> 02:10:57
which was a very violent time.

02:10:56 --> 02:10:59
Pre-civilizational era was quite violent.

02:10:58 --> 02:11:01
- Do you think on the topic of tribalism

02:11:00 --> 02:11:03
in our modern world,

02:11:02 --> 02:11:06
what are the pros and cons of tribes?

02:11:05 --> 02:11:06
Is that something

02:11:05 --> 02:11:09
where we should try to outgrow as a civilization?

02:11:08 --> 02:11:11
- I don't think it's ever gonna be possible

02:11:10 --> 02:11:13
to fully outgrow tribalism.

02:11:12 --> 02:11:15
I think it's a natural human condition

02:11:14 --> 02:11:18
to want to be with people who think like you

02:11:17 --> 02:11:20
or have a common set of beliefs.

02:11:19 --> 02:11:22
And, I think trying to obliterate

02:11:21 --> 02:11:23
that in the name of a universalism,

02:11:22 --> 02:11:25
likely leads to utopian results

02:11:24 --> 02:11:26
that have devastating consequences.

02:11:25 --> 02:11:31
Utopian sort of universalism has been failing every time

02:11:30 --> 02:11:32
it's tried, whether you're talking about now

02:11:31 --> 02:11:35
it seems to be sort of a liberal universalism;

02:11:34 --> 02:11:37
which is being rejected by a huge number of people

02:11:36 --> 02:11:39
around the world in various different cultures.

02:11:38 --> 02:11:41
Or you're talking about religious universalism,

02:11:40 --> 02:11:45
which typically comes with religious tyranny

02:11:44 --> 02:11:46
or they're talking about communistic

02:11:45 --> 02:11:49
or Nazistic sort of universalism,

02:11:48 --> 02:11:50
which comes with mass slaughter.

02:11:49 --> 02:11:53
So this is, universalism, I'm not a believer in.

02:11:52 --> 02:11:56
I think that you have some values

02:11:55 --> 02:11:59
that are fairly limited, that all human beings

02:11:58 --> 02:12:02
hould hold in common, and that's pretty much it.

02:12:01 --> 02:12:04
I think that everybody should have the ability

02:12:03 --> 02:12:07
to join with their own culture.

02:12:06 --> 02:12:09
I think how we define tribes is a different thing.

02:12:08 --> 02:12:11
So I think that tribes should not be defined

02:12:10 --> 02:12:14
by innate physical characteristics, for example.

02:12:13 --> 02:12:16
Because I think that, thank God,

02:12:15 --> 02:12:18
as a civilization we've outgrown that.

02:12:17 --> 02:12:23
And I think that that is a childish way to view the world.

02:12:22 --> 02:12:25
And, all the tall people aren't a tribe.

02:12:24 --> 02:12:25
All the black people aren't,

02:12:24 --> 02:12:27
all the white people aren't a tribe.

02:12:26 --> 02:12:29
- So the tribes should be formed over ideas

02:12:28 --> 02:12:30
versus physical characteristic?

02:12:29 --> 02:12:31
- That's right, which is why,

02:12:30 --> 02:12:32
actually to go back to sort of the beginning

02:12:31 --> 02:12:35
of the conversation when it comes to Jews,

02:12:34 --> 02:12:39
I'm not a big believer in ethnic Judaism.

02:12:38 --> 02:12:42
As a person who takes Judaism seriously.

02:12:41 --> 02:12:44
Judaism is more to me than you were born with a last name

02:12:43 --> 02:12:46
like Berg or Stein.

02:12:45 --> 02:12:46
And so I-

02:12:45 --> 02:12:48
Hitler would disagree with you.

02:12:47 --> 02:12:49
- He would disagree with me.

02:12:48 --> 02:12:50
But that's 'cause he was a tribalist.

02:12:49 --> 02:12:52
Who thought in racial terms.

02:12:51 --> 02:12:56
- So maybe robots will help us see humans as one tribe,

02:12:55 --> 02:12:57
maybe that, as long as-

02:12:57 --> 02:12:58
This is Reagan's idea, right?

02:12:57 --> 02:13:00
Reagan said, "Well, if there's an alien invasion,

02:12:59 --> 02:13:01
"Then we'll all be on the same side.

02:13:00 --> 02:13:02
"So I'll go over to the Soviets and we'll talk about it."

02:13:01 --> 02:13:04
- There's some deep truth to that.

02:13:05 --> 02:13:09
What does it mean to be a good man?

02:13:08 --> 02:13:12
The various role that a human being takes on

02:13:12 --> 02:13:15
in this role theory that you've spoken about.

02:13:14 --> 02:13:17
What does it mean to be good?

02:13:17 --> 02:13:20
- It means to perform,

02:13:19 --> 02:13:21
now I will do Aristotle.

02:13:20 --> 02:13:24
It means to perform the function well.

02:13:23 --> 02:13:26
What Aristotle says is the good, is not like moral good,

02:13:25 --> 02:13:29
moral evil, in the way that we tend to think about it.

02:13:28 --> 02:13:32
He meant that a good cup holds liquid,

02:13:31 --> 02:13:34
a good spoon holds soup.

02:13:33 --> 02:13:36
It means that, a thing that is broken

02:13:35 --> 02:13:37
can't hold those things, right?

02:13:36 --> 02:13:39
So the idea of being a good person means that you are

02:13:38 --> 02:13:42
fulfilling the function for which you were made.

02:13:41 --> 02:13:45
It's a teleological view of humanity.

02:13:44 --> 02:13:46
So if you're a good father,

02:13:45 --> 02:13:48
this means that you are bringing up your child in durable

02:13:47 --> 02:13:51
values that is going to bring them up healthy,

02:13:50 --> 02:13:53
capable of protecting themselves and passing on

02:13:52 --> 02:13:54
the traditional wisdom of the ages

02:13:53 --> 02:13:55
to future generations;

02:13:54 --> 02:13:57
while allowing for the capacity for innovation.

02:13:56 --> 02:13:58
That'd be being a good father.

02:13:57 --> 02:14:02
Being a good spouse would mean protecting and unifying

02:14:01 --> 02:14:05
with your spouse and building a safe family,

02:14:04 --> 02:14:08
and a place to raise children.

02:14:07 --> 02:14:09
Being a good citizen of your community

02:14:09 --> 02:14:12
means protecting the fellow citizens of your community;

02:14:11 --> 02:14:16
while incentivizing them to build for themselves.

02:14:15 --> 02:14:19
And, it becomes actually much easier to think of how to,

02:14:18 --> 02:14:22
this is why I like the role theory because it's very hard,

02:14:21 --> 02:14:23
since in sort of in virtue theory,

02:14:22 --> 02:14:24
to say be generous.

02:14:23 --> 02:14:25
How does that manifest?

02:14:24 --> 02:14:26
I don't know.

02:14:25 --> 02:14:27
I don't know what that looks like.

02:14:26 --> 02:14:30
Sometimes being generous might being not generous

02:14:29 --> 02:14:31
to other people.

02:14:30 --> 02:14:34
When Aristotle says that you should be benevolent,

02:14:33 --> 02:14:35
what does that mean?

02:14:34 --> 02:14:36
This is very vague.

02:14:35 --> 02:14:37
