When I say be a good dad,

02:14:36 --> 02:14:39
most people sort of have a gut level understanding

02:14:38 --> 02:14:39
of what it means to be a good dad.

02:14:38 --> 02:14:41
And mostly, they have a gut level understanding

02:14:40 --> 02:14:43
of what it means to be a really bad dad.

02:14:42 --> 02:14:46
And so, what it means to be a good man is to fulfill those

02:14:45 --> 02:14:49
roles as many of them as you can,

02:14:48 --> 02:14:50
properly and at full function.

02:14:49 --> 02:14:52
And that's a very hard job.

02:14:51 --> 02:14:53
And I've said before that,

02:14:52 --> 02:14:56
because I engage a lot with the public and all of this,

02:14:55 --> 02:14:57
the word great comes up a lot.

02:14:56 --> 02:14:58
What is it to be a great leader?

02:14:57 --> 02:14:59
What is it to be a great person?

02:14:58 --> 02:15:00
And I've always said to people,

02:14:59 --> 02:15:02
it's actually fairly easy to be great.

02:15:01 --> 02:15:04
It's very difficult to be good.

02:15:03 --> 02:15:06
There are a lot of very great people, who are not very good,

02:15:05 --> 02:15:07
and they're not a lot of good people.

02:15:06 --> 02:15:09
And most of them,

02:15:08 --> 02:15:10
frankly, most good people

02:15:11 --> 02:15:14
die mourned by their family and friends.

02:15:13 --> 02:15:15
And two generations later, they're forgotten.

02:15:14 --> 02:15:17
But, those are the people who incrementally move the ball

02:15:16 --> 02:15:19
forward in the world sometimes much more than the people

02:15:18 --> 02:15:21
who are considered great.

02:15:20 --> 02:15:25
- Understand the role in your life that involves being a cup

02:15:24 --> 02:15:26
and be damn good at it.

02:15:25 --> 02:15:27
- Exactly, that's right.

02:15:27 --> 02:15:30
- [Lex] Hold the soup, it's very-

02:15:29 --> 02:15:31
Jordan Peterson.

02:15:30 --> 02:15:33
- Yeah, it's like lobster with Jordan Peterson.

02:15:32 --> 02:15:34
- [Ben] Exactly, exactly.

02:15:33 --> 02:15:36
I think people quote you for years and years

02:15:35 --> 02:15:38
to come on that.

02:15:37 --> 02:15:39
What advice would you give,

02:15:38 --> 02:15:41
a lot of young people look up to you,

02:15:40 --> 02:15:44
what advice, despite their better judgment.

02:15:43 --> 02:15:46
No, I'm just kidding. (hearty laughing)

02:15:45 --> 02:15:48
Only kidding, only kidding.

02:15:47 --> 02:15:51
They seriously look up to you and draw inspiration

02:15:50 --> 02:15:53
from your ideas, from your bold thinking.

02:15:52 --> 02:15:55
What advice would you give to them?

02:15:54 --> 02:15:58
How to live a life worth living?

02:15:57 --> 02:16:00
How to have a career

02:15:59 --> 02:16:03
they can be proud of and everything like that?

02:16:04 --> 02:16:06
- Live out the values that you think

02:16:05 --> 02:16:07
are really important

02:16:06 --> 02:16:09
and seek those values in others,

02:16:08 --> 02:16:11
would be the first piece of advice.

02:16:10 --> 02:16:12
Second piece of advice,

02:16:11 --> 02:16:15
don't go on Twitter until you're 26.

02:16:14 --> 02:16:15
- Why 26?

02:16:14 --> 02:16:18
- Because your brain is fully developed at that point.

02:16:18 --> 02:16:20
As I said early on,

02:16:19 --> 02:16:22
I was on social media and writing columns

02:16:21 --> 02:16:24
from the time I was 17.

02:16:23 --> 02:16:25
It was a great opportunity.

02:16:24 --> 02:16:26
And as it turns out,

02:16:25 --> 02:16:29
a great temptation to say enormous numbers of stupid things.

02:16:28 --> 02:16:30
When you're young,

02:16:29 --> 02:16:31
you're kind of trying out ideas

02:16:30 --> 02:16:32
and you're putting them on,

02:16:31 --> 02:16:33
you're taking them off.

02:16:32 --> 02:16:34
And, social media permenatizes those things

02:16:33 --> 02:16:36
and engraves them in stone,

02:16:35 --> 02:16:38
and then that's used against you for the rest of your life.

02:16:37 --> 02:16:39
So I tell young people this all the time,

02:16:38 --> 02:16:41
if you wanna be on social media, be on social media,

02:16:40 --> 02:16:43
but don't post, watch.

02:16:42 --> 02:16:45
If you wanna take in information, more importantly,

02:16:44 --> 02:16:46
you should read books.

02:16:45 --> 02:16:48
As far as other advice,

02:16:47 --> 02:16:51
I'd say engage in your community.

02:16:50 --> 02:16:53
There's no substitute for engaging in your community,

02:16:52 --> 02:16:54
and engage in interpersonal action

02:16:53 --> 02:16:56
because that that will soften you

02:16:55 --> 02:16:58
and make you a better person.

02:16:57 --> 02:17:00
I've become a better person since I got married.

02:16:59 --> 02:17:02
I've become an even better person since I've had kids.

02:17:01 --> 02:17:03
So you can imagine how terrible I was

02:17:02 --> 02:17:04
before all these things.

02:17:05 --> 02:17:09
Engaging your community does allow you

02:17:08 --> 02:17:10
to build the things that matter

02:17:09 --> 02:17:13
on the most local possible level.

02:17:12 --> 02:17:14
The outcome, by the way, of the sort of the politics

02:17:13 --> 02:17:16
of fulfillment that I was talking about earlier

02:17:15 --> 02:17:17
is a lot of localism.

02:17:16 --> 02:17:19
Because the roles that I'm talking about

02:17:18 --> 02:17:20
are largely local roles.

02:17:19 --> 02:17:22
So that stuff has to be protected locally.

02:17:21 --> 02:17:24
I think we focus way too much in this country and others,

02:17:23 --> 02:17:27
on world beating solutions, national solutions,

02:17:26 --> 02:17:29
solutions that apply to hundreds of millions of people.

02:17:28 --> 02:17:32
How about we get to the solutions that apply for like five?

02:17:31 --> 02:17:34
And then we get to the solutions that apply to like 20,

02:17:33 --> 02:17:36
and then we get to the solutions that involve 200 people

02:17:35 --> 02:17:37
or a thousand people.

02:17:36 --> 02:17:38
Let's solve that stuff.

02:17:37 --> 02:17:41
And I think the solutions at the higher level

02:17:40 --> 02:17:42
flow bottom up, not top down.

02:17:41 --> 02:17:46
- What about mentors and maybe role models?

02:17:45 --> 02:17:50
Have you had a mentor or maybe people you look up to,

02:17:49 --> 02:17:52
either you interacted on a local scale,

02:17:51 --> 02:17:54
you actually knew them or somebody you really looked up to?

02:17:53 --> 02:17:55
- For me, I'm very lucky.

02:17:54 --> 02:17:57
I grew up in a very solid two-parent household.

02:17:56 --> 02:17:59
I'm extremely close with my parents.

02:17:58 --> 02:18:01
I've lived near my parents literally my entire life,

02:18:00 --> 02:18:03
with the exception of three years of law school.

02:18:03 --> 02:18:07
Right now, they live a mile and a half from us.

02:18:07 --> 02:18:10
- What'd you learn from about life

02:18:09 --> 02:18:12
from your parents and your father?

02:18:13 --> 02:18:17
- Oh man, so many things from my parents.

02:18:16 --> 02:18:17
- [Lex] Good and bad.

02:18:16 --> 02:18:18
- That's a hard one.

02:18:17 --> 02:18:22
I mean, I think the good stuff from my dad

02:18:21 --> 02:18:23
is that you should hold true to your values.

02:18:22 --> 02:18:25
He's very big on, you have values.

02:18:24 --> 02:18:26
The values are important and hold true to them.

02:18:25 --> 02:18:28
- Did you understand what your values are?

02:18:27 --> 02:18:30
What your principles are early on?

02:18:29 --> 02:18:31
- Fairly quickly, yeah.

02:18:31 --> 02:18:34
And so, he was very big on that,

02:18:33 --> 02:18:35
which is why, for example,

02:18:34 --> 02:18:37
I get asked a lot in the Jewish community,

02:18:36 --> 02:18:38
why I wear a kippah.

02:18:37 --> 02:18:39
And the answer is,

02:18:38 --> 02:18:40
it never occurred to me to take off the kippah.

02:18:39 --> 02:18:42
I always wore it.

02:18:41 --> 02:18:43
Why would I take it off at any point?

02:18:42 --> 02:18:44
That's the life that I wanna live.

02:18:43 --> 02:18:47
And, that's the way it is.

02:18:46 --> 02:18:49
So that was a big one from my dad.

02:18:48 --> 02:18:50
From my mom, practicality.

02:18:49 --> 02:18:51
My dad is more of a dreamer.

02:18:50 --> 02:18:52
My mom is much more practical.

02:18:51 --> 02:18:56
And so, the sort of lessons that I learned

02:18:55 --> 02:18:57
from my dad are that you can have,

02:18:57 --> 02:19:00
the counter lesson is that you can have a good idea,

02:18:59 --> 02:19:02
but if you don't have a plan for implementation,

02:19:01 --> 02:19:03
then it doesn't end up as reality.

02:19:02 --> 02:19:05
And I think actually he's learned that

02:19:04 --> 02:19:07
better over the course of his life too.

02:19:06 --> 02:19:10
But, my dad from the time I was very young,

02:19:09 --> 02:19:12
he wanted me to engage with other adults

02:19:11 --> 02:19:14
and he wanted me to learn from other people.

02:19:13 --> 02:19:16
And, one of his rules was if he didn't know something,

02:19:15 --> 02:19:18
he would find somebody who he thought did know the thing

02:19:17 --> 02:19:20
for me to talk to.

02:19:19 --> 02:19:21
That was a big thing.

02:19:20 --> 02:19:22
So, I'm very lucky.

02:19:21 --> 02:19:23
I have wonderful parents.

02:19:22 --> 02:19:25
As far as sort of other mentors,

02:19:24 --> 02:19:28
in terms of the media, Andrew Breitbart was, was a mentor.

02:19:27 --> 02:19:31
Andrew, obviously, he was kind of known in his latter days,

02:19:30 --> 02:19:33
I think more for the militancy

02:19:32 --> 02:19:35
than when I was very close with him.

02:19:34 --> 02:19:37
- So for somebody like me who doesn't,

02:19:36 --> 02:19:39
who knows more about the militancy.

02:19:38 --> 02:19:43
Can you tell me what makes him a great man?

02:19:42 --> 02:19:46
- What made Andrew great is that he engaged with everyone.

02:19:45 --> 02:19:47
I mean, everyone.

02:19:46 --> 02:19:51
So there are videos of him rollerblading down the boulevard,

02:19:50 --> 02:19:52
and people would be protesting,

02:19:51 --> 02:19:54
and he would literally roller blade up to them

02:19:53 --> 02:19:55
and he would say,

02:19:54 --> 02:19:55
"Let's go to lunch together."

02:19:54 --> 02:19:56
And, he would just do this.

02:19:55 --> 02:19:58
That's actually who Andrew was.

02:19:57 --> 02:20:00
- What was the thinking behind that?

02:19:59 --> 02:20:01
- That's who he was.

02:20:00 --> 02:20:02
He was just garrulous.

02:20:01 --> 02:20:03
He was much more outgoing than I am actually.

02:20:02 --> 02:20:05
He was very warm with people.

02:20:04 --> 02:20:08
Like for me, I would say that with Andrew,

02:20:07 --> 02:20:12
I knew Andrew for, let's say I met him when I was 16.

02:20:11 --> 02:20:15
He passed away when I would've been 28.

02:20:14 --> 02:20:18
So I knew Andrew for 10, 12 years.

02:20:17 --> 02:20:21
And people who met Andrew for about 10 minutes

02:20:22 --> 02:20:26
knew Andrew 99% as well as I knew Andrew

02:20:25 --> 02:20:27
because he was just all out front.

02:20:26 --> 02:20:28
Everything was out here.

02:20:27 --> 02:20:29
And, he loved talking to people.

02:20:28 --> 02:20:31
He loved engaging with people.

02:20:30 --> 02:20:33
And so, this made him a lot of fun and unpredictable,

02:20:32 --> 02:20:35
and fun to watch and all of that.

02:20:34 --> 02:20:37
And then, I think Twitter got to him.

02:20:36 --> 02:20:39
Twitter is one of the lessons I learned from Andrew

02:20:38 --> 02:20:40
is the counter lesson.

02:20:39 --> 02:20:42
Which is Twitter can poison you.

02:20:41 --> 02:20:42
Twitter can really wreck you.

02:20:41 --> 02:20:44
If you spend all day on Twitter,

02:20:43 --> 02:20:46
reading the comments and getting angry at people

02:20:45 --> 02:20:47
who are talking about you,

02:20:46 --> 02:20:48
it becomes a very difficult life.

02:20:47 --> 02:20:50
And I think that, in the last year of his life,

02:20:49 --> 02:20:52
Andrew got very caught up in that

02:20:51 --> 02:20:55
because of a series of sort of circumstances.

02:20:54 --> 02:20:56
- It can actually affect your mind.

02:20:55 --> 02:20:57
It can actually make you resentful.

02:20:56 --> 02:20:58
All that kind of stuff.

02:20:57 --> 02:21:00
- I tend to agree with that.

02:20:59 --> 02:21:02
But, the lesson that I learned from Andrew is

02:21:01 --> 02:21:03
engage with everybody.

02:21:02 --> 02:21:07
Take joy in sort of the mission that you're given.

02:21:06 --> 02:21:08
And, you can't always fulfill that.

02:21:07 --> 02:21:10
Sometimes, it's really rough and difficult.

02:21:09 --> 02:21:12
I'm not gonna pretend that it's all fun

02:21:11 --> 02:21:13
and rainbows all the time,

02:21:12 --> 02:21:14
'cause it isn't.

02:21:13 --> 02:21:16
And some of the stuff that I have to cover I don't like,

02:21:15 --> 02:21:17
and some of the things I have to say,

02:21:16 --> 02:21:19
I don't particularly like.

02:21:18 --> 02:21:20
That happens.

02:21:19 --> 02:21:23
But, that's what I learned from Andrew.

02:21:22 --> 02:21:24
As far as sort of other mentors,

02:21:23 --> 02:21:26
I had some teachers when I was a kid,

02:21:25 --> 02:21:28
who said things that stuck with me.

02:21:27 --> 02:21:31
I had fourth grade teacher named Ms. Genetty who said,

02:21:30 --> 02:21:33
"Don't let potential be written on your tombstone."

02:21:32 --> 02:21:35
Which is a pretty...

02:21:34 --> 02:21:36
- That's a good line.

02:21:35 --> 02:21:38
- It's a great line, particularly to a fourth grader.

02:21:40 --> 02:21:42
There was guy, an 11th grade English teacher

02:21:41 --> 02:21:44
named Anthony Miller, who was terrific, really good writer.

02:21:43 --> 02:21:46
He had studied with James Joyce

02:21:45 --> 02:21:47
at Trinity College in Dublin.

02:21:46 --> 02:21:49
And so, he and I really got along.

02:21:48 --> 02:21:52
And, he helped my writing a lot.

02:21:51 --> 02:21:54
- Did you ever have doubt in yourself?

02:21:53 --> 02:21:57
Especially, as you gotten into the public eye

02:21:56 --> 02:21:57
with all the attacks.

02:21:56 --> 02:22:00
Did you ever doubt your ability to stay strong,

02:21:59 --> 02:22:02
to be able to be a voice of the ideas

02:22:01 --> 02:22:04
that you represent?

02:22:03 --> 02:22:06
- Definitely, I don't doubt my ability to say

02:22:05 --> 02:22:07
what I wanna say.

02:22:06 --> 02:22:09
I doubt my ability to handle the emotional blow back

02:22:08 --> 02:22:10
of saying it.

02:22:09 --> 02:22:11
Meaning that, that's difficult.

02:22:10 --> 02:22:15
I mean, again, to take just one example.

02:22:14 --> 02:22:18
In 2016, the ADL measured that I was the number one target

02:22:17 --> 02:22:20
of antisemitism on planet Earth.

02:22:19 --> 02:22:23
That's not fun, that's unpleasant.

02:22:22 --> 02:22:25
And when you take critiques, not from Antisemites,

02:22:24 --> 02:22:28
but when you take critiques from people generally,

02:22:27 --> 02:22:30
we talked about near the beginning,

02:22:29 --> 02:22:32
how you surround yourself with people

02:22:31 --> 02:22:33
who are gonna give you good feedback.

02:22:32 --> 02:22:34
Sometimes it's hard to tell.

02:22:33 --> 02:22:36
Sometimes people are giving you feedback

02:22:35 --> 02:22:37
and you don't know whether it's well motivated

02:22:36 --> 02:22:39
or poorly motivated.

02:22:38 --> 02:22:41
And if you are trying to be a decent person,

02:22:40 --> 02:22:43
you can't cut off the mechanism of feedback.

02:22:42 --> 02:22:44
And so, what that means is

02:22:43 --> 02:22:46
ometimes you take to heart the wrong thing

02:22:45 --> 02:22:49
or you take it to heart too much,

02:22:48 --> 02:22:50
you're not light enough about it.

02:22:49 --> 02:22:52
You take it very, very seriously.

02:22:51 --> 02:22:53
You lose sleep over it.

02:22:52 --> 02:22:54
Man, I can't tell you the number of nights

02:22:53 --> 02:22:55
where I've just not slept

02:22:54 --> 02:22:56
because of some critique somebody's made of me.

02:22:55 --> 02:22:59
And I've thought to myself, maybe that's right.

02:22:58 --> 02:23:01
And sometimes it is right.

02:23:00 --> 02:23:02
- Some of that is good to stew in that criticism,

02:23:03 --> 02:23:06
but some of that can destroy you.

02:23:05 --> 02:23:06
Do you have a shortcut?

02:23:05 --> 02:23:10
So Rogan has talked about taking a lot of mushrooms.

02:23:09 --> 02:23:13
Since you're not into the mushroom thing.

02:23:13 --> 02:23:15
What's your escape from that?

02:23:14 --> 02:23:17
Like when you get low,

02:23:16 --> 02:23:18
when you can't sleep?

02:23:17 --> 02:23:20
- Usually writing is a big one for me.

02:23:19 --> 02:23:21
The writing for me is cathartic.

02:23:20 --> 02:23:23
I love writing that.

02:23:22 --> 02:23:25
That is a huge one.

02:23:24 --> 02:23:27
Spending time with my family.

02:23:26 --> 02:23:30
Again, I usually have a close circle of friends,

02:23:29 --> 02:23:31
who I will talk with

02:23:30 --> 02:23:33
in order to sort of bounce ideas off of them.

02:23:32 --> 02:23:36
And then, once I've kind of talked it through,

02:23:35 --> 02:23:38
I tend to feel a little bit better.

02:23:37 --> 02:23:40
Exercise is also a big one.

02:23:39 --> 02:23:41
I mean, if I go a few days with that exercise,

02:23:40 --> 02:23:44
I tend to get pretty grumpy pretty quickly.

02:23:43 --> 02:23:46
I gotta keep this six pack going somehow, man.

02:23:45 --> 02:23:47
(hearty laughing)

02:23:46 --> 02:23:48
- There you and Rogan agree.

02:23:49 --> 02:23:53
Well, we haven't, aside from Twitter mentioned love.

02:23:52 --> 02:23:56
What's the role of love in the human condition?

02:23:55 --> 02:23:57
Ben Shapiro.

02:23:57 --> 02:24:00
- Man, don't get asked for love too much.

02:23:59 --> 02:24:01
In fact, I was-

02:24:02 --> 02:24:05
[Lex] You don't get that question on college campuses?

02:24:04 --> 02:24:06
- No, I typically don't, actually.

02:24:05 --> 02:24:09
In fact, we were at an event recently,

02:24:08 --> 02:24:10
it was a Daily Wire event,

02:24:09 --> 02:24:12
and in the middle of this event,

02:24:11 --> 02:24:14
was a meet and greet with some of the audience.

02:24:13 --> 02:24:15
And, in the middle of this event,

02:24:14 --> 02:24:17
this guy walks by with this girl.

02:24:16 --> 02:24:18
They're talking and they're talking to me

02:24:17 --> 02:24:20
and their time kind of runs, the security's moving them.

02:24:19 --> 02:24:21
And he says, "No, no, no, wait.

02:24:20 --> 02:24:22
"Hold on a minute."

02:24:21 --> 02:24:23
And he gets down on one knee and he proposes to the girl

02:24:22 --> 02:24:24
in front of me.

02:24:23 --> 02:24:25
And I said to him,

02:24:24 --> 02:24:28
"This is the weirdest proposal in human history.

02:24:27 --> 02:24:30
"What, what, what is happening right now?

02:24:29 --> 02:24:32
"I was your choice of cupid here?"

02:24:31 --> 02:24:33
He said, "Well, you know,

02:24:32 --> 02:24:34
"We actually like got together

02:24:33 --> 02:24:36
"'Cause we listened to your show."

02:24:35 --> 02:24:36
And I said,

02:24:35 --> 02:24:38
"I can perform it like a Jewish marriage right now.

02:24:37 --> 02:24:40
"We're gonna need like a glass, we're gonna need some wine.

02:24:39 --> 02:24:42
"It's gonna get weird real fast."

02:24:41 --> 02:24:44
But yeah, so love doctor.

02:24:43 --> 02:24:47
I'm typically not asked too much about.

02:24:46 --> 02:24:47
The role of love

02:24:50 --> 02:24:54
is important in binding together human beings,

02:24:54 --> 02:24:56
who ought to be bound together.

02:24:55 --> 02:24:59
And the role of respect is even more important in binding

02:24:58 --> 02:25:01
together broader groups of people.

02:25:00 --> 02:25:02
I think one of the mistakes that we make in politics

02:25:01 --> 02:25:04
is trying to substitute love for respect

02:25:03 --> 02:25:04
for love.

02:25:03 --> 02:25:06
And, I think that's a big mistake.

02:25:05 --> 02:25:10
So I do not bear tremendous love

02:25:09 --> 02:25:11
in the same sense that I do for my family,

02:25:10 --> 02:25:13
for random strangers.

02:25:12 --> 02:25:15
I don't, I love my family.

02:25:14 --> 02:25:15
I love my kids.

02:25:14 --> 02:25:17
Anybody who tells you they love your kid

02:25:16 --> 02:25:17
as much as you love your kid

02:25:16 --> 02:25:18
is lying to you.

02:25:17 --> 02:25:19
It's not true.

02:25:18 --> 02:25:23
I love my community more than I love other communities.

02:25:22 --> 02:25:25
I love my state more than I love other states.

02:25:24 --> 02:25:27
I love my country more than I love other countries.

02:25:26 --> 02:25:30
That's all normal and that's all good.

02:25:29 --> 02:25:31
The problem of empathy

02:25:30 --> 02:25:33
can be when that becomes so tight knit

02:25:32 --> 02:25:35
hat you're not outward looking,

02:25:34 --> 02:25:37
that you don't actually have respect for other people.

02:25:36 --> 02:25:39
So in the local level,

02:25:38 --> 02:25:41
you need love in order to protect you and shield you,

02:25:40 --> 02:25:42
and give you the strength to go forward.

02:25:41 --> 02:25:43
And then beyond that,

02:25:42 --> 02:25:44
you need a lot of respect for people

02:25:43 --> 02:25:47
who are not in the circle of love.

02:25:46 --> 02:25:49
And I think trying to extend love to people

02:25:48 --> 02:25:53
who either are not gonna love you back

02:25:52 --> 02:25:56
or are gonna slap you in the face for it,

02:25:55 --> 02:25:57
or who you're just not that close to.

02:25:56 --> 02:26:02
It runs the risk of being arsehats and fake.

02:26:01 --> 02:26:05
Or it can actually be counterproductive in some senses.

02:26:04 --> 02:26:07
- Well, there's some sense

02:26:06 --> 02:26:10
in which you could have love for other human beings

02:26:09 --> 02:26:15
just based on the humanity that connects everybody.

02:26:14 --> 02:26:18
So you love this whole project

02:26:17 --> 02:26:20
hat we're a part of

02:26:19 --> 02:26:24
and actually, sort of another thing, we disagree on.

02:26:23 --> 02:26:27
So loving a stranger,

02:26:26 --> 02:26:29
like having that basic empathy

02:26:28 --> 02:26:30
and compassion towards a stranger,

02:26:30 --> 02:26:33
even if it can hurt you,

02:26:32 --> 02:26:35
I think it's ultimately...

02:26:36 --> 02:26:40
That to me is what it means to be a good man.

02:26:39 --> 02:26:43
To live a good life is to have that compassion

02:26:42 --> 02:26:44
towards strangers.

02:26:43 --> 02:26:45
'Cause to me, it's almost,

02:26:44 --> 02:26:48
it's easy and natural and obvious

02:26:47 --> 02:26:50
to love people close to you.

02:26:49 --> 02:26:53
But, to step outside yourself and to love others.

02:26:52 --> 02:26:55
I think that's the fabric of a good society.

02:26:54 --> 02:26:57
You don't think there's value to that?

02:26:56 --> 02:26:58
- I think there can be,

02:26:57 --> 02:26:59
but I think we're also discussing love

02:26:58 --> 02:27:01
almost in two different senses.

02:27:00 --> 02:27:03
Meaning that when I talk about love,

02:27:02 --> 02:27:06
what I think of immediately is the love I bear for my wife

02:27:05 --> 02:27:09
and kids or my parents or my siblings.

02:27:08 --> 02:27:10
- [Lex] What about friendship?

02:27:09 --> 02:27:12
- Or the love of my close friends.

02:27:11 --> 02:27:13
But, I think

02:27:13 --> 02:27:17
that using that same term to describe how I feel

02:27:16 --> 02:27:19
about strangers, I think would just be inaccurate.

02:27:18 --> 02:27:22
And so, that's why I'm suggesting that respect

02:27:21 --> 02:27:23
might be a more solid

02:27:22 --> 02:27:27
and realistic foundation for the way that we treat people

02:27:26 --> 02:27:28
far away from us or people who are strangers.

02:27:27 --> 02:27:31
Respect for their dignity, respect for their priorities,

02:27:30 --> 02:27:34
respect for their role in life.

02:27:33 --> 02:27:37
It might be too much of an ask, in other words.

02:27:36 --> 02:27:38
There might be the rare human being

02:27:37 --> 02:27:40
who's capable of literally loving a homeless man

02:27:39 --> 02:27:42
on the street the way they he loves his own family.

02:27:41 --> 02:27:43
But, if you respect the homeless man on the street,

02:27:42 --> 02:27:47
the way that you respect your own family,

02:27:46 --> 02:27:48
because everyone is deserved,

02:27:48 --> 02:27:50
everyone deserves that respect.

02:27:49 --> 02:27:52
I think that you get to the same end

02:27:51 --> 02:27:54
without forcing people into a position

02:27:53 --> 02:27:57
of unrealistically expecting themselves

02:27:56 --> 02:27:59
to feel a thing they don't feel.

02:27:58 --> 02:28:01
One of the big questions in religion

02:28:00 --> 02:28:03
that comes up is God makes certain requests

02:28:02 --> 02:28:05
that you feel certain ways.

02:28:04 --> 02:28:06
You're supposed to be (indistinct).

02:28:05 --> 02:28:08
You're supposed to be happy about certain things

02:28:07 --> 02:28:11
or you're supposed to love thy neighbor as thyself.

02:28:10 --> 02:28:13
You'll notice that in that statement,

02:28:12 --> 02:28:14
it's thy neighbor.

02:28:13 --> 02:28:16
It's not just like generally anyone.

02:28:15 --> 02:28:18
It's love thy neighbors, in any case.

02:28:17 --> 02:28:20
- I think that extends to anyone that follows you

02:28:19 --> 02:28:22
on Twitter, thy neighbor,

02:28:21 --> 02:28:25
because God anticipated the social network aspect

02:28:24 --> 02:28:28
hat is not constrained by geography.

02:28:27 --> 02:28:30
- Yeah, I'm gonna differ with you on the interpretation

02:28:29 --> 02:28:31
that, but in any case.

02:28:32 --> 02:28:36
The kind of extension of

02:28:35 --> 02:28:38
love outwards might be too big an ask.

02:28:37 --> 02:28:39
So maybe we can start with respect

02:28:39 --> 02:28:42
and then hopefully out of that respect can grow

02:28:41 --> 02:28:44
something more,

02:28:43 --> 02:28:46
if people earn their way in.

02:28:45 --> 02:28:47
'Cause I think that one of the big problems

02:28:46 --> 02:28:48
when we were talking about universalism

02:28:47 --> 02:28:49
is when people say,

02:28:48 --> 02:28:50
"I'm a world citizen.

02:28:49 --> 02:28:52
"I love people of the other country.

02:28:51 --> 02:28:55
"As much as I love myself or as much as I love my country."

02:28:54 --> 02:28:57
It tends to actually lead to,

02:28:56 --> 02:28:59
and almost cram down utopianism.

02:28:59 --> 02:29:02
That, I think can be kind of difficult

02:29:01 --> 02:29:03
because with love

02:29:02 --> 02:29:07
comes a certain expectation of solidarity.

02:29:06 --> 02:29:09
And I think,

02:29:08 --> 02:29:10
when you love your family, you love your wife,

02:29:09 --> 02:29:12
there's a certain level of solidarity that is required

02:29:11 --> 02:29:14
inside the home in order to preserve

02:29:13 --> 02:29:15
the most loving kind of home.

02:29:14 --> 02:29:17
And so, if you love everybody,

02:29:16 --> 02:29:19
then that sort of implies a certain level of solidarity

02:29:18 --> 02:29:19
that may not exist.

02:29:18 --> 02:29:22
So maybe the idea is for me,

02:29:21 --> 02:29:23
start with respect,

02:29:22 --> 02:29:25
and then maybe as people respect each other more,

02:29:24 --> 02:29:27
then love is an outgrowth of that as opposed to starting

02:29:26 --> 02:29:28
with love and then hoping that respect develops.

02:29:27 --> 02:29:31
- Yeah, there's a danger that that word becomes empty

02:29:30 --> 02:29:35
and instead is used for dogmatic kind of utopianism.

02:29:36 --> 02:29:39
- I mean, this is the way that, for example,

02:29:38 --> 02:29:41
religious theocracies very often work.

02:29:40 --> 02:29:42
We love you so much,

02:29:41 --> 02:29:43
we have to convert you.

02:29:42 --> 02:29:45
- So let's start with respect.

02:29:44 --> 02:29:49
What I would love to see after our conversation today,

02:29:48 --> 02:29:51
is to see a Ben Shapiro

02:29:50 --> 02:29:53
that continues the growth on Twitter

02:29:52 --> 02:29:56
of being even more respectful than you've already been.

02:29:55 --> 02:30:00
And maybe one day, converting that into love on Twitter.

02:29:59 --> 02:30:02
If I could see that in this world,

02:30:01 --> 02:30:05
that would make me die a happy man.

02:30:04 --> 02:30:06
- [Ben] Wow.

02:30:05 --> 02:30:07
- A little bit more.

02:30:06 --> 02:30:08
- If I can make that happen for you.

02:30:07 --> 02:30:11
- Love in the world, for me, as a gift, for me.

02:30:10 --> 02:30:11
- I'll try to make that happen.

02:30:10 --> 02:30:15
I do have one question, I'm gonna need you to tell me.

02:30:14 --> 02:30:16
Which jokes are okay?

02:30:15 --> 02:30:17
Are jokes still okay?

02:30:16 --> 02:30:20
- So yeah, can I just run your Twitter from now on?

02:30:19 --> 02:30:21
You just send it to me.

02:30:20 --> 02:30:23
- 100%, I will pre-screen you the jokes,

02:30:22 --> 02:30:25
and you can tell me if this is a loving joke

02:30:24 --> 02:30:27
or if this is a hate filled.

02:30:26 --> 02:30:28
- People will be very surprised

02:30:27 --> 02:30:30
by the all the heart emojis that start popping up

02:30:29 --> 02:30:32
on your Twitter.

02:30:31 --> 02:30:34
Ben, I thank you so much for being bold and fearless,

02:30:33 --> 02:30:38
and exploring ideas, and your Twitter aside.

02:30:37 --> 02:30:41
Thank you for being just good faith and all the arguments

02:30:40 --> 02:30:42
and all the conversations you're having with people.

02:30:41 --> 02:30:44
It's a huge honor.

02:30:43 --> 02:30:45
Thank you for talking to me.

02:30:44 --> 02:30:46
- Hey, thanks for having me.

02:30:45 --> 02:30:47
I really appreciate it.

02:30:46 --> 02:30:48
- Thanks for listening to this conversation

02:30:47 --> 02:30:50
with Ben Shapiro.

02:30:49 --> 02:30:51
To support this podcast,

02:30:50 --> 02:30:54
please check out our sponsors in the description.

02:30:53 --> 02:30:56
And now, let me leave you with some words

02:30:55 --> 02:30:57
from Ben Shapiro himself.

02:30:56 --> 02:31:00
"Freedom of speech and thought matters,

02:30:59 --> 02:31:03
"Especially when it is speech and thought

02:31:02 --> 02:31:05
"With which we disagree.

02:31:04 --> 02:31:08
"The moment the majority decides to destroy people

02:31:07 --> 02:31:10
"For engaging a thought it dislikes,

02:31:09 --> 02:31:13
"Thought crime becomes a reality."

02:31:13 --> 02:31:17
Thank you for listening and hope to see you next time.

<!-- YOUTUBE_TRANSCRIPT_END -->
